#2905. Cardinals name Gardner Minshew starting quarterback, Carson Beck his backup

Brissett was benched on Sunday after completing 10-of-23 passes for 119 yards with an interception. In eight games this season, he's completed 63.3 percent of his passes for 1,719 yards with 10 touchdowns and seven interceptions.

Sunday was Minshew's first appearance of the season. He completed 17-of-23 passes for 188 yards with three touchdowns.

#2899. Social Security may have to cut retirement benefits in six years. Here's how much money is at stake for beneficiaries

Social Security retirement benefits may face an automatic, across-the-board cut in just six years.

Without intervention before then, the Social Security retirement trust fund, formally known as the Old-Age and Survivors Insurance Trust Fund, which helps pay benefits to eligible workers and qualifying family members, will run out in the fourth quarter of 2032, the program's trustees said in a June report.

At that time, the trustees project retirement benefits will be cut by 22%.

The Congressional Budget Office has a more dire forecast, anticipating that the retirement trust fund may run out sooner, in mid-2032, and that retirees may see larger benefit cuts, at 26%, upon insolvency.

Beneficiaries may receive an average $500 cut to their monthly benefits across the U.S. based on a 24% benefit reduction, according to a June report from the Committee for a Responsible Federal Budget. In 29 states, retirees would see even bigger average reductions, according to the nonpartisan organization focused on educating the public on fiscal policy issues.

Senators elected this November will be in office in 2032, when the retirement trust fund depletion date hits. Their ability to right the ship is crucial for retirees and the economy. But it's not a sure thing given the political climate inside the beltway.

"The attention on 2032 is appropriate in the sense that that is when beneficiaries and future beneficiaries will see and feel the consequences of inaction," said Shai Akabas, vice president of economic policy at the Bipartisan Policy Center, a public policy think tank focused on promoting bipartisan cooperation.

"Nothing gets Congress to move like that type of deadline," he said.

Of course, brinksmanship and political deadlock may stymie efforts. Congress could opt to kick the can down the road, rather than enact changes to shore up Social Security — which may require tax increases, benefit cuts or a combination of both.

If lawmakers decide to combine Social Security's retirement and disability trust funds, the depletion date may move to the third quarter of 2034, when benefits may be cut by 17%, according to Social Security's trustees.

Akabas said any delay by lawmakers would have consequences: Social Security's shortfall would continue to increase.

There could also be consequences for the economy, he said.

In 2023, Social Security benefits generated $2.6 trillion in total economic output and supported more than 12 million jobs, according to 2025 research by the National Institute on Retirement Security, a Washington, D.C., based nonprofit, nonpartisan organization.

A 19% benefit cut could reduce gross domestic product by hundreds of billions of dollars, according to the NIRS research.

Moreover, failing to muster Social Security reform would prompt bond market strain, according to research published in June by George Mason University's Mercatus Center.

Social Security is tied to the bond market because its trust funds are invested in special issue U.S. Treasury bonds.

If Social Security reform is delayed, that may put upward pressure on Treasury yields, with investors wanting higher returns for long-term bonds.

Delayed reform may either prompt higher interest rates and borrowing costs or inflationary adjustments that hurt the value of government liabilities, according to the Mercatus Center research. Those changes may affect household budgets through higher borrowing costs for mortgages, auto loans and other types of consumer credit as well as student loans, according to the research.

If interest rates and prices rise, that will interfere with economic growth, Akabas said.

"Those effects are also ones that the American people feel, in addition to adjustments to their benefits," Akabas said.

Heading to the midterms, some Democratic congressional candidates have said they would raise or eliminate the Social Security payroll tax cap so high earners pay more into the program. In 2026, the 6.2% tax paid by both workers and their employers applies to earnings up to $184,500.

Yet it remains to be seen whether Washington leaders will have the political will to address the problem soon. Tax increases, in particular, may not be viewed as politically popular heading into the 2028 presidential election.

As such, payroll taxes are "extraordinarily unlikely" to rise in the next couple of years, said Jessica Riedl, a fellow at the Urban-Brookings Tax Policy Center, which provides independent analyses of tax issues. If those tax hikes happen, it would likely be closer to 2032, she said.

"In Washington, they love to wait until the last minute," Riedl said. "There's no interest on either side for these sorts of tax increases in the short term. In the long term, I think everything is on the table."

The National Committee to Preserve Social Security and Medicare, founded by President Franklin D. Roosevelt's son, former U.S. Rep. James Roosevelt, has scored incumbent lawmakers on whether their votes have protected those programs.

The advocacy organization has also endorsed candidates who support raising payroll taxes and protecting Social Security benefits from cuts. With those endorsements, the NCPSSM gives candidates a set of boxing gloves as a symbol of the fights they'll face in Congress, according to Dan Adcock, the NCPSSM's director of government relations and policy.

The organization is also working to educate voters that the senators elected this year will be among those to deal with Social Security, he said.

"We've been trying to impress upon voters why this is an issue that should be top tier," Adcock said. "They need to be asking candidates about what their plans for the program would be."

#2898. Brandon Aiyuk hires super agent. Is NFL return realistic for 49ers receiver?

The saga of Brandon Aiyuk might yet have another chapter.

The disgruntled wide receiver, who is on the San Francisco 49ers' left squad list, has reportedly hired Drew Rosenhaus as his new agent with the goal of "getting him back to work and playing again in the NFL," according to ESPN's Adam Schefter. #2896. Lakers’ Austin Reaves forced to take headband away from fan for most understandable reason

The Lakers improved to 5-2 on the young season with a 131-125 win over the Raptors on Sunday night. The score is not indicative of how much Los Angeles controlled the game, which set in motion the most hysterical moment of the game.

When Austin Reaves subbed out with 1:43 remaining in the Lakers' eventual win over the Raptors, he did so with clear intentions of not returning to the game.

The biggest tell: The Lakers star guard gave his signature headband to a fan sitting behind the team's bench, with Reaves expecting to watch the remainder of the game from the sidelines.

Austin Reaves (15) had to get his signature headband back when Lakers coach JJ Redick needed to put him back in the game. NBAE via Getty Images

With their end-of-bench players in the game, the Lakers' lead was cut from 15 points with 1:24 left to single digits within 20 seconds of game time.

Austin Reaves didn't expect to be needed for the closing stretch. NBAE via Getty Images

Coach JJ Redick had seen enough from the reserves when Jaden Bradley converted the and-1 layup that trimmed the Raptors' deficit to six points with 44 seconds left (and five points after making the free throw), deciding to sub his main rotation players back in to close the game out.

Which meant that Reaves had to retrieve his headband as he, Matisse Thybulle, Walker Kessler and Luka Dončić all subbed back in the game with 44 seconds left to secure the victory.

Luckily for Reaves, it didn't appear the fan had any issues with handing the headband back over.

The Lakers got the job done after getting their main rotation players back in the game. NBAE via Getty Images

With Redick saying he won't make this mistake again, subbing in players if they aren't ready to play late in games, it appears to be unlikely that the 6-foot-5 guard will be in this position again.

But in case it does, Reaves better hold onto his headband until the game's officially over.

#2893. AI debt fears ease ahead of Oracle, Alphabet and Amazon updates. Here's what to watch.

Fears over artificial intelligence debt have eased after a bout of volatility. But more tests for AI debt are coming.

Oracle holds its Investor Day on Wednesday. And hyperscalers Alphabet and Amazon.com are scheduled to report quarterly results after the market close on Wednesday and Thursday, respectively.

Credit markets are on edge over a flood of AI debt, though fears have eased up a little lately. Meta Platforms' plans for a $95 billion stock offering, announced on Monday, helped bring down its credit-default swap spreads. Those spreads had risen last week when the company reported negative free cash flow in the third quarter. Other CDS spreads had moved higher too, though they have since backed off.

Investors had expected Meta to bring a large bond deal to market shortly after its earnings announcement, said Richard Cheng, head of Nuveen's investment-grade corporate sector team. The fact that it wasn't announced came as a relief.

"Just the absence of the bond deal itself is a direct catalyst for AI-related spread tightening," he said.

A credit spread is the difference in the yield between the corporate bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. When spreads tighten, it means investors are becoming more confident about the bond issuer.

The market action over the last few days shows that investors are recognizing that "credit fears may have been overblown," said CNBC contributor Paul Meeks, head of technology research at Freedom Capital Markets.

"I expect that these better feelings, maybe not good but better, may continue as companies announce their quarters over the next few weeks."

Still, spreads on CDS remain wider over the past year due to a surge of debt issuance and free cash flows turning negative across the AI space. Meta's CDS spread, for instance, is still up 177% year to date, while Amazon's spread has gained 129%.

"It's not necessarily fundamentals," said CreditSights analyst Jordan Chalfin. "It's more, I'd say, 80% driven by technicals and sentiment."

Alphabet and Amazon pose the next big hurdle for tech bonds as they update their capex plans and free cash flow forecasts. Microsoft's earnings are also due Wednesday, but the company has yet to issue any bonds to pay for its AI spending.

Both Amazon and Alphabet reported negative free cash flow in the second quarter and analysts expect that trend to continue.

Analysts expect Alphabet to report free cash flow of -$2.3 billion while spending $55.2 billion on capital expenditures in the third quarter, according to FactSet consensus estimates. Amazon is expected to report -$9.9 billion in free cash flow against capex of $58.7 billion.

S&P Global Ratings estimated in an August report that the hyperscalers will have negative free operating cash flow in 2026 and 2027.

More critical will be their outlooks, Cheng says. "The singular focus on the investors' minds is really what the capex guidance trajectory looks like across the hyperscaler space."

Meeks believes elevated capex is a sign of strong AI demand. The hyperscalers have "decided that with all the evidence they have — and they have more than you and me — that this is worthwhile spending, and not just worthwhile; it's required," he said.

CreditSights' Chalfin doesn't expect the hyperscalers to announce major new bond issuance around their earnings reports. Oracle's updates may be more critical for credit markets. The company has issued $117 billion in long-term debt and is clinging to an investment-grade credit rating of BBB-, the lowest level of investment grade above junk, according to S&P.

"It's the most important IG tech credit, just given how it's a bit more stressed relative to the A-rated names," he said. "It's trading more like a high-yield credit, even though it's IG, and so there's just a lot of investor concern on that one."

One wrinkle to the AI debt story: balance sheets don't reveal the whole picture. The companies are using complicated off-balance sheet arrangements for data centers and chip financing, and they aren't fully transparent, S&P Global said in a September report.

"There are commitments they are entering into, for example, into data center leases or purchase commitments on chips that aren't necessarily treated as debt," Mark Habib, digital infrastructure sector lead at S&P Global Ratings, said on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" Monday.

S&P is trying to provide the market with more visibility on those commitments "to give a fuller picture of what kinds of obligations they're actually entering into," he added.

Oracle aside, hyperscaler credit ratings are all comfortably above A levels — reflecting the fact that they have broadly diversified revenue streams, ample coverage for interest payments, and capacity to add leverage if they choose.

Nuveen's Cheng isn't concerned, pointing out that while there has been some credit repricing, there is no credit crisis.

"They continue to have exceptional credit profiles, strong cash generation liquidity," he said of the largest hyperscalers.

"The market is really not concerned about credit quality at this point," Cheng added. "It's really just the trajectory of the capex needs and the supply of debt to fund that. Both of those are pointing in one direction — and that's up."

Correction: This story has been revised to reflect that Oracle's investment-grade credit rating is BBB-, according to S&P. A previous version mischaracterized the company's credit rating.

#2892. Texas governor race tightens in prediction markets, but Abbott remains favored

Political observers are watching the Texas gubernatorial election for signs of an artificial intelligence data center backlash in the 2026 U.S. midterm elections on Tuesday, and prediction market traders see growing odds of the Democratic candidate pulling off an upset.

Gov. Greg Abbott, a Republican seeking a fourth term, has a 60% chance of beating Democratic state Rep. Gina Hinojosa on Tuesday, according to speculators on platform Kalshi.

Hinojosa's odds to win have jumped from 8% in mid-July to 40% on Monday, with many of those gains coming in recent weeks as a series of polls have shown the race to be tightening. On Polymarket, Hinojosa has seen her odds jump from as low as 5% in late July to 39% on Monday.

Contracts are resolved using data from the Texas Secretary of State's office.

Odds and gambling platforms do not use methodologies used by traditional political polling, and therefore are not substitutes for political polls.

Hinojosa is seeking to be the first Democratic governor in Texas in more than 30 years. She has used Abbott's previous support for data center development in the state as an attack against the incumbent.

In November 2025, Abbott touted a $40 billion investment by Google — which included bringing three new data center campuses to the state — by saying that Texas will be the "epicenter of AI development." But Abbott has changed his tune as voters' opinion of data centers has soured over the past year.

Abbott in August ordered an audit of data centers that sought to connect to the state's grid, and then in September he halted the approval of all data center-related permits until an audit of the existing waitlist is conducted.

Hinojosa is running on an immediate data center moratorium until the legislature puts guardrails on the industry, according to her campaign website.

In-person early voting in Texas concluded on Friday, and more than 7.7 million Texans have already cast their ballots.

#2891. How will the Packers manage their IR designations?

Each NFL team can use eight activations from the injured reserve in the regular season, with two more in the postseason if teams qualify, per league rules. How the Green Bay Packers manage these returns could define their season down the stretch, as they may have to pick and choose who comes back if they have more options than return designations by the end of the season.

The Packers have already used two of their eight potential activations this season on receiver Savion Williams and edge defender Collin Oliver, who were both placed on IR at the cutdown deadline with return designations. Williams has already been activated, and he's currently the team's starting slot receiver after both Jayden Reed and Skyy Moore went down with injuries.

ED Collin Oliver (already designated to return – can return now)

Oliver (calf) has not been brought back yet, but the team has pre-paid his return against the team's return count with his designation at the cutdown deadline. He's able to come back to the team immediately, and has been since Week 5.

Defensive linemen Warren Brinson (calf) and running back Chris Brooks (ankle) also have missed four games of action, which means they can also return to the active roster this week, if that's what the Packers choose.

The players yet to miss four games, the minimum for players who are placed on the injured reserve, are receiver Skyy Moore (ankle), defensive lineman Jonathan Ford (calf) and offensive lineman Jacob Monk (quadriceps). Moore and Ford have windows that open for their return to the active roster next week, while Monk must wait until at least Week 13.

So if you're counting at home, the Packers have used two of their eight return options (Williams and Oliver) and have five options left for their last six return spots — and the calendar just turned to November.

TE Thomas Yassmin (reverted to IR after being unclaimed with injured designation)

S Johnathan Baldwin II (reverted to IR after being unclaimed with injured designation)

The players above have either been reported to have season-ending injuries or are players who were waived with injury designations, went unclaimed, reverted to the Packers' injured reserve and the team has not paid them an injury settlement. That's a really wordy way to say that Green Bay appears to be squatting on the rights to tight end Thomas Yassmin and safety Johnathan Baldwin II, as they likely want those players to be members of the 2027 roster.

For the sake of avoiding the "Where's Micah Parsons?" questions, these are the Packers' players who began the season on the physically unable to perform list. These players do not count against Green Bay's injured reserve count. They are allowed to return whenever the Packers wish, at this point in the season.

Green Bay has hinted at Micah Parsons opening his 21-day practice window this week, while there has been no timeline provided on the Luke Musgrave front.

#2890. Fantasy Basketball Waiver Wire: The Playlist for your Week 3 lineup

As we head into Week 3 of the fantasy basketball season, one early-season narrative continues. The injury list keeps getting longer. Ja Morant has a Grade 2 hamstring strain and will miss at least four weeks; Anthony Edwards had knee surgery Thursday and could be out two months or more. Jaren Jackson Jr. tore his MCL and has a timeline of 6-8 weeks. Chet Holmgren has a small patellar tendon tear in his right knee, costing him 8-10 weeks. Kawhi Leonard (knee) is out 3-4 weeks and Tyrese Haliburton will miss at least two weeks with a hamstring injury too — all bad. Check out my injury roundup for more details.

Moving on to some good news: Coby White (calf) is off the injury report for Monday, and Brandon Ingram (Achilles) was assigned to the Clippers' G League affiliate Saturday. Let's get to the pickups!

Every league is different, so I'm focusing on players under 50% rostered on Yahoo.

One piece of general advice for this week: don't rush to make a streaming move before Wednesday. Tuesday has no games (Election Day in the United States), so you're unlikely to fit streamers into your lineup on Monday, and the best streaming nights come later in the week.

Larsson is a good pickup for anyone in 9-cat leagues. He ranks 86th coming into Monday, averaging 11.8 points, 3.5 rebounds, 4.0 assists and 1.2 steals with just 1.0 turnover in 29.5 minutes. His current shooting splits are a robust 49/40/89 with a TS% of 67. We'll see how long the Heat continue to start Klay Thompson. It's clear to anyone that Larsson is the better player currently. He should be rostered in far more leagues, especially with Miami playing four games this week, with two on lighter slates (Thursday and Sunday). Miami also has a Wednesday-Thursday back-to-back where vets could rest.

The former No. 1 overall pick of the 2024 NBA Draft looks like a completely different player in Dallas. Shoutout to Dusty May for giving him the minutes and role to prove what he can do. Risacher ranks sixth in total stocks this season, averaging 3.7 per game. The defensive numbers on top of the rebounds (6.0 per game) and scoring nearly 10 points a game in 30 minutes makes him a must-roster player. He's a top-60 player in 9-cat and top-90 in points leagues. Dallas has four games this week, but I would hold him for the foreseeable future if he's available on waivers.

Walter has taken off since Kawhi Leonard (knee) went down. In the four games since, he's at 14.0 points, 4.0 rebounds, 1.3 steals and 3.5 3s in about 31 minutes per contest. He's starting in place of Leonard and has been a top-50 player in 9-cat. Toronto only plays three games this week, but with Leonard set for a multi-week absence, Walter should be rostered in all formats, especially 9-cat.

Tobias Harris' calf injury has boosted Champagnie's fantasy value. He's playing over 28 minutes a night and has emerged as one of the league's top 3-point specialists. His 3.3 3s per game rank ninth and his 3-point percentage ranks fifth in the NBA. His defense has been the real surprise. He's producing 1.2 steals with a block per game. Like the others, he's a must-roster for me ahead of Week 3 because San Antonio plays four games and Harris doesn't appear close to returning.

Gillespie is a schedule add for those in leagues like High Score. Phoenix plays four times, including two of the lighter nights (Thursday and Saturday). His numbers aren't amazing, but he's the best playmaker on the roster outside of Devin Booker. He leads the Suns in assists despite coming off the bench and is sporting a very strong 19% usage rate. #2889. Commanders’ Dan Quinn Announces Concerning Injury News After Eagles Win

The Washington Commanders secured a much-needed victory over the Philadelphia Eagles on Sunday night to earn their second win of the season.

They trailed 10-0 but ultimately overcame the deficit, outscoring the Eagles 27-0 the rest of the way to secure a 27-10 win.

However, despite the victory, Commanders head coach Dan Quinn shared some concerning injury news about an already depleted roster.

Speaking to the media Monday, Quinn revealed that running back Jeremy McNichols is undergoing additional testing for what the team believes is a significant midfoot injury.

McNichols, who began the year on injured reserve with a quad injury, had stepped into a much larger role since returning with Jacory Croskey-Merritt being placed on IR.

Via ESPN's John Keim: "Dan Quinn said he's concerned that Jeremy McNichols' foot injury is 'serious.' He'll get more scans Tuesday."

With McNichols likely to miss time if his injury is indeed serious, the Commanders are now down to Rachaad White and veteran Austin Ekeler, who no longer handles the heavy workload he once did.

Croskey-Merritt must miss at least two more games on injured reserve before the Commanders can open his practice window. With the trade deadline set for Nov. 10, that raises the question of whether Washington could make an aggressive move to bolster the position.

The Commanders also have practice squad options such as Craig Reynolds and Devin Neal, who could contribute to the offense over the next few weeks until Croskey-Merritt returns.

With Sunday's victory, the Commanders sit at 2-4-1, which isn't exactly an impressive record. Fortunately, they're in one of the weakest divisions in the NFL this season.

The NFC East has just one team above .500: the first-place New York Giants at 4-2-1. The Dallas Cowboys are 3-5 after losing to the Arizona Cardinals on Sunday, while the Eagles are 2-6 and even more banged up than Washington.

Everything remains right in front of the Commanders, which is why a team with a 2-4-1 record would usually become a seller at the trade deadline. In this case, it may not be a bad idea for Washington to put their chips on the table if they believe they have a legitimate shot at winning the division.

#2887. Do the Rams have an impressive win yet this season?

The Los Angeles Rams have won their last two games to get back to .500, but have they done anything yet to make fans confident that the franchise is headed in the right direction?

The Rams have four losses: 49ers 27-7, Denver Broncos 30-26, Buffalo Bills 37-34, and Arizona Cardinals 18-16.

The Rams have four wins: Giants 28-6, Eagles, 24-20, Raiders 35-21, and Chargers 24-9.

The combined record of the four teams that the Rams have lost to is 21-9, and five of those losses belong to the Cardinals. The other three teams have six wins each and are in first place.

The combined record of the four teams the Rams have beaten is 10-19-1, including the winless Chargers. The Giants are in first place but they have feasted on one of the easiest schedules and worst divisions in the NFL.

If the Rams had defeated a team that is good — and they almost did, twice — the perception of L.A. would be different. However, they didn't and football is a game where your reputation is decided by inches.

Do you think the L.A. Rams beating the Giants by 22 points is impressive enough to earn respect? Or do they need to beat a good team (something that they literally can't do until face the Packers on November 25) before praising Sean McVay for winning the last two games?

In my opinion, the Rams don't have an impressive win this season. If anything, their loss to the Broncos may be more impressive than their wins over the Raiders or Giants. Their loss to the Cardinals may cancel that out.

However, we haven't seen L.A. at full strength yet.

If Myles Garrett returns this season, it's like adding a big name at the trade deadline. Davante Adams will be back at some point. Can the rest of the roster stay in tact when it happens? It's impressive to not be even worse with that many injuries, but the Rams still need a game where they showed up early and finished off a good team before fans can be totally confident in a rebound.

#2886. JJ Redick sends strong warning to Lakers bench after pathetic 60-second span

Lakers head coach JJ Redick made it clear that what happened on Sunday night won't occur again.

And that there are consequences, especially for end-of-bench players, for not being ready to play in an NBA game when their number is called.

Los Angeles Lakers head coach JJ Redick wasn't pleased with how his team closed out Sunday's win over the Raptors. NBAE via Getty Images

The Lakers coach thought his team's lead was safe when he subbed out most of his main rotation players – Austin Reaves, Walker Kessler and Matisse Thybulle – with the Lakers leading by 14 points (123-109) with 1:43 left of their home game against the Raptors.

Luka Dončić, who had already scored 41 points by that point, had been out of the game for 45 seconds, with the Lakers and Redick believing the end-of-bench players would put the finishing touches on securing the victory.

Los Angeles Lakers head coach JJ Redick had plenty to be frustrated about with how the team's end-of-bench players performed late in the win over the Raptors. AP Photo/Scott Strazzante

Even though the Lakers did eventually beat the Raptors, it didn't come to fruition as Redick and the team hoped, with the end-of-bench players nearly blowing a double-digit lead in less than a minute.

"I won't make that mistake again," Redick said. "I'll leave the guys in. You gotta play to the final buzzer, and if guys are not ready to play, then I just won't put them in."

After one of the lone rotation players, Quentin Grimes, split a pair of free throws to put the Lakers up 124-109 with 1:24 left, Jamison Battle hit a catch-and-shoot 3-pointer to cut the Raptors' deficit to 12.

A failed lob attempt from Grimes to Jarred Vanderbilt that resulted in a turnover turned into another Battle catch-and-shoot 3 in transition that quickly cut the Lakers' lead to nine points.

And a failed inbounds pass by Bronny James led to the Lakers' lead dropping down to seven after Battle hit a floater on the Raptors' ensuing possession, making it eight straight points in less than 20 seconds of game time for the 2024 undrafted forward out of Ohio State.

Jamison Battle scored eight straight points in less than 20 seconds of game time to make a significant dent in the Lakers' lead. NBAE via Getty Images

Redick had seen enough by the time Jaden Bradley converted an and-1 layup to trim the Raptors' deficit to six points with 44 seconds left (and five points after making the free throw) after the Lakers led by 15 points just 40 seconds prior.

Dončić, Reaves, Kessler and Thybulle all subbed back in for James, Vanderbilt, Jaden Hardy and Cameron Carr (who was making his NBA debut) before Bradley shot his free throw.

The Lakers did just enough to secure the win with its main players back in the game, but it wasn't pretty.

"A lot of times as a player when you kind of get something like that in that point, you kind of look at it as garbage minutes but the reality is, every time you step on the court, you got to treat it like it's minutes to play the game," Kessler said.

"Because my rookie and sophomore year, I got put in the garbage minutes and you got to play to the whistle because the reality is you're only going to get your shot. And I'm not saying that those guys aren't competitors 'cause a lot of them are, but they got to be a lot more more fundamental, for sure."

#2885. I bonds now pay 5.51% — but there are trade-offs. What to know before buying

Now that Series I bonds pay higher interest, the government-backed, nearly risk-free asset could be more attractive for certain investors. But I bonds have downsides, and there are alternatives to consider.

Newly purchased I bonds currently pay 5.51% annual interest through April 30, up from the 4.26% yield offered on purchases made through October.

Amid rising inflation, the variable part of I bond interest rose to 4.08%, while the fixed part increased to 1.40%, the highest since 2006. Combined, the 5.51% composite rate is the biggest yield since November 2022.

The new I bond rate is "very attractive," according to Ken Tumin, founder of DepositQuest.com, a blog that tracks I bond rates, among other deposit accounts.

The variable part of I bond interest resets every six months, based on the consumer price index. For the next six months after purchase, I bonds look "very competitive" compared to other shorter-term options for cash, such as savings accounts or certificates of deposit, according to Tumin.

Meanwhile, the I bond's 1.4% fixed rate stays the same for as long as you own it, which could appeal to longer-term investors, he said.

But I bonds aren't right for everyone. Here are some key things to know before purchasing.

Investors can buy $10,000 of electronic I bonds per person per calendar year via their TreasuryDirect account. You can open additional TreasuryDirect accounts for other family members or businesses to buy more.

I bond interest is tax-deferred until redemption and is not subject to state or local taxes. You can also avoid federal taxes if you use the money for qualified education expenses.

But experts say there are some downsides. The funds are locked up for 12 months from purchase, and you'll lose your last three months of interest if you redeem within five years.

Advisors typically recommend keeping at least three to six months of living expenses in cash for emergency savings. I bonds could boost those reserves, experts say, as long as you keep enough readily accessible cash elsewhere.

"I always say [I bonds are] a secondary emergency fund," said David Enna, founder of Tipswatch.com, a website that tracks Treasury inflation-protected securities, or TIPS, and I bond rates.

Once you own I bonds for five years, you can redeem the money as needed, penalty-free.

With I bond purchase limits, restrictions and some TreasuryDirect complaints, advisors often recommend other options for clients who want to preserve purchasing power.

"For most of my clients, I bonds are more hassle than they're worth," said certified financial planner Eileen Olson, founder of advisory firm Emerald Wealth in Daufuskie Island, South Carolina. "The amount is too small to matter."

On a $2 million portfolio, the $10,000 annual purchase limit is only 0.5%, and it earns about $550 in a year before federal taxes, she said. "That won't protect anyone's lifestyle from inflation."

Instead, Olson prefers TIPS, which don't have purchase limits and can be bought alongside other assets, outside TreasuryDirect.

TIPS have five-, 10- and 30-year terms, with yields ranging from about 2.3% to above 3% for the 30-year term, as of Nov. 2.

However, the right option depends on your investing goals and timeline, experts say.

"For short-term cash, Treasury bills and high-yield savings are simpler," Olson said.

Also backed by the U.S. government, Treasury bills, or T-bills, have terms ranging from four weeks up to 52 weeks. As of Nov. 2, some T-bill yields were well over 4%.

By comparison, the top 1% average of high-yield savings accounts were paying above 4%, according to rate comparison site DepositAccounts. Some one-year certificates of deposit were also offering rates over 4%, the same day, according to Bankrate.

Money market funds are another option. Some of the biggest funds were paying around 4%, as of Nov. 2, according to Crane Data.

#2884. What to watch for in Quenton Nelson's Seahawks debut

As the Seattle Seahawks and Chicago Bears face off on Monday Night Football in Week 8, each will debut a brand-new superstar. Future Pro Football Hall of Fame guard Quenton Nelson will make his Seahawks debut while perennial All-Pro defensive tackle Jeffery Simmons makes his Bears debut. The two faced off just two weeks ago in a Week 6 AFC South matchup between their former teams, the Indianapolis Colts and Tennessee Titans. First, let's break down what you need to know about the monster that just migrated west in Nelson.

The main thing people forget to mention about Nelson is that he's a 96th-percentile athlete as a guard. His physicality is obvious once you watch him play, and his intelligence is apparent — but the athletic traits allow him to utilize all the other facets of his game. His ability to "pull" in the run game at 6-foot-5, 325 pounds, and backed by the strength of a 35-rep (96th-percentile) bench press, is a sight to behold. Pairing those size measurables with 82nd-percentile agility testing helps him reach the 32nd-highest Relative Athletic Score (RAS) of all 927 guards tested since 1987. The score doesn't even include his gargantuan 10.375-inch hands and 33.75-inch arms.

Numbers are cool, but you're ready to watch some football tonight. So, how does it actually look on the field? His recent matchup against Simmons shows how he utilizes his tools against the very best.

Simmons rarely got the better of Nelson in pass protection. Nelson's excellent at being mean when he needs to. He gets violent with his upper body while staying smooth in his lower half. He extended his hands early in that matchup and allowed his sheer strength, arm size and hand size to stonewall Simmons. In the run game, the pancakes flow as naturally as you'll ever see at the NFL level. He consistently drives defenders several yards downfield before they succumb to his momentum, which sometimes doesn't stop until he goes sliding over the top of their body altogether.

You've learned about his athleticism and technique. Lastly, his personality is as perfect as you could imagine. He annihilates players between the whistles, then asks if they're OK and helps them up. Defensive linemen are fueled by hate. Nelson even makes that part of their game hard.

#2883. Washington Commanders Whiteboard: Notes and observations from vital Week 8 win over Eagles

Before the advent of media devices, football coaches at all levels used simple whiteboards to outline plays, map out player positions and explain schemes in real time. Nowadays digital playbooks and tablets have taken over, but coaches still use whiteboards on occasion to impart information quickly and effectively. The following are some quick notes and observations from the Washington Commanders' 27-10 win on Sunday Night Football.

Coming out of a bye week in which Dan Quinn stressed the need for the team to win "four quarters of football," the Washington Commanders came out looking flat to start the game. Jayden Daniels was horribly inaccurate in the first quarter, completing only four of his first 11 attempts for 26 yards on his way to a lackluster first half.

The penalties and miscues Washington's coaching staff had vowed to clean up during the bye week continued to plague the team early as Washington's offense had 4 penalties, 1 dropped pass, 1 fumble, and 1 missed field goal during the first three drives of Sunday night's game. The Commanders would finish with 7 penalties for 50 yards in the first half, after simplifying their offense in an attempt to clean up the pre-snap mistakes. The NFL average for an entire game this season is right around eight per team.

The net result was a 10-0 deficit to start the contest, before Jayden rallied his offense , scoring 13 unanswered points to take the lead before the half. The offense would go on to score 27 total points, and while the bounce back from the Commanders was nice to see, they aren't going to win many games spotting teams multiple scores early while incurring self-inflicted wounds at the same time. Despite the inaccuracies, Jayden Daniels has yet to throw an interception this season.

After failing to limit their opponents to less than 23 points in the first six games of the season, Daronte Jones' defense showed up on Sunday night. They held the Eagles' offense to just 10 points, getting to quarterback Tanner McKee all night and generating pressure on 46.5% of drop backs , a season-high, with 10 pressures and a sack coming on 12 blitzes.

The defense entered the game without two starters on the defensive line, as Javon Kinlaw and Daron Payne were ruled out earlier in the week. Multiple players stepped up in their absence, including Charles Omenihu (4 tackles, 2 SOLO, 1 sack, and 1 TFL) and Shy Tuttle (6 tackles, 4 SOLO, 1 sack with 2 TFLs). Edge defenders Dorance Armstrong and K'Lavon Chaisson gave rookie right tackle Markel Bell fits all night. Chaisson finished with 7 pressures, the second most of career. Overall, the defense had five total sacks on the night.

The secondary also looked improved, with Trey Amos playing significant snaps (87%) in his second game of the season. They kept McKee under 200 yards in passing until the final play of the game, when the Commanders allowed a 34-yard pass to tight end Eli Stowers. Rasul Douglas had his second interception of the season, and Nick Cross was flying around all night, leading the team with 9 tackles (5 solo), including one tackle for a loss. Kain Medrano and Mike Sainristil recorded zero defensive snaps on the night, playing exclusively on special teams.

While the Eagles' offense has not been good, credit the Washington Commanders' defense for getting the job done in prime time.

The absence of Terry McLaurin had many speculating that Antonio Williams was headed towards a break out performance on Sunday night, and while the rookie did play his most offensive snaps (83%) of the season, his production was relatively low. Daniels couldn't find Williams early, with 30 of his 54 total receiving yards coming on a tough reception in the fourth quarter. It was a group effort from the Washington Commanders' pass catchers, with Treylon Burks continuing to act as a deep threat. The former Tennessee Titan led the team in receiving with three receptions on five targets for 85 yards including a long of 54 yards.

The offense was frequently stopped short of the end zone, although kicker Drew Stevens kept putting points on the board, going 4-for-5 on the evening after missing the 55-yarder early. Daniels and offensive coordinator David Blough were able to a secure a touchdown in the second quarter using a variation of the bunch set they showed on Stefon Diggs' Week 1 touchdown. Eventually, Rachaad White would also punch in a rushing touchdown at the end of the game.

The Philadelphia Eagles were missing several key players that contributed to their poor performance, but Washington was missing several important pieces of their own, including Laremy Tunsil, Terry McLaurin, Bill Croskey-Merritt and Daron Payne. While this helps to keep the Commanders' win in perspective, it won't score the team any points going forward.

In some many ways, the Commanders' victory met the bare minimum requirements of what needed to happen Sunday night. It was nice to see the secondary gel and the defensive line finally generate some pressure, but the team continues to suffer from slow starts, penalties, and undisciplined play. While the win preserved the possibility of claiming the division crown in a weak NFC East, Dan Quinn and his staff will need to do a lot more in the coming weeks to take advantage of the improved strength of schedule ahead.

#2882. Warriors rookie drops extremely rare admission after falling short of making history

In Sunday's 124-112 victory against the Los Angeles Clippers, Golden State Warriors rookie phenom Yaxel Lendeborg fell just shy of scoring 40 points. It is the most for a Warriors rookie since Stephen Curry in 2010.

Lendeborg finished with 37 points and was asked why he didn't go for the 40-point figure. He indeed tried, but fell short because of the pressure from his peers.

Yaxel Lendeborg scores 37 points in Sunday's game against the Los Angeles Clippers. NBAE via Getty Images

"I've never been a stat chaser in my life, so just falling into peer pressure like I did just now was pretty much bad on my part," Lendeborg said. "Especially because I'm a guy that just loves ethical hoops. I'm a guy that loves efficient basketball, and I didn't do that the last two or three minutes. So it's kind of bad on my part."

Lendeborg said his teammate De'Anthony Melton approached him and said, "Hey man you need to get 40. You're three points away.' And I'm like, 'Nah, man. It's all good.' And he peer pressured me into doing it."

Yaxel Lendeborg celebrating with Will Richard during his 37 point performance on Sunday night. NBAE via Getty Images

"He said he's going to give me the ball. And he literally like, was holding it just to give me the ball. And I'm like, 'All right, fine. I'll shoot it.' And I missed."

Lendeborg said he tried to barter with Melton about being content with his 37 points, but then Will Richard approached him and said, "You got to do it."

"Everybody was just egging me on, and I kind of just fell into it. But I know next time, I'mma just play in the flow of the game, 'cause they came out of me like four threes in a row, and we almost lost the game because of that. So, next time I'm never doing that again."

It is not often you hear a professional athlete openly talk about trying to pad their stats. Yet, the Warriors rookie is not one for holding back.

Golden State Warriors rookie, Yaxel Lendeborg scores 37 points against the Clippers on Sunday night. Getty Images

"If it comes to me, it does. If I don't get it, then I wasn't meant to get it that day," Lendeborg said. "Imma be way better next time."

Steve Kerr told reporters he's enjoyed watching his rookie shine, saying, "he's playing this way and he really doesn't know the NBA game yet. …He's going to get a lot better."

#2881. Robinhood shares fall as crypto slump weighs on profits, offsetting gains in new businesses

Robinhood shares were down around 8% in pre-market trading on Wednesday as investors digested weaker-than-expected third-quarter earnings despite growth in options trading and prediction markets.

Here's how the brokerage performed in its quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with Wall Street estimates, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Robinhood's net income fell to $468 million, or 51 cents per share, from $556 million, or 61 cents per share, a year ago. Revenue represented a 12% increase from $1.27 billion in the same quarter last year.

Despite that gain, transaction-based revenue of $867 million came in below Wall Street estimates from StreetAccount of $879 million, putting focus on whether Robinhood can sustain momentum across its trading businesses and reduce its reliance on volatile market activity.

Robinhood has been diversifying its business as it tries to expand its brokerage app into a broader financial services platform. Options trading and event contracts have become increasingly important sources of transaction revenue, while the company has continued to invest in wealth management, subscriptions and other offerings.

Transaction-based revenue grew 19% from the same period last year to $867 million, driven primarily by a more than 500% increase in event contracts revenue. Additionally, options revenue grew 31% to $399 million and equities revenue rose 41% to $121 million.

Those gains were partially offset by cryptocurrency revenue, which fell 55% to $120 million.

Chief Financial Officer Shiv Verma told CNBC event contracts on crypto movements did well in the quarter, offering investors another way to express a view on crypto despite the slump in prices and spot trading.

Net deposits on the Robinhood platform totaled $15.7 billion for the quarter, equal to a 17% annualized growth rate based on total platform assets at the end of Q2 2026. Over the past year, net deposits totaled $71 billion, representing 21% growth compared with total platform assets at the end of Q3 2025.

#2879. CNBC's The China Connection newsletter: Nvidia's humanoid push relies on China-made robot hands

Hi, this is Evelyn, writing to you from Beijing. Welcome to the latest edition of The China Connection — a snapshot of what I'm seeing and hearing from local businesses.

Chinese robot companies still underpin many U.S. tech endeavors, especially when it comes to the pain point of robotic hands. Who is one of the latest on Nvidia's radar?

In just a few months, Nvidia has showcased two humanoid robot training packages: one with mechanical hands from Sharpa, and another with hands from Wuji.

Pan Yunzhe founded Wuji in 2019, eventually moving much of the team to Shenzhen and drawing backers such as Ant Group, HSG and Future Capital, according to PitchBook. A demo video last week claimed a Wuji Hand 2 could autonomously spin a pen, despite interference.

The Wuji Hand 2 made it onto a slide Oct. 21 behind Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang as he told a crowd in Berlin about a new humanoid hardware system for training the robots on Nvidia software. The robot body comes from Agile Robots, which has offices in Germany, China and the U.S.

"It will be the flywheel by which we can accelerate development of robotics," Huang said of the hardware system, called Agile One SN. Agile Robots announced the system, which uses Nvidia's Isaac robotics software and Jetson Thor computing platform.

Research institutions, such as Stanford and ETH Zurich, can likely start using the Agile One SN system in early 2027, according to Nvidia.

Stanford and ETH Zurich were also among the institutions Nvidia named in connection with its first reference humanoid design, announced May 31. That system uses a body from Chinese humanoid company Unitree and mechanical hands from Sharpa. It was scheduled for delivery from late 2026.

Nvidia did not immediately respond to a request for comment on whether deliveries had begun, or whether the researchers would try both reference robots, or just one.

Sharpa, headquartered in Singapore, was co-founded by David Li, CEO of Shanghai-based lidar company Hesai. Sharpa has said its manufacturing R&D is in Shanghai.

While these are just two of the many robotic hand makers in China — and globally — they've grown so much in prominence that Google DeepMind research lead Keerthana Gopalakrishnan directly compared the two startups in a podcast last month.

Sharpa Wave is a bit stronger than Wuji, which is closer in size to a typical human hand but has the strength of a ten-year-old, she said. It was not clear whether she was testing the Wuji Hand 2 or an earlier version.

In September, humanoid startup JoyIn's Zhu Mingxuan told me she found Wuji's mechanical hands very light and very good at completing tasks, but prone to heating up while doing so. She said Sharpa's performed better, but were far more expensive.

Hardware is limiting humanoid development, and hands are the main challenge, she said. Zhu said she left U.S. humanoid company Figure last year, where she had also worked on models to help humanoid robots mimic human actions.

As China pushes its humanoid industry to develop more core technologies, mechanical hands are one way companies can try to stand out in the robot race.

Consumer electronics company Xiaomi said last week that, by testing its robotic hand in its car factory, it found that most of the tasks it tested required only a specific range of movement.

And in another sign of just how far along the tech may have come in China, Meta has worked with Chinese startup Fourier for a humanoid prototype, The Information reported Oct. 31, citing sources. Neither company immediately responded to a request for comment.

Why China is desperate to ship cars outside of the country

Car salesman Li Ke said that, other than the Covid-19 pandemic, the "golden September and silver October" season is the slowest this year he's seen in his 26 years on the job.

China industrial profits see strongest growth since June

Profits at large industrial firms rose 14.4% in September from a year earlier, according to official data.

Labubu-maker Pop Mart's shares extend declines as sales plunge

The company reported a 45% to 50% year-on-year drop in third-quarter revenue, with the Americas and Asia-Pacific seeing the steepest sales drops.

#2878. Jim Cramer says these six fashion stocks are buys beyond the AI trade

CNBC's Jim Cramer highlighted six fashion and apparel stocks on Monday for investors looking for alternatives to the struggling AI trade.

While technology stocks have come under pressure in recent weeks, consumer stocks have been resilient. The SPDR S&P Retail ETF (XRT) gained 10% this quarter, while the Technology Select Sector SPDR Fund (XLK) fell 11%. Cramer said falling interest rates could provide an additional boost to apparel companies heading into the holiday shopping season.

"Ralph Lauren, Tapestry, Levi's, VF Corp, Kontoor Brands and Reformation are some of the fashion stocks that we think look best right now," the "Mad Money" host said.

Ralph Lauren remains Cramer's favorite apparel stock, thanks to its consistent growth under CEO Patrice Louvet. The company has continued gaining momentum across women's apparel, accessories and outerwear, and has been strengthening its international presence, particularly in Asia. Although the stock pulled back in August and September, it has since recovered, and Cramer is optimistic ahead of Thursday morning's earnings report.

Tapestry, the parent of Coach and Kate Spade, also reports Thursday. Cramer likes Coach's position in the accessible luxury market, particularly its popularity among younger female consumers. However, shares have fallen 20% since the company's August earnings report, when guidance failed to impress investors following a massive multiyear rally. "I hope this Thursday's report can represent the start of a comeback for Tapestry," he said.

Levi Strauss is another potential turnaround story. Shares fell below $20 following disappointing earnings a few weeks ago, but CEO Michelle Gass told Cramer that the company had moved quickly to address inventory shortages in popular styles. The stock has since rebounded more than 20% from its post-earnings low, though Cramer still considers it inexpensive at roughly 14 times earnings, with a 2.8% dividend yield. "I think this is a good option to play the broader strength in denim that we keep hearing about," he said.

VF Corp, which owns The North Face, Vans and Timberland, is showing signs of improvement after years of struggles, particularly at Vans. Cramer pointed to encouraging results from a recent Piper Sandler teen survey and a strong quarterly report as evidence that the sneaker brand's turnaround may be gaining traction. Shares have risen roughly 14% since he recommended the stock about two weeks ago.

Kontoor Brands offers another opportunity tied to denim and corporate restructuring. The company plans to sell its Lee brand for up to $1 billion, allowing it to focus on Wrangler and outdoor apparel maker Helly Hansen. Cramer expects the sale to be followed by substantial share repurchases and said the remaining businesses have room to grow. Shares trade at roughly 14 times earnings and offer a dividend yield near 3%.

Cramer's more speculative pick is Reformation. The women's fashion brand initially struggled after its IPO in late July, but Cramer said it "has great growth, it's profitable, and it has a differentiated shopping experience with a technology kicker." Cramer highlighted the stock in late September; shares surged more than 31% in October.

#2877. Knicks Bulletin: ‘If you skip steps doing that, you’re going to die’

On Jose Alvarado's availability for Monday's game after practicing on Sunday:

"I have not talked to our medical people."

On Tyler Kolek's role while Alvarado remains sidelined and going forward:

"Tyler is definitely going to keep playing while Jose is out. When he comes back, we'll figure it out."

On finally having a full practice over the weekend amid early-season issues:

"When you have an opportunity to practice, you hope that you can address some of the things that bothered you and get a little bit better."

On failing to deal with opposing bench players getting hot:

"I'm OK with that because sometimes guys are going to get hot and make shots. Now, are there little things we can do to correct it? Yes.

"But what I was concerned with is our first half inability to control the dribble-drive, OK, and then how bad our turnovers were. Those were the two biggest things that I had concern with."

On the need for rebuilding chemistry after the championship season:

"You're starting all over. It's like climbing a mountain. We're climbing a mountain as a team and we conquer the mountain, then you gotta come back down, take some time off, then you gotta climb another mountain. It's the same thing. And when you climb that mountain the second time, you're starting from base camp again. And if you skip steps doing that, you're going to die. It's not that tragic for us. When you're sitting at 2-4, people equate it to that sometimes, but it's the same thing, taking each step the right way and embracing it and trying to get a little better."

On why the Knicks must experience growing pains again despite having won the title:

"If we don't go through our growing pains and we can't get through our growing pains, for whatever reason — because we don't want to go through it being that hard again or we think we know or whatever it may be — if we can't get through our growing pains, we have no chance.

"It's like anything you do in life. When you complete something and you have to do it again, you're not just starting right here [at the top] when you do it again. You're starting here [at the bottom] and you're building up. And sometimes, your second or your third year doing it, you're getting here [at the top] a little quicker. But you still gotta go through all the necessary steps to get there."

On the Knicks' struggles finishing in the paint:

"If you think about it, if you're shooting the way we're shooting the ball at the perimeter, it's like in football, if you're not able to pass the ball, they're gonna put eight guys in the box. It's the same thing. If we're not making shots, they're going to do this [collapse in the paint] and it's gonna be harder to finish in the paint.

"And so eventually, at least the green [high-quality] 3s, we're gonna have to continue generating green threes but we're gonna have to make some green threes. But in the same breath, while we're not doing that, we gotta be better in other areas to compensate for our ability to shoot the ball right now."

On the Knicks' problems stopping Philly in the paint:

"They were getting in our paint left and right, right and left, and it was really, really easy doing it. If it's a post-up game or something like that, OK, maybe, I don't know. But to continue to have that ball driven in the paint, especially in the first half when they shot 5 of 17 or whatever it is from the 3-point line, was not good."

On the Knicks' defensive communication and inconsistent execution:

"It's not just one thing. It's a little thing here, little thing there, little thing there, and that's why it adds up. We're competitive when we need to be, and that's why all our games that we're in, we have a chance down the stretch.

"But trying to make sure we continue to do the little things throughout the course of the game is gonna be huge for us because we're not doing it right now."

On the Knicks failing to execute fundamentals:

"It was almost like we were just gonna show up and go hoop and not doing the little things that we need to do to go get a win."

On trusting the Knicks' decision-making and not being afraid to make mistakes:

"We have the right intentions. We've just gotta be confident in our decision-making and don't be afraid to fail. We have the confidence in this locker room to do it. We all work hard. We see it every day. Everything's going to fall into place as it should be."

On the condition of his sprained left wrist heading into Monday's game:

On the Knicks needing to rediscover their championship form more consistently:

"We gotta find a way to be that magical team we were last year at a more consistent pace. You don't always want to just wait until the end to find that magic. Lightning don't strike twice, so we gotta be that team right now and we gotta continue to build off of what we did last year."

On the Knicks' offensive struggles and failure to execute:

"We just haven't played our best, and we haven't executed at a high level."

On the Knicks' stagnant offense contributing to early deficits:

"I think when that's happened, we're just coming in a little stagnant offensively. Forced into either bad shots or turnovers. And then, you know, stops are harder."

On his affection for the Nets and Suns, his former teams:

"No matter them or Phoenix, people I played for, there's always a spot in my heart for them and I always remember the time being there."

On the Knicks' practice session and correcting their mistakes:

On rebuilding the chemistry the Knicks developed during their championship season:

"Yeah, that's why I think for us, we just gotta continue to grow. Last time we played was in June. I think [we were] probably the most polished as a team we were at the end of the year, as we should be. So, I know it's tough to see last time we played to the beginning of the year, and obviously we should be better than we are, me personally. But it takes time and just not skipping steps and just kinda going through the process and keep learning, get back in that rhythm."

"I'm sure it'll be a lot of fun. No, I don't know nothing about (the Knicks' winning streak against Brooklyn). I live in the present, baby. … Focus on what we need to do to get a win, build off of what we did [Sunday] and stick together as a group."

On opposing fans taking over Barclays Center during Nets home games:

"I don't give a shit. I've been on teams that weren't good and didn't have the excitement around them. Then you start winning and the whole arena is there to see your team and cheer. So I think that's just part of being a fan. For us as a team, we got to give them something to root for."

On whether he sees himself as responsible for developing Brooklyn's next generation of fans:

"Oh, hell no. It doesn't really matter what team I play for. I was at my son's basketball game yesterday and it was like, Man, I'm a Nets fan. You're going to kill it this year. Then I get on the other side and it's, Thank you for everything you've done for the Knicks' …"

#2876. Florida's Top Storyline Has Everything to do With Denzel Aberdeen

The University of Florida will have one of the more intriguing games of the opening night in college basketball, taking on a No. 14-ranked Miami team that many believe has a chance to be one of the best squads in not just the ACC, but all of the country, with national championship expectations.

As for Florida, there's a strong argument to be made that this is the best team in the country by a somewhat wide margin.

All Florida has done throughout the past couple of years is win at a high level, and with all the guys returning, as well as some transfer additions, there's no reason why the Gators can't be among the best teams in college basketball.

Perhaps the biggest storyline, however, is Denzel Aberdeen.

Aberdeen, now a fifth-year senior, started his career at the University of Florida. He played three seasons for the Gators, starting in just five games throughout that span.

The young man took a chance by going to the University of Kentucky last season and really showed why he's one of the better players in college basketball.

He ended up averaging 13.5 points per game, shooting 36.3% from three-point range on 4.8 attempts per game. Throughout the offseason, there were plenty of questions about whether he was going to be eligible or not. Florida got some good news in October that he was going to be good to play, and as of now, it seems like he's in a good spot to play for the rest of the season.

The Athletic recently named him one of the more interesting players in the country, however, and a lot of it is because of everything that happened throughout the offseason.

"Few better represent the state of college athletics than Aberdeen, who transferred to Florida from rival Kentucky a year after transferring to Kentucky from rival Florida, and whose participation was made possible by a temporary injunction.

"It's still possible another judge could take that eligibility away at some point. For now, Aberdeen is a crucial cog for a Florida team with a bountiful frontcourt and a need for better guard play than a year ago," Joe Rexrode of The Athletic wrote.

Florida has to be mindful that it could lose an eligibility case throughout the year with Aberdeen, though this is something that's going to happen to others in college basketball, not just Florida.

It remains uncertain right now what the NCAA plans to do with all of this, but it certainly does lead to some intrigue about how teams are going to look in March compared to November, when a lot of these guys are playing.

There are also a lot of players around the country not playing to start the season, so to put things as nicely as possible, this college basketball season is going to be absolutely ridiculous with all of the things that pop up over the next couple of weeks to months.

#2875. Trey Zuhn III gets vote of confidence from Klint Kubiak after Las Vegas Raiders Week 8 win over New York Jets

Right guard has been a trouble spot on the Las Vegas Raiders' depth chart so far this season. Through eight games/weeks, the Raiders have already had three starters at the position: Jackson Powers-Johnson, Caleb Rogers and Trey Zuhn III.

Week 8 against the New York Jets was Zuhn's crack at it, making his first NFL start, and the rookie played well enough to get a vote of confidence from head coach Klint Kubiak.

"I thought Trey was calm in there. I thought he was well prepared," Kubiak said during his Monday post-game press conference (h/t The Athletic's Sam Warren). "That was not a surprise, but I thought he played well. I think there are things that he's going to want to improve on. But I was pleased, along with the coaching staff, with the effort that he put on the field and what his future looks like for us."

Kubiak didn't outright say that Zuhn will remain the starting right guard moving forward, but it's hard to imagine that the third-round pick will be sent back to the bench after his performance. According to Sumer Sports, he recorded three "impact plays", which tied for the third-most of the team's offensive linemen and only two behind the team leader, center Tyler Linderbaum.

Additionally, the Raiders struggled to run the ball during a four-game losing streak in October, and the offense recorded the most rushing yards (95) it's had since the team's last win in Week 3. Also, quarterback Kirk Cousins was only sacked once on Sunday, giving even less reason to make a change to the offensive line heading into next week's matchup against the San Francisco 49ers.

Kubiak dropped a few other interesting nuggets of information on Monday, as a handful of other highlights from his presser are below:

On whether the workload split between running backs Mike Washington Jr. (25 carries) and Ashton Jeanty (two carries) will continue, he said: "It's always a moving target…Who is playing the best ball at the time. It's a 17-game regular season where things change every week. (via Warren)

Cornerback Darien Porter (groin) left the game after one play, and his status for Week 9 is up in the air as the Raiders will monitor his progress throughout the week. (via Warren)

Jermod McCoy replaced Porter initially instead of Hezekiah Masses because Kubiak said the coaching staff wanted to get McCoy some exposure, and "that's going to continue". (via Warren)

On McCoy's and Masses' performances: "I think obviously Zeke made some plays for us. Had a big tipped pass. Jermod, his first action, he's gonna continue to get better. He's got plenty of things to improve on, as does Zeke, as does DP, as does everybody. But we feel like we're gonna continue to rotate those guys through." (via Warren)

Veteran cornerback Eric Stokes was picked on by the Jets, but the Raiders never considered benching him, according to Kubiak. "I think the Jets had a really good plan overall. And I think, again, when you play defensive back in the NFL, at some point you're gonna get beat, but how do you respond when that happens? And when a defensive back gets beat, everybody knows about it. But if a guard loses in the run game, it's not discussed as much. So you gotta have thick skin; you've gotta be able to move on to the next down. And so, I'm really glad we got No. 5." (via Warren)

Defensive tackle Tonka Hemingway only played 13 snaps, partially due to a stinger, which the team will monitor throughout the week. (via Warren)

#2874. Lakers’ JJ Redick exposes NBA’s officiating flaw after win over Raptors

When it came to officiating the Raptors' aggressive perimeter defense, Sunday night went exactly as JJ Redick described it would.

Before his team's victory over the Raptors at Crypto.com Arena, the Lakers coach was asked whether there's anything the Raptors do tactically to be as proficient with forcing turnovers as they are.

Los Angeles Lakers head coach JJ Redick wasn't happy with how the Raptors defense had been officiated on Sunday. AP Photo/Scott Strazzante

The Raptors entered Sunday ranked No. 1 in turnovers forced and defensive turnover percentage, but also committed the most personal fouls per game.

"If you look at the team – and this is not like a shot, this is just reality – a lot of the teams that force turnovers, they foul a lot," Redick said. "And so by fouling on every possession, the refs are just naturally gonna allow you leeway.

"Oklahoma City does that. Detroit, watching this team, this version of this Raptors team on film, they remind me a lot of like last year's Detroit team where they're just going to foul you on every possession. And obviously they've also been called for the most fouls, and they've given up the most free throws, so that's part of it."

Los Angeles Lakers head coach JJ Redick sees flaws in how NBA officials referee teams with aggressive perimeter defenders. NBAE via Getty Images

The Lakers finished with 24 turnovers that directly resulted in 30 points for the Raptors, who also committed 26 personal fouls.

While taking care of the ball has been a struggle for the Lakers, Redick saw what happened Sunday as a reflection of how NBA officials referee games in the modern era.

"I mean you guys can see it – some of those turnovers are because we're legitimately getting fouled," Redick said. "I told one of the referees this 'cause all these guys do it, all the on ball defenders [do it] now. What they do is they pick you up and then they grab the arm you're not dribbling with and then they try to steal the ball from you.

"Famously Cason Wallace did it with TJ McConnell [in Game 7 of the 2025 NBA finals]. And that's just the NBA right now.

"It's really hard when you play teams like this that are just fouling every possession. We did enough to play through it. But a lot of those turnovers are just that and then you can point to some decision making [with] some passes, all that stuff. Those naturally happen within the flow of the game."

The Lakers aren't shy with their issues with officials. Getty Images

Lakers superstar Luka Dončić, who's often being guarded by the team's best defenders, has noticed the shift in how perimeter defenders are officiated over the last couple of years.

"In the last two years, the game has become way more physical," Dončić said.

"They allow way more contact. But obviously like you said, the refs can't call everything. We just need to learn from them. We need to do the same things. Use that on the defensive end. You got to use aggression towards them. We just got to learn from that and we should do all the same on defense."

#2873. Trump Account holders to receive SpaceX stock from Gwynne Shotwell in December

Eligible Trump Account holders will begin receiving donated shares of SpaceX stock next month, according to an X post on Friday.

"Robert and I are thrilled that our gift of over 2 million shares of SpaceX stock will be distributed to children's Trump accounts in December!" President and COO Gwynne Shotwell wrote in the post.

The gift from Shotwell and her husband will provide a share apiece to around 2 million Trump Accounts holders ages 11 to 17 who live in ZIP codes with median family incomes of $57,000 or less, or in ZIP codes with too few residents for a median family income to be reported by the U.S. Census Bureau. These families should receive a notice by Monday, Shotwell wrote on X.

"In a few weeks, an additional gift will be finalized for eligible 11 to 17-year-old children in military families serving overseas, children in foster care, and more children whose MFI is $90,000 or less living in counties near our home in the great state of Texas," she also wrote.

Parents or guardians can check eligibility for the donation through Invest America, a nonprofit advocacy group, and track account activity with the Trump Accounts app.

Trump Accounts, also known as 530A accounts, are open to any U.S. child under 18 with a Social Security number.

Children born from 2025 through 2028 may also receive a one-time $1,000 deposit from the U.S. Department of the Treasury.

Other funds may also be available, depending on certain criteria. In a separate funding announcement in October, Elon Musk wrote in an X post that he plans to give every kid in America a share of SpaceX sometime in 2027, which may also come via Trump Accounts.

The U.S. Department of the Treasury rolled out temporary regulations at the end of September that allow Trump Accounts to hold donated individual stocks, a change from previous guidelines that only allowed diversified, low-cost index funds.

The move to permit stock donations could pave the way for more large-scale philanthropic gifts, Internal Revenue Service CEO Frank Bisignano told CNBC's "Squawk Box" on Oct. 8.

On Oct. 29, Bisignano announced his own donation to Trump Accounts for eligible children ages 2 to 8 living in 11234, Bisignano's hometown ZIP code, according to The Bisignano Family Fund.

#2871. Crude oil prices sell off more than 7% on Iran peace talk hopes ahead of U.S. midterms

Crude oil prices fell sharply Monday as the presidents of the U.S. and Iran signalled they are open to a diplomatic solution to the conflict that has disrupted energy markets for eight months.

U.S. West Texas Intermediate futures shed 7.4% to close at $96.46 a barrel, the biggest daily loss since July 27. Brent crude, the international benchmark, lost 7.6% to settle at $104.77 a barrel.

President Donald Trump told reporters Monday that Iran wants to "make a deal and that could happen." U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio said over the weekend that Trump prefers not to use military force against Iran and would like to resolve the conflict diplomatically.

Trump has previously said that he will not attack Iran before the U.S. midterms. But the oil market had priced in the risk of a major military escalation after the elections, gaining more than 10% in October.

The oil market has sold off during previous phases of the war on rumors of a possible settlement, only for the conflict to subsequently escalate and spike prices again.

Trump "hasn't made a decision" on whether to strike Iran again, Rubio told reporters.

"What the president expresses to you is incredible confidence that if we needed to do something militarily, we would have the ability to do it," the secretary of State said. "But we would prefer to do it peacefully."

Iran's President Masoud Pezeshkian said Tehran is seeking to resolve problems through dialogue, according to state-owned Islamic Republic News Agency. Iran's Supreme Leader Ayatollah Mojtaba Khamenei has granted the government certain authorities to do this, Pezeshkian said.

Iran's foreign minister, Abbas Araghchi, said an agreement with the U.S. is possible if Washington "demonstrates goodwill" and is "truly seeking a peaceful solution," according to IRNA.

Araghchi said mediators are discussing the proposal Iran made during the United Nations General Assembly in September. Tehran expects the U.S. to lift sanctions, reduce its military presence in the region to previous levels and lift the blockade, the foreign minister said.

But Khamenei on Saturday called for Tehran's armed forces to meet "any level or type of threat" with "an immediate, impactful, and geographically unrestricted response."

Reports about diplomatic efforts will have a big impact on crude oil prices because much of the market's recent gains were risk premium in anticipation of a possible escalation, according to Ryan McKay, director of commodity strategy at TD Securities.

"With that said, so long as there is no concrete conclusion to the war, and tanker and Houthi attacks remain prevalent, we do not anticipate a complete erosion of the risk premium via diplomacy headlines alone," McKay said.

— CNBC's Lim Hui Jie contributed to this report.

#2870. The NFL, Caleb Williams shoe dispute takes center stage on Monday night

Monday night's game between the Bears and Seahawks in Seattle comes with a subplot. Other than the ongoing Joe Buck and Troy Aikman drama.

The league has twice fined Chicago quarterback Caleb Williams for displaying an "unauthorized logo" (i.e., "the logo of a company that doesn't pay us") on his shoes. After the Week 6 game against the Falcons, the league fined Williams $11,941. After the Week 7 game against the Patriots, the league fined Williams $23,882 — twice the amount of the first fine.

Williams has said he will continue to wear the Caddix cleats, citing safety considerations. Unlike most if not all other NFL players who use Caddix footwear, Williams declines to cover the identifying markings with tape.

While the controversy calls back to former Bears quarterback Jim McMahon's battles with the league over his Adidas headband during a playoff game (after being fined $5,000, he went with "ROZELLE" for the NFC Championship and "POW-MIA" for the Super Bowl) and to Bears linebacker Brian Urlacher's whopping $100,000 fine for wearing a VitaminWater hat to Super Bowl XLI media day, Williams's choice of footwear is rooted in the basic belief that the shoes promote player safety.

As to the refusal to cover the shoes with tape to hide the Caddix markings, it's unclear whether he's concerned about undermining the performance of the shoe or whether he's simply trying to prove a point.

The NFL is clearly trying to prove a point, by fining him repeatedly. And while both prior fines are pending on appeal, a third fine is inevitable unless Williams bends.

Regardless, the next chapter arrives tonight. Neither of the fine letters threaten Williams with preventing him from playing if he doesn't tape over the logos and markings. If the league decides to do that, Williams will have a pretty big decision to make.

Coach Ben Johnson was asked about that possible outcome on Saturday. "He's all about the team, and I know he's not going to hurt the team," Johnson said.

The question of whether Johnson would be "hurting the team" is a matter of perspective. Williams wouldn't be "hurting the team" by refusing to play; the NFL would be "hurting the team" by refusing to let Williams play in his preferred shoes.

Either way, both sides have been stubborn. It seems that a compromise could be reached, somehow.
