#2868. Anthropic reports $17 billion in Q3 revenue, purchase commitments of $895.4 billion in revised S-1

Anthropic reported updated financials as part of an amended Form S-1 filing with the Securities and Exchange Commission on Monday, giving investors a more comprehensive look into the state of its business.

The artificial intelligence company said it generated $33.24 billion in revenue for the nine months ended Sept. 30, up roughly 1,200% from the same period last year. It reported a $104 billion net loss during the period, though most of that was due to the change in fair value of convertible notes and derivative liabilities. Anthropic had a loss of $25.1 billion over the same stretch in 2025.

Anthropic unveiled its highly anticipated prospectus last week, bringing the company one step closer to a potentially historic initial public offering. The initial filing only included the Claude maker's financials through the first half of the year.

During its third quarter, Anthropic generated roughly $17 billion in revenue, up about 1,127% from the same period last year. Revenue growth slowed on a quarter-to-quarter basis, however. Third-quarter revenue increased around 47%, a steep drop from 144% growth in Q2 and 132% in Q1.

The company reported a net loss of $23.29 billion during its third quarter.

As the 5-year-old company prepares to kick off its roadshow, it will have to reckon with growing market jitters around the AI buildout. Wall Street has been worried that rising interest rates will make debt unaffordable for many data center developers and neoclouds, and quarterly reports from Microsoft, Alphabet and Amazon last week added fuel to those concerns.

The hyperscalers' aggressive AI spending is eating into their cash flows, causing some investors to worry that computing capacity may not come online quickly enough to meet Anthropic's demands. Anthropic gets much of its capacity from Amazon, Google and SpaceX, but it also relies on neoclouds for some compute as it races to expand as quickly as possible.

In the amended S-1, Anthropic said it has entered into purchase commitments and lease agreements with an aggregate committed spend of about $895.4 billion, "substantially all of which is related to compute and infrastructure."

Anthropic said its compute and infrastructure expenses increased by $14.4 billion for the nine months ended Sept. 30, up 329% compared with the same period last year.

"As our compute and infrastructure capacity grows, the associated compute and infrastructure expenses have increased," Anthropic said in the filing.

The company's CEO, Dario Amodei, co-founded Anthropic alongside a handful of other researchers who defected from OpenAI, now its chief rival. OpenAI confidentially filed its prospectus with the SEC in June, but its executives have signaled that it's aiming for a 2027 debut.

Anthropic has worked to position itself as more safety-conscious than its competitors, and its S-1 includes more than 90 pages of risk factors. The company highlights risks, including potential political headwinds, its complex infrastructure needs as well as the difficulties that can come from balancing safety and commercial objectives.

"We consider it our personal responsibility to help the world understand these risks, to prevent or mitigate them when they arise from our AI models, and to work with governments and the rest of the industry to address them at a societal level," Anthropic's co-founders said in the filing.

#2867. Supreme Court rejects DOJ's bid to block California from counting disputed ballots

The Supreme Court, in an order Monday evening, denied the Department of Justice's last-minute request to temporarily block California from counting mail-in ballots from about 20,000 voters who it suggested may not be U.S. citizens.

The one-sentence order came hours after the DOJ sought the injunction, and on the eve of Election Day.

The Supreme Court did not explain its reason for the denial, which had no noted dissents. The order came after Justice Elena Kagan presented the DOJ's request to the full court, which did not seek a response from California officials before issuing the decision.

The swift ruling is yet another courtroom blow to the Trump administration, which is engaged in multiple other legal fights related to the midterm elections.

Those contests will determine which political party holds majorities in the House and Senate. Pollsters and analysts widely predict Democrats will make significant gains.

The DOJ's application to the Supreme Court came three days after the 9th Circuit U.S. Court of Appeals rejected the department's attempt to overturn a federal district court judge's ruling that allowed the California ballots to be counted.

That same day, another federal district judge in a separate case in California barred DOJ from threatening election workers with criminal prosecution for refusing to stop processing ballots or resisting other demands.

During a hearing Monday in that second case, a DOJ lawyer admitted to Judge Trina Thompson that the lists of purported noncitizens on California's voter rolls had a "high error rate," MS NOW reported. But that lawyer also argued the errors are evidence of why election officials need to examine those rolls and make sure that everyone on them is a U.S. citizen.

Solicitor General D. John Sauer, in his application to the Supreme Court on Monday morning, said, "In this case, this Office has been informed that the United States has identified more than 20,000 individuals who appear to be registered to vote in California based on publicly available information at the time that information was received, but who, in the United States' view, are likely not U.S. citizens."

Sauer said "the United States is likely to succeed on the merits of its appeal" of the lower court ruling denying the DOJ's request.

"The district court concluded that the United States lacks Article III standing to bring this suit, but the United States suffers a judicially cognizable 'injury to its sovereignty' from the 'violation of its laws,'" Sauer said in the petition. "Once California commingles the challenged ballots with lawful ones, there is no way to get the challenged ballots back."

The DOJ sued California on Oct. 15 in U.S. District Court in Los Angeles. The complaint sought to prevent state election officials from tabulating mail-in ballots "from likely non-citizens in the upcoming federal election until the voter's citizenship status can be determined."

The DOJ previously gave Los Angeles County officials a thumb drive containing names of voters the department suggested could be noncitizens, who by law are barred from voting in federal elections.

Last Wednesday, Judge David Carter denied the DOJ's request that he order California officials to set aside mail ballots from alleged noncitizens named on a list prepared by the Department of Homeland Security, and not count their votes until their citizenship is confirmed.

"This case is about the right of every American citizen to vote in a free and fair election," Carter said in his ruling.

"Never before in American history has the federal government, on the eve of an election, sought to coerce a state and local government to abandon their own Constitutional elections procedures and bend to the will of the Executive Branch," Carter wrote.

"In bringing this lawsuit, the Executive would have this Court believe that it reigns as a sole sovereign. But California and its people are sovereign, too."

The DOJ then appealed that ruling to the 9th Circuit. A three-judge panel on that court denied the appeal in a 2-1 ruling on Friday

"There is a material risk that granting the United States its requested relief would result in disenfranchisement," said the ruling by the majority, Circuit Judges Mark Bennett and Gabriel Sanchez. Bennett was appointed by President Donald Trump, and Sanchez was appointed by former President Joe Biden.

On Sunday, a federal judge dismissed a DOJ lawsuit that sought to block Alaska election officials from immediately certifying the result of the U.S. Senate election there if incumbent Republican Sen. Dan Sullivan loses that race by a margin that could be attributable to voter confusion about another candidate with his name.

#2866. $6.3 billion Binance hack is largest crypto theft on record

Binance said Friday that a security incident impacted about $6.3 billion in client assets, with around $3 billion frozen on-chain, marking the largest crypto hack on record.

The company later added it began recovering assets impacted by the breach after pledging to cover losses using its Secure Asset Fund for Users, which the company created in 2018 as a protection for clients in the event of a hack.

It wasn't clear how the attack took place. The world's largest crypto exchange initially said the hack may involve "a compromised DevOps engineer's laptop, which allowed an attacker to gain access to a service container and submit unauthorized withdrawal transactions." However, Co-CEO Richard Teng later said the attack involved a more complex process rather than the misuse of a single engineer's permissions.

The FBI said Friday it was aware of the incident and was in touch with "relevant public and private partners to understand the scope of the activity, evaluate ​potential impacts, ​and ⁠coordinate appropriate investigative steps."

It wasn't clear either who was responsible for the breach. But TRM Labs, a blockchain intelligence and AI investigations company, pointed to North Korean hackers as the potential culprits. "Several features of this attack match earlier North Korea-linked heists, including the Bybit hack and the KelpDAO hack in April 2026: small Tornado Cash withdrawals funding an operator wallet, staging that began well before the theft, and signatures obtained in advance then executed in one burst," the company said in a post.

Blockchain data platform Chainalysis also said this appears "be the work of North Korean cybercrime group TraderTraitor. This group has repeatedly targeted the cryptocurrency industry, exchanges, DeFi protocols, and investors, to raise funds for the regime. We base our intelligence assessment on on-chain activity observed in the hours following the hack."

Binance temporarily suspended withdrawals while it overhauls its withdrawal security systems and has revoked the affected address authorizations, Teng said. The exchange is also working with law enforcement and blockchain intelligence firms to trace and recover the stolen funds, while deposits remain unaffected.

Binance co-founder Yi He said deposits remained unaffected and continued to operate normally, while the exchange was working on enhancements to its withdrawal service and related address controls. It expects to begin resuming withdrawal services within the next 48 hours.

"We have found no evidence of any wallet private key leak. We will share a detailed technical post-mortem once the investigation is complete and the facts are fully verified," Binance said.

The announcement followed moves by several crypto firms, including Circle Internet Group, Tether and Ripple, to freeze funds tied to the hack.

Exploits are not uncommon in the crypto space. In 2025, Bybit suffered roughly $1.5 billion in losses due to a hack. More recently, Bitget lost more than $350 million worth of digital assets in a cyber theft incident. Over the past several years, bad actors have also drained billions of dollars in crypto from decentralized exchanges.

This week's breach "proves our exact point in proposing regulations for crypto markets earlier this month," Michael Selig, chairman of the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, said Friday in a CNBC "Squawk on the Street" interview. The U.S. derivatives regulator on Oct. 5 proposed a framework that would effectively put crypto exchanges under federal government oversight — after Congress failed to approve legislation to regulate the asset.

"The real issue here is that these are unregulated offshore exchanges, and they don't have the same sorts of safeguards that we have in our financial markets here in the United States. We want to offer a federal option to these exchanges to come and register with the CFTC, and they would be subject to the same sorts of cyber security safeguards that we have in our markets today," he said.

Bitcoin fell more than 4% to $77,187.60 on Friday as the hack weighed on crypto markets.

Circle CEO Jeremy Allaire said Thursday evening in a social media post that the company had frozen more than 800 million in USD Coin associated with the incident. USDT stablecoin issuer Tether CEO Paolo Ardoino also said in on X that his firm had frozen more than 1.5 billion of its stablecoin that may have been related to a potential breach.

Jack McDonald, Ripple's senior vice president of stablecoins, said it was actively freezing affected funds held in RLUSD, its U.S. dollar-backed stablecoin.

#2863. Stock market after midterm elections: What S&P 500 history shows

Markets are sweating it out ahead of the midterm elections, but the event historically heralds strong performance for stocks — regardless of whether it's a red or blue wave, split decision, or status quo in Washington.

The S&P 500 tends to perform well after both midterm and presidential elections. According to a CNBC analysis of S&P 500 returns after elections from 1980 through 2024, the index rose an average of 0.2% after one day and one week, and 1.1% after one month across both presidential and midterm elections.

A year later, the index gained an average of 13.7% after midterms and 13.8% after presidential elections. Returns were positive in 91% of cases. And the S&P 500 was up after all 11 midterms from 1982 through 2022.

Short-term returns were less reliable, particularly after presidential contests, which produced slightly negative average returns after one day and one week.

"There's some anxiousness before these elections," Ryan Detrick, CNBC contributor and chief market strategist at Carson Group, told CNBC. "Once you are through it, it's a big relief and markets tend to do pretty well."

Still, the market usually prefers divided government: one party in charge of the White House and the other holding at least one house of Congress.

Across 10 midterm and five presidential elections that left no single party in charge, the S&P 500 rose an average 15% after one year. One-year returns were positive in 14 out of 15 cases. By comparison, the index gained an average of 11.5% over the year after elections that resulted in single-party control.

"If you have a huge red wave or a big blue wave, markets actually kind of don't like that as much because you have too much spending one way and too much power," Detrick said.

Leaving Democrats in control of Congress with a Republican in the White House — a scenario that may emerge from the elections on Tuesday — was also positive. A CNBC analysis found that after the three midterms (1986, 1990 and 2006) when Democrats controlled both houses and a Republican held the presidency, the S&P 500 averaged a 0.5% decline on the reaction day but was up 3.4% a month later and 10.9% after a year.

Granted, elections may be coincident events to factors that matter far more for market performance.

The S&P 500 plunged 22.1% in the year after the 2000 elections due to the dotcom bust, marking the worst post-election performance since 1980. Conversely, the S&P 500's best post-election year was a 38.3% gain after the 2020 elections, when markets mounted a massive comeback from the COVID-induced selloff.

Investors should bear in mind that Federal Reserve policy and the corporate earnings cycle ultimately matter more than elections, according to Société Générale.

Stocks declined in the aftermath of the 2018 midterms, for instance, when Democrats wrested control of the House during President Donald Trump's first term. More importantly, the Fed tightened monetary policy that year, raising interest rates four times, and the S&P 500 ended the year down 6.2%, though the market was up 11.7% a year after the election.

The big swing factor now is AI and whether declines in tech will continue. As long as the AI wave is still moving and the economy keeps growing, "this bull market is probably still alive and well," Detrick says.

But a Democratic sweep of the House and Senate would almost certainly pressure the AI infrastructure buildout and could lead to more retrenchment.

The bond market is also skittish with yields rising globally and a mini debt crisis in France. And the war with Iran could get worse post-election if President Trump follows through on threats of more strikes or Iran escalates.

"Seasonality isn't destiny, and worries around Iran, AI and France won't mysteriously vanish," said Jim Reid, Deutsche Bank's global head of macro and thematic research, in a note.

If history is any guide, we could see a Santa rally and S&P 500 gains after the midterms. But this is still an AI-driven market, with geopolitics, the Fed, and bond market very much in charge.

— CNBC's Chris Hayes contributed reporting.

#2861. The midterm election could reshape the muni bond market. Here’s what investors need to know

The outcome of Tuesday's election is expected to have broad implications across the market, including municipal bonds.

The spotlight has been on the battle for congressional control and whether Democrats will wrest the House of Representatives, the Senate or both from Republicans. But for the muni market, what happens at the state level could have bigger repercussions — at least in the near term.

There is an array of initiatives on ballots around the nation that could change tax laws and add to bond supply. Taxes matter to muni investors since the income received is free of federal tax, as well as state tax if the holder lives in the state where the bond is issued.

Depending on the location, ballot measures are up for approval that would either provide tax breaks, or raise taxes on the wealthy.

"It's a complete bifurcation between red and blue states," said Dan Close, head of municipals at Nuveen. "Blue states are trying to put in place graduated income tax structures, trying to capture more revenue from high-income earners. Red states are promoting affordability by trying to cut property taxes."

Red states are those that predominantly vote Republican, while blue states swing toward Democrats.

For instance, California has a proposition known as the "billionaire tax," which would impose a one-time levy equal to 5% of the wealth of certain taxpayers with at least $1 billion in assets. The state also has a measure on the ballot that would make a temporary tax hike permanent on individuals earning over $371,000, adjusted annually for inflation. The tax is currently set to expire in 2031.

"The impact to the muni bond market will be to make the exemption more valuable," Close said of the possible passage of tax increases. Investors will likely seek shelter in the muni market, which would send bond prices higher, he noted.

It is a different story in Florida, where voters are set to decide whether to increase the Sunshine State's homestead exemption for non-school district property taxes. Qualified homeowners currently have a $25,000 property tax exemption, as well as another for $25,000 that doesn't apply to school property taxes. The new proposal raises the latter exemption to $150,000 in 2027 and $250,000 by 2028. New residents have a five-year waiting period before receiving the benefit.

If passed, some local governments will have to find other revenue sources or increase the overall tax rate to replace what they've lost, said Tom Kozlik, head of public policy and municipal strategy at HilltopSecurities.

"It's not necessarily going to result in downgrades for sure, but there would have to be some balancing with regard to overall revenues," he said.

Not all municipalities will be hurt equally, which means credit research is important, said Close.

"Like everything in municipal finance, it is really on a case-by-case basis that you could have some credit deterioration from [a] rollback of property taxes," he said.

Other voters on Tuesday will be asked to approve more state or local government debt. That could weigh on a market that has already seen heightened supply.

Some $473.8 billion has already been issued year to date, through September, according to the Securities Industry and Financial Markets Association. That's a 7.8% increase over the same period a year ago. In 2025, total muni issuance reached $587.3 billion, 14.3% higher than 2024, Sifma said.

Cooper Howard, director of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research, believes supply is the biggest issue coming out of the election.

"That amount of issuance has been one of the reasons why you have to see that strong amount of demand continue to keep pace with it for total returns to keep up," Howard said.

Fortunately, new supply doesn't necessarily come quickly into the market. "There's a window over a couple years that they can issue," he noted.

The ultimate makeup of the next Congress could also have consequences for muni investors as lawmakers sort out their priorities. Most notable is what lawmakers will do to tackle the country's affordability problem, Kozlik at Hilltop said.

"There could be some tax policy initiatives and even affordability-related initiatives that manifest themselves in the first quarter of next year," he said. "That could impact housing. It could impact higher education. It could impact public power financing."

Investors considering moves before the election may want to look at those states with tax increases on the ballot, said Close at Nuveen.

For instance, Nuveen recently began offering a separately managed account dedicated to Washington after getting an enormous number of inquiries from financial advisors. The state recently enacted a 9.9% tax on individuals with taxable annual income above $1 million, set to go into effect in 2028. But the issue is now being put to voters, who can decide whether to repeal the new law.

"At the margin, if you do think that some of these measures are going to pass, knowing that bonds usually move up with an increase in taxes, there might be some opportunities to buy ahead of the election for blue states," Close said.

Howard believes munis are attractive right now, particularly for those who are in a tax bracket of 24% or above. Fundamentals are good and the bonds largely have good credit quality, he said. "About 70% of the broad muni index is either triple-A or double-A rated."

Kozlik is also bullish, calling yields "really attractive." He suggests investors concentrate on AA-rated or higher bonds.

"The muni space is part of the portfolio that most investors want," he said. "It helps them sleep well at night."

The Buffalo Bills defensive tackle told reporters that he is grateful for the support of his teammates after the death of his 2-year-old son

Ed Oliver III died on June 28 after drowning in the family's Houston-area swimming pool

Ed Oliver is thanking his Buffalo Bills teammates for their support in the wake of the death of his 2-year-old son.

The defensive tackle returned to action on Sunday, Nov. 1, during the team's 36-10 win over the Baltimore Ravens, after weeks of injuries and four months after the death of his son Ed Oliver III, who drowned in the family's Houston-area swimming pool on June 28.

Now, he's sharing how his teammates have helped him through the past weeks of adversity.

"I think a lot of them don't know it, but man, some days I don't be feeling too high, I come in that D-Line room, and you got guys like Zane Durant that just put a smile on my face," Oliver, 28, told reporters. "As a teammate of his and as someone in this locker room, all we can do is love on him," Allen, 30, said, according to an X post from journalist Andy Young.

"There's nothing you can say to make the situation any better, except being there for him in whatever ways he needs," Allen continued. "And that's what we're going to continue to do. That's what teammates do. We love upon each other and try to hold each other up when we're down."

"I can't imagine what he's going through. But the way he's dealt with it, it's been unbelievable," Allen added.

The Bills' next game is scheduled for Monday, Nov. 9, against the Minnesota Vikings.

#2859. Fantasy Basketball Top 200 Rankings: Reason for concern for Shai Gilgeous-Alexander?

Should fantasy managers who drafted Oklahoma City Thunder guard Shai Gilgeous-Alexander be concerned? While the league's reigning Most Valuable Player is healthy, some of his key teammates couldn't avoid injuries that will sideline them for extended periods. As a result, SGA has been left to shoulder even more responsibility, leading a rotation that has become less experienced than it was on Opening Night.

First, the Thunder lost Cason Wallace (shoulder) and Jalen Williams (ankle/fibula) during the same game, an October 25 win over the Clippers, and they're not guaranteed to play again this season. That game is also the last time Chet Holmgren (knee) played, and he'll be out for at least 8-10 weeks. Lastly, Alex Caruso was sidelined by a sprained ankle suffered during an October 26 win over the Suns and has not played since.

In Oklahoma City's last three games, Gilgeous-Alexander has averaged 24.3 points, 4.3 rebounds, 2.7 assists, 0.7 steals, 0.7 blocks and 1.7 three-pointers per game, shooting 49.1 percent from the field and 85.7 percent from the foul line. Those certainly aren't "bad" numbers, but they aren't on par with what most fantasy managers expect from SGA when selecting within the first three or four picks of a draft.

Can he continue to produce at a level that would align with his preseason ADP? And do the injuries in Oklahoma City open the door for Lakers guard Luka Dončić to crack the expected top three of Gilgeous-Alexander, San Antonio's Victor Wembanyama and Denver's Nikola Jokić (in no particular order)? It will be interesting to see how things play out for SGA, including whether or not the Thunder use some of their future draft capital to improve the roster.

#2858. Sean McVay reveals newest Myles Garrett injury update ahead of Week 9

Help is on the way for the Los Angeles Rams. Head coach Sean McVay announced Monday that star pass rusher Myles Garrett will practice this week, marking a major step forward in his recovery as the Rams prepare for their NFL Week 9 matchup.

The news comes after weeks of uncertainty surrounding Garrett's return. The Rams had previously indicated that Garrett would remain on injured reserve and would not return to practice during Week 8, but the situation has now changed heading into Week 9.

MORE: New Micah Parsons injury update revealed by Matt LaFleur ahead of NFL Week 9

Sep 10, 2026; Melbourne, Victoria, Australia; Los Angeles Rams defensive end Myles Garrett (95) during practice at Marvel Stadium. Mandatory Credit: Kirby Lee-Imagn Images Credit: IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect (Reuters)

McVay confirmed Monday that Garrett will practice this week, giving the Rams their first major indication that the star defender is nearing a return to the field.

Garrett has been sidelined since the Rams' Week 1 game after dealing with a knee injury that ultimately required surgery. He has remained on injured reserve while working his way back.

Now, Garrett is finally set to take the next step in his recovery. His return to practice will open a 21-day window for the Rams to evaluate him before deciding when to activate him to the 53-man roster.

The Rams have underachieved so far this season without Garrett, but his return could give them the shot in the arm they have been looking for.

They play the Commanders in Week 9, who have been getting great play from quarterback Jayden Daniels lately. Although Garrett may not return for that game, the fact that he is even practicing this week means he is finally on the right track.

MORE: Commanders concerned with 'significant injury' on offense following Week 8 win

The news is exciting, but the Rams may not rush him back. Being on the right track is one thing, but being 100% game-ready is another. The Rams want Garrett at his best, so if they have to wait another week, they should.

Still, getting Garrett back on the practice field represents a major development for Los Angeles. The team has been waiting for its star defender to make progress, and McVay's announcement finally provides some clarity on his status.

After missing the first several weeks of the season, Garrett is now moving closer to a potential return. For the Rams, having their star pass rusher back in the fold would provide a major boost as they enter the second half of the NFL season.

#2857. Trump focuses on Reflecting Pool in final public speech before midterms

President Donald Trump began his final scheduled in-person speech before Tuesday's Election Day with a lengthy disquisition about his efforts to renovate the Lincoln Memorial Reflecting Pool and other sites in Washington.

"Today I'm thrilled to announce the official opening of the Lincoln Memorial Reflecting Pool," Trump said Monday afternoon in front of the water feature, which was fenced off for months amid a problem-plagued renovation the president had ordered.

The speech, which lasted nearly an hour, was expected to serve as Trump's "closing argument" on the midterms before millions of voters head to the polls to determine which party will control the House and Senate in the next Congress.

But Trump kicked off his remarks with more than 20 minutes of commentary that focused largely on the Reflecting Pool, along with his administration's work on refurbishing other Washington-area monuments and cracking down on crime in the nation's capital.

"The pool is now better and more beautiful than it's ever been before, and we're very proud of it," Trump said, before repeating unfounded claims that vandals were responsible for damaging the pool and delaying repairs.

U.S. Attorney for the District of Columbia Jeanine Pirro told a court July 31 that the damage was caused by a contractor's botched installation. Trump lashed out at Pirro over the admission and has continued to claim vandals were to blame.

Trump's first explicit reference to the midterms came around the 23-minute mark.

"As you know, this is the last day before the midterm elections, the most important midterm, I believe, in our lifetime," Trump said.

"The Democrat Party has nominated the most extreme and dangerous slate of candidates in American history," he said.

He touted his administration's record and lashed out at some Democratic candidates by name, including Michigan Senate nominee Abdul El-Sayed. He also repeated his controversial claim that if Republicans win, "everybody is going to get $5,000."

"We have to keep our Congress strong, free," Trump said. "America is ready to go. They're ready to vote. I hope they're ready to do the right thing."

But Trump, as he has done in other recent remarks, seemed to acknowledge that Democrats had an advantage — at least when looking to historical trends that show the president's party tends to underperform in midterm elections.

"For some reason, the sitting president doesn't win the midterms. But we gave this a very strong shot," he said.

Later in the speech, Trump continued discussing the pool, as well as his push to erect a massive triumphal arch in Washington and his plans to build a promenade along the Potomac River.

He also said the government planned to build a "National Garden of American Heroes," comprising "250 to 500 great American heroes in statue form." And he talked about ongoing efforts to renovate a public golf course in Washington that is subject to legal challenges.

#2856. 1st-place Rose's rout of Spartans sets up battle for HCAC top spot at Mount St. Joe

After staying unbeaten in the Heartland Collegiate Athletic Conference with a 52-14 home-field rout of Manchester on Saturday, Rose-Hulman's football team faces a decisive duel to keep first place this coming weekend.

Coach Jeff Sokol's Engineers now stand 6-2 overall and 4-0 in the HCAC after the Senior Day victory at Cook Stadium. They'll play 1:30 p.m. this Saturday at second-place Mount St. Joseph in Cincinnati. The Lions are now 7-1 overall and 4-1 in the conference after dominating Anderson 76-0 last Saturday.

In this coming Saturday's clash at Mount St. Joseph, Rose will be trying to get its first win over the Lions since 2021. Last season, Mount St. Joseph rolled to a 48-0 win over the Engineers in Terre Haute. This season, the Lions' lone defeat was a 40-38 setback at Hanover on Oct. 3. On Oct. 24, Rose won 27-20 at Hanover.

In the Manchester win, Rose quarterback junior Brock Zanetell completed 24 of 39 passes for 266 yards and three touchdowns. Alexander Marsteller rushed 18 times for 93 yards and two touchdowns. Joseph Alexion led the receivers with nine catches for 106 receiving yards and two touchdowns.

Alex Shryock and Brandon Chan led the Rose-Hulman defense with four tackles each. William Fleck and Curtic Gault were able to get in the backfield to record one sack each. Andrew Madlem had a forced fumble and fumble recovery, and Antonio Barnes came up with an interception.

Rose got on the board on their first drive of the game, executing a fake punt with Andrew Mrozinske passing across the middle to Hugh Meighan for a 40-yard gain to keep the drive alive. That led to a 35-yard field goal for Cohen Peters to make the score 3-0.

The Engineers extended their lead on the next drive, going 62 yards in nine plays, crossing the end zone on a two-yard rush by Marsteller to make the score 10-0. Marsteller scored again in the first quarter from one yard out to extend the Rose-Hulman lead to 17-0.

Rose-Hulman finished their explosive first quarter with a seven-play, 66-yard drive that ended in a two-yard passing touchdown from Zanetell to Austin Porter to make the score 24-0.

Manchester reached the end zone in the second quarter to avoid the shutout, making the score 24-7.

But the Rose-Hulman offense could not be stopped, adding another score on a 46-yard touchdown pass from Zanetell to Alexion right before the half to make the score 31-7 at the break.

Rose opened the second half with a 13-play, 60-yard drive that ended in a three-yard touchdown reception by Alexion, his second of the day, to extend the lead to 38-7. The Fightin' Engineers secured the big win with two more scores, a 12-yard rushing touchdown by Porter and a six-yard rushing touchdown by Frank Niyonyishu to defeat the Manchester Spartans with a final score of 52-14.

#2855. Palantir reports booming U.S. commercial revenue growth, topping government for first time

Palantir beat Wall Street's third-quarter estimates after the bell on Monday, with U.S. commercial revenue topping government for the first time as businesses adopt more of the company's artificial intelligence tools.

Shares dipped about 3% in extended trading.

Here's how the company did compared to LSEG estimates:

Revenue nearly doubled from a year ago and blew past $2 billion for the first time ever, Palantir said. Net income more than doubled to $1.13 billion, or 44 cents per share, from about $476 million, or 18 cents per share.

"I don't believe anyone's had consistent results like this in the history of software AI, ever," CEO Alex Karp told CNBC's Seema Mody in an interview, adding that the business is succeeding without salespeople.

Palantir has long been known for its strength in the government sector, but its commercial segment caught up in Q3.

U.S. commercial revenue jumped 148% to $984 million, surpassing U.S. government revenue of $953 million, which was up 96%. The company's U.S. commercial revenue has more than quintupled over the last two years.

Palantir lifted its guidance, saying it now expects U.S. commercial revenue "in excess of" $3.56 billion for the full year. Last quarter, the company boosted its forecast to $3.42 billion. The company projects about $2.65 billion in revenue for the fourth quarter and about $8.53 billion for full-year 2026.

Karp has openly supported the use of open-weight models and criticized leading AI labs, which he told CNBC earlier this year are forcing businesses to sell their data sovereignty. Karp, on Monday, also reiterated recent calls to nationalize AI labs, calling it the "only realistic way" to manage liability.

In September, Karp joined major tech CEOs at the White House as calls for regulation mounted following claims from AI researchers that the technology has a more than 10% of killing humanity.

"My message to everybody is: Beware of thinking you can delay the future in AI," he told CNBC. "The future is here. The political ramifications of it are here."

A team goal awarded to Belmont early in the match gave the Bruins a 1-0 victory over the host Indiana State Sycamores at Memorial Stadium in Missouri Valley Conference women's soccer on Sunday.

Miscommunication from the Sycamores led to Belmont's own goal in the eighth minute, the Belmont University athletic website noted.

Indiana State controlled much of the action on Sunday afternoon, outshooting Belmont and earning twice as many corner kicks. After Belmont got its early breakthrough, ISU countered with consistent pressure through the remainder of the first half. The Sycamores nearly found an equalizer before halftime, but Quinn Urquhart's shot on target was turned away in the 42nd minute.

Urquhart produced one of Indiana State's best chances of the afternoon in the 88th minute, placing a shot on target that required another save. Defensively, Sofie Hansen recorded three saves and helped limit Belmont's opportunities throughout the final 82 minutes of play.

#2853. Packers Roster: Green Bay works out 5 on Monday

The Green Bay Packers worked out a group of edge rushers on Monday, an interesting decision considering the team has two available roster spots on its practice squad at the moment. Worth noting: Green Bay has worked out 43 players since the cutdown deadline, and only one of them — an international player from Australia by the name of Cruz Smith — has been an edge rusher up to this point.

Below are the players that the Packers brought in today.

Anthony is an undrafted rookie who spent training camp with the Kansas City Chiefs and was signed to the San Francisco 49ers' practice squad after the cutdown deadline. Anthony was released by the 49ers in late September, and he's had workouts with the New Orleans Saints and New York Jets since then.

Anthony was guaranteed $267,500 by the Chiefs as a UDFA, more than any Packers player in the 2026 class. The Duke product was the 208th overall prospect on the consensus board.

Coleman has been a part of three teams since joining the NFL in 2025, but he hasn't played any regular-season action in the league. He initially signed with the Seattle Seahawks, but was waived at the cutdown deadline of his rookie season.

He later resurfaced with the Indianapolis Colts for a one-week stint on their practice squad before signing a reserve/futures contract with the Miami Dolphins, where he spent training camp in 2026. This appears to be Coleman's first workout since he was let go by the Dolphins.

Johnson was a 2023 third-round pick who was mostly drafted off his athleticism, rather than production at the college level. The Miami-to-Oregon transfer has played 31 games in the NFL, including five starts — all of which came with the Carolina Panthers. In total, he's played 677 defensive snaps to 220 special teams snaps.

After being waived in-season by the Panthers in 2025, he was picked up by the Washington Commanders' practice squad late in the season and then inked a reserve/futures deal with the team going into 2026. He was let go by the Commanders at the cutdown deadline and re-signed to the team's practice squad for about two weeks to start the 2026 season.

This appears to be Johnson's first workout since he was released by the Commanders.

McCullough is another rookie edge defender who went undrafted in 2026, but received sizable guarantees in his deal. McCullough signed for $265,000 guaranteed when he inked a contract with the Denver Broncos, more money guaranteed than any Packers UDFA received this cycle.

McCullough was ultimately let go by the Broncos at the cutdown deadline. He's the son of Deland McCullough, a long-time coach with experience in the NFL and high-end college football. He's also the grandson of Sherman Smith, who was the running backs coach under Pete Carroll, among other stops in the league.

Since being let go by Denver, McClullough has had workouts with the New England Patriots, Las Vegas Raiders, Arizona Cardinals, Cleveland Browns, Carolina Panthers and Indianapolis Colts.

Williford is the one non-edge defender who the Packers brought in today. The super athlete is actually a true off-ball linebacker.

He initially signed with the Baltimore Ravens for just a $15,000 signing bonus and no other guarantees. The Ravens let him go with an injury settlement in August, and this appears to be his first workout since he's returned to full health.

#2852. Ravens Make Surprise QB Roster Move After Lamar Jackson’s Injury

Baltimore Ravens general manager Eric DeCosta announced a big change to the team's offense for the 2026 NFL season. Getty

Many questions linger about the Baltimore Ravens after their 36-10 loss to the Buffalo Bills in Week 8, but the biggest one concerns the quarterback position.

Ravens quarterback Lamar Jackson returned from his ankle injury and threw only one pass before injuring his thumb. After the game, Ravens head coach Jesse Minter announced that Jackson would go on IR and need surgery on his finger.

Backup quarterback Tyler Huntley also got hurt in the first half of the Bills game with a toe injury. Huntley is expected to miss some time as well.

That leaves undrafted rookie Joe Fagnano to most likely be the starting quarterback in the Ravens' Week 9 Thursday Night Football showdown against the Jacksonville Jaguars. The question is who will be Fagnano's backup.

GettyORCHARD PARK, NEW YORK – NOVEMBER 01: Lamar Jackson #8 of the Baltimore Ravens looks on prior to the game against the Buffalo Bills at Highmark Stadium on November 01, 2026 in Orchard Park, New York. (Photo by Bryan M. Bennett/Getty Images)

The Ravens announced they would sign quarterback Austin Reed to the roster. Reed is coming off a stint in the United Football League with the Dallas Renegades and was in training camp with the Ravens afterward.

"Reed spent part of training camp and the preseason with the team, giving him familiarity with the Ravens' playbook and ability to step in if needed.

The Ravens have a quick turnaround with a "Thursday Night Football" game against the Jacksonville Jaguars later this week."

"Reed was one of the Ravens' feel-good stories of the preseason, the father of twin girls who were born prematurely in 2025. While they were fighting for their lives, Reed led the United Football League in passing with the Dallas Renegades, trying to take care of his family with a career playing football.

Reed also has familiarity with Doyle's offense dating back to when he spent the summer of 2025 with the Chicago Bears and Doyle.

Reed will likely back up Joe Fagnano, who stepped in for his NFL regular-season debut after Jackson and Huntley went down."

During the preseason, Reed completed 6 of 12 passes for 59 yards, 1 touchdown, and 1 interception. He also had a rushing touchdown in his first preseason game with Baltimore after spending just 72 hours with the team.

GettyINDIANAPOLIS, INDIANA – FEBRUARY 24: General manager Eric DeCosta of the Baltimore Ravens speaks to the media during the 2026 NFL Scouting Combine at Lucas Oil Stadium on February 24, 2026 in Indianapolis, Indiana. (Photo by Justin Casterline/Getty Images)

Reports indicate the Ravens are looking to add a quarterback through a trade. They have spoken with the Chicago Bears about backup quarterback Tyson Bagent.

Similar to Reed, Bagent was with Doyle last year in Chicago. Bagent knows the offense and has started multiple games.

Baltimore has Reed there as insurance to help with the quarterback position this week. If the Ravens can get Bagent, they will have their starter for the rest of the 2026 season.

#2851. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 stocks for the long haul

The ongoing earnings season, Middle East tensions and buzz ahead of the U.S. midterm elections have been keeping investors busy. Further, worries about elevated artificial intelligence spending are weighing on investor sentiment.

Investors looking beyond the short-term noise and focusing on long-term returns can track top Wall Street analysts to pick the right stocks, backed by strong research.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

This week's first stock pick is Datadog, an AI-powered observability and security platform. The company will announce its results for the third quarter on Nov. 4. Datadog is seeing strong demand for its offerings, thanks to cloud migration and rapid AI adoption. TipRanks' KPIs tool for DDOG shows the growth in its customer base over the past few years amid increasing market penetration and growing adoption of its products.

Heading into Q3 FY26 earnings, Bank of America analyst Koji Ikeda reiterated a buy rating on Datadog stock and raised his price target to $335 from $305. The analyst noted that industry checks and data indicate that infrastructure software demand is strengthening, with AI increasingly driving usage and growth.

Ikeda called Datadog and Atlassian his top picks, saying that companies that can show that AI is a tailwind to growth could see their valuation rerate, even at premium multiples. He added that the total addressable market for observability is growing faster than anticipated, driven by accelerated growth of enterprise AI and the rapid ramp of consumer AI agents.

The five-star analyst believes that focus will be on revenue growth, which he estimates to be 28.7% for Q3 FY26 versus the Street's consensus of 29.1%. Ikeda thinks that the market is underestimating the growth prospects of DDOG's security products. Meanwhile, he cautioned investors about concentration risk related to large customers.

"We think results will be supportive of our long-term thesis that Datadog is a strong share gainer in the rapidly expanding observability opportunity," said Ikeda.

While Nvidia's GTC Berlin offered no major updates, Moore noted the conference reflected that the company continues to add to its significant advantages heading into 2027. The analyst said that Vera Rubin is gaining momentum, though details on next year's Rubin Ultra were lighter than expected.

With demand outpacing supply, Moore said that focus is on closing the gap. In this regard, he highlighted the supply chain deals on substrate, packaging and testing. While the analyst continues to view TSMC 3 nm wafer supply as the biggest challenge, he expects it to ease over time.

Impressed by the results, Needham analyst Ryan Koontz reiterated a buy rating on SPCX stock with a price target of $250. Koontz highlighted that SpaceX's Q3 results significantly exceeded expectations, proving that the company's vertically integrated business model is driving massive scale.

Furthermore, Koontz emphasized the 195.2% sequential growth in SpaceX's AI revenue, fueled by wholesale spot compute deals. He noted management's increasing focus on its AI strategy, given that it is optimistic about adding 10 gigawatts of incremental capacity during 2027.

"With increasing confidence on Starship re-use, we meaningfully increase our estimates," said Koontz.

#2850. Detroit Lions Week 8 snap counts: How the defense adjusted to multiple injuries

In Week 8, the Detroit Lions moved to 5-2 on the season and 2-0 in the NFC North after taking down the NFL's last remaining undefeated team: the division rival Minnesota Vikings.

Let's look at the snap counts from this game and see what they mean for the depth chart hierarchy and what we can ascertain moving forward.

If you just looked at the box score, you may think Goff was not his usual self, but he showed tremendous poise under consistent pressure—the Vikings blitzed nearly 90% of the time—and kept his head when it mattered.

Sione Vaki: 20 (28%) — 16 special teams snaps (70%)

Jacob Saylors: 0 (0%) — 7 (30%) — Injured (neck)

Whew, what a game. The Lions ran the ball 16 times for 66 yards in the first half, with Gibbs accounting for 37 yards on 11 carries (3.4 yards per carry), while Vaki had five runs for 29 yards (5.8 ypc). But the dedication to the run game, coupled with more inside trap plays, opened things up in the second half, as Gibbs took his 12 carries for 116 yards (9.7 ypc) and Vaki delivered 36 yards on six carries (6.0 ypc).

Gibbs will be all over the highlight reels this week—and deservedly so—but it's important not to overlook the "huge step" Vaki took in this game and why he was able to earn "a lot of trust" with this coaching staff. Vaki consistently gained positive yardage on his runs, which allowed the Lions to scale back Gibbs' workload and should give the Lions tremendous confidence moving forward that they have two legitimate options in the backfield.

Despite playing the No. 1 defense in the NFL, it was rinse and repeat for the Lions receivers. Their offensive splits and contributions were close to their season average—TeSlaa's was scaled back a bit due to the Lions' two tight end sets—as St. Brown was the volume option, Williams was the deep threat (he has a 46-yard reception), and all three were smashing people in the run game. St. Brown's arena football formation was technically considered a run because Goff threw the ball slightly backward, which gave the Lions another 10 yards to their rushing totals.

The NFL has struggled with the Vikings' "double mug" blitzing concepts for some time now, but Lions offensive coordinator Drew Petzing unlocked the puzzle by deploying two tight ends in the backfield—LaPorta and Wright—and running an inside trap blocking scheme. This unique wrinkle complicated the Vikings' aggressive approach by countering with their own aggressive downhill blocking and shifting the "gaps" of the Lions' front.

With the Lions finding success in this approach and leaning into it in the second half, Wright saw his highest number of offensive snaps since Week 11 of last season. Additionally, according to The Athletic's Colton Pouncy, the Lions saw a large uptick in 13 personnel (three tight end sets), utilizing the formation 16 times against the Vikings (previously they used it 19 times through the first seven weeks) and had a success rate of 73.7%.

LG Christian Mahogany: 72 (100%) — 4 (17%)

The Lions offensive line had a strong game, showing flashes of dominance in the fourth quarter that should remind fans of the 2023 unit that bullied teams. While Sewell was flagged for four penalties, he was a monster in the rushing attack, and the Lions ran behind him all game. The guards continue to excel and improve, while Miller remains steady, which is all you can ask from a rookie against the Vikings' confusing front.

Mays continued to shine since his return, but an early injury knocked him out of the game, and Scruggs stepped back into a starting center role. While the Lions' run game took off after Scruggs entered the game, I believe that was more due to a second-half shift in blocking philosophy, but that shouldn't discount Scruggs' impact, as it's an incredible luxury to have a player who can seamlessly step in and help the OL dominate.

While the rest of the defense has undergone some change in snap distributions, the interior defensive line has stayed pretty consistent. With the Lions deploying more pass rush, Gill-Howard gets a slight edge in snaps over Williams—and the rookie rewarded the team with a sack—but we could see these two flip snap counts in future weeks.

Rush End Derrick Moore: 22 (42%) — 3 (13%)

Rush End DJ Wonnum: 16 (30%) — 2 (9%) — Injured (concussion)

Rush End Eric O'Neill: 8 (15%) — 10 (43%)

After Wonnum was forced from the game with a concussion, the Lions leaned more on rookie Derrick Moore to fill in at the rush edge. While coaches tend to prefer to lean on their veterans in these situations, they didn't deviate from their player roles and went to Moore, allowing Lacy to stay in the big-end-only role. For the second week in a row, O'Neill is getting sprinkled into obvious pass-rushing situations.

MIKE Jack Campbell: 41 (77%) — 2 (9%) — Injured (neck)

WILL/SAM Trevor Nowaske: 26 (49%) — 19 (83%)

MIKE Jimmy Rolder: 17 (32%) — 13 (57%) — Injured (hamstring)

WILL/MIKE Malcolm Rodriguez: 15 (28%) — 16 (70%)

WILL Amen Ogbongbemiga: 0 (0%) — 16 (70%)

The Lions entered this game with an apparent plan to rotate some of their linebacker roles, with the exception of Jack Campbell. Unfortunately, his scary neck injury forced him out, and with Rolder also exiting with a hamstring injury, the Lions had to shuffle their linebackers even more so.

Overall, the plan appeared to be to scale back Barnes, increase Nowaske, and get Rolder on the field for significant reps, but the injuries altered roles and opened the door for Rodriguez to expand his role. Watching live, it wasn't clear who the primary MIKE linebacker was—the All-22 was not yet available at the time of publication—but it looked like both Barnes and Rodruguez stepped into different spots.

Ogbongbemiga was the only defensive player not to play on defense in this game.

Reed was forced from the game with a hip injury, but he was made available in the locker room following the game, which is typically a good sign his injury won't be a long-term one. Reed has been sensational of late, and in the last three weeks has leveled up his game:

McCreary saw his reps scaled back from previous weeks and continues to rep both inside and out, but it appeared he made room for Ya-Sin to see a large increase in snaps, his largest since being benched after Week 2. Dorsey continues to step up in "and-long" and third-down situations, while Rakestraw was limited to only one snap, which came after Reed was sidelined.

S Brian Branch: 23 (43%) — Injured (concussion)

With Branch exiting early with a concussion, Clark saw his playing time expand and opened the door up for Abney to get back in the mix. Izien was deployed in specific situations vs. the tight ends, but he continues to struggle with his assignments.

Bates missed his first kick of the season in this game, but the Lions were able to roll with it, and it didn't become too much of a factor in the end.

#2849. Owens returns at QB, but Sycamores' MVFC loss streak now five games

Quarterback Elijah Owens returned to the Indiana State lineup for Saturday's football game at Northern Iowa, but the Panthers defeated the Sycamores 45-23 to snap their two-game skid.

The loss leaves ISU with records of 2-7 overall and 0-5 in the Missouri Valley Football Conference going into Saturday's 6 p.m. home game against Southern Illinois. The Salukis will be coming off a bye week. They sport records of 4-5 overall and 2-3 in the conference, and most recently beat Northern Iowa 20-17 on Oct. 24 at Carbondale.

As for the Sycamores' loss to Northern Iowa on Saturday, ISU coach Curt Mallory said the decision to bring Owens back into the fold as starter was made in the middle of the week.

"We were looking at both [Owens and recent starter Brady Allen] because we know we have two really good quarterbacks," Mallory said. "As we got later in the week, we decided to go with Elijah."

He said Owens is a gritty player and fought for everything on the field.

"I thought he made some big plays out there," Mallory said.

Owens responded to the opportunity with his statistically best day as a Sycamore, completing 24 of 47 passes for a career-high 313 yards and two touchdowns, while running the ball 24 times for 122 yards and one TD. Owens was intercepted once.

ISU even outgained Northern Iowa in total yardage 473 to 438. But multiple players had solid yardage days for the Panthers, including quarterback Jaxon Dailey with 12 pass completions for 198 yards and a touchdown, Christian Collins with 107 rushing yards and two TDs and Xavier Terrell with 103 ground yards and two TDs.

UNI's 45-23 victory Saturday handed ISU its fifth consecutive Missouri Valley Football Conference loss. Mallory is now 0-7 against Northern Iowa, and on Saturday the Panthers scored 28 points in the fourth quarter.

Yet Mallory said he thought the effort and the way the team battled was good. However, he acknowledged that big plays hurt the Sycamores.

"[We] left a lot of plays out there, have to do a better job as coaches and we have to execute better when we had the opportunities," Mallory said.

Owens completed a 25-yard pass to B.J. Wuest to get in the red zone with 6:14 to go in the first. Sebastian Lopez ended up knocking through a 22-yard field goal with 5:12 to go in the opening frame to make it 3-0.

The Panthers tied it on the following possession and then took the lead with 8:43 to go in the second quarter with Christian Collins rushing 15 yards into the end zone.

The Sycamores scored, after a review that indicated Keshon Singleton had hung onto a pass from Owens and was able to will his way into the end zone with defenders draped over him. But Lopez shanked the extra point off to the right, which allowed the Panthers to keep a one-point lead. And right before the half ended, the Panthers extended their lead with a touchdown to make it 17-9.

On the final drive of the first half, the Sycamores looked to find the end zone but ended up fumbling on the last play of the half at the Panthers' 2-yard line.

The Panthers extended their lead with 2:24 to go in the third quarter, but Max McCool received a 14-yard pass from Owens to bring the Sycamores within 24-16. But Collins got another touchdown shortly after to make it 31-16.

Again ISU cut the lead to one possession, with Owens diving into the end zone for a 2-yard rush to make it 31-23. But just like they did all game, the Panthers answered the Sycamore touchdown with another one of their own, then scored another touchdown on a fumble return.

#2848. Fantasy Basketball Week 2 High Score Perfect Lineup: Luka Dončić uses another Sunday outburst to top leaderboard

It's officially November and we're through the first two weeks of the 2026-27 NBA regular season. It's been a tough go of it for fantasy basketball managers trying to navigate an unusually high amount of injuries early on. But with that comes plenty of value from players having to step up into bigger roles.

For Yahoo High Score leagues, it's still about the stars (and always a few surprising names) in the perfect lineup week-to-week. Let's go over the top performances from Week 2.

Luka Dončić: Luka did that thing again. The thing where he waits until Sunday to pop off and lift your squad to a victory. Last week, Dončić used an 86-point fantasy performance to lift your team to end of the week. In Week 2, he raised the stakes with 90 points to top all scorers, posting a 45-15-6 line with five swipes and a block in 35 minutes in a win over Toronto. Managers who snagged Luka should be toward the top of the standings all season.

Cooper Flagg: Can you even call a season a breakout when it was this obvious? Flagg was a first-round asset in drafts and is already paying dividends for managers who took on the small, small amount of risk. He's carrying an improved Mavericks team that enters Week 3 4-2 on the season, averaging 26.8 points, 6 rebounds and 6.6 assists while posting elite stocks (3.8 per game). Flagg had his best performance of the young season against the Heat, scoring 29 points with 11 assists, 6 rebounds and 8 stocks. Just wait until the triple-doubles start to come.

Victor Wembanyama: After a slow start by his standards, Wemby is beginning to consistently reach his ceiling for fantasy. He posted back-to-back games with 75+ fantasy points, including a 79-point gem on Halloween. The 43 points was the second highest total of his young career, the Spurs big also added 7 rebounds, 4 assists and 7 stocks in a win over the T-wolves. Imagine if Wemby had gone for a higher rebound total? His ceiling remains astonishing.

Alperen Şengün: This is the beauty of High Score: even when a player misses games, he can still bring you home. The Rockets big spent most of last week on the mend while dealing with a sore back. Fortunately for managers, he started the week with 29-9-9 and 7 stocks for 77 fantasy points in a win over the Hawks. Durability may be an issue but Şengün possesses one of the better ceilings in fantasy basketball.

Giannis Antetokounmpo: The new-look Heat got off to a slow start in the Giannis era with four straight losses. But they bounced back in Week 2 with back-to-back wins to close things out. Antetokounmpo remains the focal point with a usage rate just over 30% of the season. The Greek Freak should contend for runner-up in the scoring race (Luka is running away with the thing right now). Giannis ended Week 2 with a 31-18-10 triple-double to take down the Pacers.

Jeremiah Fears: There's almost always one heavy outlier in this story each week. In Week 2, it's Fears, who went from a High Score of 24 points to 74, which has to be one of the biggest jumps in the history of this game. Shoutout to the Kings, who don't play much defense. Fears put up 22 points, 13 assists, 8 rebounds and 6 stocks in just 29 minutes in the win over Sacramento. Also shoutout Dejounte Murray on the birth of his child, which forced him to miss the game, boosting Fears into a more prominent role. That won't always be the case, but we know what Fears' ceiling is if Pelicans guards miss time.

#2847. The Lakers 'live on a spaceship,' and Luka Dončić looks like the best damn pilot in the galaxy

Perhaps, while watching him flame-broil the Toronto Raptors on Sunday night to the tune of 45 points on 12-for-23 shooting with eight 3-pointers, you found yourself thinking, "Man, Luka Dončić is on an all-time heater to start the season."

Well, if it seems that way, it's because he very, very much is:

"You need three guys to guard him," Lakers big man Sandro Mamukelashvili said, according to ESPN. "I have a feeling that every time Luka plays, it's like 35-plus [points]."

Pretty close, Mamu. It's actually four times in six games this season. (The last four, if you're keeping score.)

Much of the postgame commentary about Dončić's performance in the Los Angeles Lakers' 131-125 win over the Raptors focused on his defensive work: on the 15 defensive rebounds he pulled down, on the five steals he snagged and six deflections he piled up, on the charges he took to draw offensive fouls and get the Lakers another possession. That certainly matters; Dončić is no stranger to catching hell for lacking defensive effort on the sport's grandest stage, and these Lakers will need him to put his best foot forward on that end if they hope to get back to that stage themselves.

"We've been relying on our offense a lot," Lakers center Walker Kessler told reporters after a loss to the Portland Trail Blazers last week. "And we have a very, very good offense and a lot of great scorers. But that's not gonna be the only thing that gets us to win a championship here."

It won't. It'll be the main thing, though. Because what the Lakers did, after ending the somewhat awkward stuck-between-stations stint that stretched from Dončić's arrival at the 2025 trade deadline and the team bidding farewell to LeBron James this summer, was build A Luka Dončić Team. And if there's one thing A Luka Dončić Team is going to do, it's score a truckload of points — with the lion's share of them coming off the fingertips of No. 77.

Sunday's monstrous performance against the Raptors made Dončić just the second player since the NBA began tracking steals in 1973 to score 45 points, collect 15 rebounds, dish 5 assists and grab 5 steals in a single game — joining, as luck would have it, Anthony Davis, the man whom the Lakers traded to Dallas to secure Dončić's services nearly two years ago. It was his 24th career 45-point game, tying George Gervin for 10th on the all-time list, and his 11th career 45-point, 10-rebound, 5-assist outing — more than anybody but Laker legends Wilt Chamberlain and Elgin Baylor.

Six times, Dončić has suited up for the Lakers this season; three times, he's topped 40 points. He is now averaging more than one point per minute — a historical rate of offensive onslaught that nobody has surpassed since Wilt did it 65 years ago.

This isn't normal. Luka just makes it seem that way.

"It feels like a supernova, but it also feels normal," Lakers head coach JJ Redick told reporters after Sunday's win. "We live on a spaceship and we're going at the speed of light at all times when he's on the court."

Dončić has opened the season looking like the best damn pilot in the galaxy, leading the NBA in scoring at 37.7 points per game, 8.1 points more than second-place Tyrese Maxey. That 8.1-point gap between first and second place is roughly the same as the gap between Maxey and Indiana Pacers forward Pascal Siakam, whose 21.4 points per game rank 28th in the NBA.

Dončić leads the NBA in made field goals per game, in 3-pointers made and attempted per game and in free throws made and attempted per game. He's converting on all those looks at or near career-best rates, shooting 78% at the rim, 53% from floater range, 47% on long-2 midrange looks, 90% from the free-throw line, and 43.2% from long distance. He's also tied for ninth in the NBA in assists, dishing 7.3 dimes per contest, making him one of two players in the top 10 in both categories, alongside Jayson Tatum of the Boston Celtics. Combine the points that Dončić is generating by setting up his big men at the rim and shooters on the perimeter with the ones that he's scoring himself, and he's creating 55.3 points of offense for the Lakers per game — most in the NBA, according to Databallr.

Including plays where he shoots or passes to a teammate who shoots, Dončić's pick-and-rolls are producing 1.18 points per play, the third-highest mark among players who've run at least 50 pick-and-rolls, according to Synergy Sports Technology's tracking. His isolations are generating 1.22 points per possession — fifth-best among players who've finished at least 20 one-on-one trips. When he orchestrates from the post, the Lakers have scored 1.36 points per trip — fourth-most among players to log at least 10 such plays.

Add it all up, and the Los Angeles offense has been an absolute buzzsaw with Luka at the controls. The Lakers have scored a scorching 130.7 points per 100 non-garbage-time possessions in Dončić's minutes; as a point of comparison, the league-leading Rockets offense averages 122.1. They're shooting the cover off the ball from everywhere with him on the floor: 80% at the rim, 58% from midrange and 43% from 3-point land.

They've been elite in the half-court, with Dončić poking and prodding and bending coverages until they snap. They've been even better in transition, where Dončić's combination of excellent defensive rebounding for a wing and perpetual eyes-up scanning for hit-ahead opportunities allow L.A. to get out and run into easy buckets:

"When he has it going, it's like, pick your poison, cause he can just make any shot," Redick said. "And then you start putting two on the ball, and everybody else is the beneficiary of that. He's a terrific offensive engine."

That engine is running red-hot to start the season, pacing the Lakers to a 5-2 record behind the NBA's No. 3 offense, with Dončić playing like an MVP frontrunner — and getting the help he needs from the Lakers' other core pieces.

Second banana Austin Reaves is averaging 23 points and five assists per game on 52/40/88 shooting. New center Kessler is holding up his end of the bargain, shooting a league-best 75.4% from the field, averaging 10 rebounds and 3 blocks per game, and holding opponents to a microscopic 41.3% at the rim when he's the closest defender; that's the best mark of any interior deterrent to face at least 25 up-close shots, according to Second Spectrum tracking.

All told, the Lakers have blitzed opponents by 84 points in 126 minutes with Dončić, Reaves and Kessler on the court together — the best point differential of any three-man unit in the league thus far — and their core lineups have largely been lights out, with a number of role players (Mamukelashvili, Ziaire Williams, Matisse Thybulle, et al.) providing the right kind of production and filling in the gaps around that high-priced, high-wattage core.

The spaceship could hit some turbulence, though. The Lakers have played one of the league's easier schedules thus far; three of their five wins have come against the Clippers and Jazz. They're also outperforming their expected field goal percentage to a higher degree than any other team in the league, according to multiple shot-quality metrics.

If that red-hot collective shooting normalizes — and if the ball-security issues that have L.A. ranking 28th in team turnover percentage, with Dončić and Reaves sitting first and second in the league in total cough-ups — then the Lakers could be in for some regression … especially if, y'know, Luka doesn't continue to average like 38 points per game on Prime Steph shooting and Prime Harden usage for a full season.

Of note: Luka's never gotten to the basket less. His 18 drives per game are the third-lowest average since entering the NBA, and a career-low 9% of his shot attempts have come at the rim, according to Cleaning the Glass. He's balanced that out by taking more than half of his attempts from 3-point land for the first time in his career, which is a pretty good idea when you're making them; what happens, though, if those stepbacks aren't cashing in at a 43% clip?

If and when that comes, some of those other shortcomings — the hole at power forward, the lack of another viable ball-handler to help Reaves buoy the offense when Dončić rests, the worrisome lack of rebounding and interior heft when Kessler's off the floor, etc. — could become more glaring. For now, though, the Lakers are off to what's been by and large a glass-half-full start: a top-three offense with a just-below-average defense, outscoring opponents by 7.5 points-per-100 (a 58-win pace), with Dončić looking like the best player on the floor damn near every time he sets foot on it, somehow making the obscene look routine.

It doesn't seem possible that a spaceship like these Lakers can keep going at the speed of light without eventually slowing down. That's the thing about superstars like Dončić, though: When they've got it going, the limits of physics and history and even our imaginations can just seem to fade away.

"All of the great, great players that have ever played have also been in the 1% competitor [level]," Lakers assistant coach Greg St. Jean recently told Thuc Nhi Nguyen of the Los Angeles Times. "They've always been the guy that is able to inspire the players around him and be able to do things that don't even seem like they're possible. And he does that nightly."

#2845. Raptors looking for consistency against Clippers

The Toronto Raptors will continue their Western Conference road trip on Monday night when they take on the Los Angeles Clippers at Intuit Dome.

Toronto enters the matchup looking to put together a more consistent performance after facing the Los Angeles Lakers yesterday.

The Raptors have shown plenty of encouraging signs early in the season, particularly on the offensive end, but will be looking to maintain that level of play against their Western Conference opponent.

Scottie Barnes and RJ Barrett continue to provide the team with scoring and playmaking, while Immanuel Quickley has given Toronto another option to create offence in the backcourt. The offence has clicked early on this season, a very promising sign.

However, Monday's matchup carries an additional storyline following the blockbuster trade between the two organizations in September.

Toronto acquired Kawhi Leonard from Los Angeles in exchange for Brandon Ingram, Gradey Dick and future draft compensation, bringing the former Raptors Finals MVP back to Toronto for a second stint with the franchise.

Unfortunately, neither Leonard nor Ingram will get the opportunity to face their former teams on Monday night. Leonard has been ruled out with a knee injury, while Ingram remains sidelined with a heel injury and a partially torn Achilles tendon.

While the two former All-Stars will be watching from the sidelines, Toronto will still get a look at one familiar face in Gradey Dick, who joined the Clippers as part of the deal.

Dick will have an opportunity to face the organization that drafted him in 2023, adding another interesting storyline to an already intriguing matchup.

(Photo by Sean M. Haffey/Getty Images) | Getty Images

Looking at how these two teams match up, Toronto should have an advantage when it comes to its defensive intensity and ability to create opportunities in transition.

The Raptors have built much of their identity around forcing turnovers, getting out in the open floor and using their athleticism to create easy scoring opportunities. With Barnes leading the way, Toronto has the personnel to put pressure on the Clippers defensively and make things difficult for them in the half court.

However, one area that could give Toronto some trouble is the centre position. While Jakob Poeltl provides the Raptors with an established presence in the starting lineup, he has struggled massively this season. Toronto will need contributions from its other frontcourt options to help manage the minutes behind Poeltl.

The Clippers could look to take advantage of that situation. Isaiah Jackson's athleticism and ability to finish around the rim, combined with Brook Lopez's size and perimeter shooting, give Los Angeles different ways to attack Toronto's interior defence.

Lopez, in particular, could create problems by pulling Toronto's bigs away from the basket, opening up driving lanes for the Clippers' guards. Rebounding and protecting the paint will be important areas for Toronto to address throughout the game.

Los Angeles also has some strengths on the perimeter. Darius Garland gives the Clippers an established playmaker who can create opportunities for himself and his teammates, while the team's perimeter shooting could present challenges for Toronto's defence.

The Raptors will need to communicate defensively, limit open looks from beyond the arc and avoid allowing Garland to dictate the pace.

TORONTO, CANADA – OCTOBER 30: Head coach Darko Rajakovi of the Toronto Raptors celebrates with Collin Murray-Boyles #30 against the Orlando Magic during the second half of a Emirates NBA Cup game at Scotiabank Arena on October 30, 2026 in Toronto, Canada. NOTE TO USER: User expressly acknowledges and agrees that, by downloading and or using this photograph, User is consenting to the terms and conditions of the Getty Images License Agreement. (Photo by Bailey McLean/Getty Images) | Getty Images

Despite those concerns, the Clippers have their own issues to work through. With Ingram, Bradley Beal and other rotation players dealing with injuries, Los Angeles has had to rely on different combinations to generate offence. The team has struggled to find consistency early in the season, and Toronto needs to take advantage of that.

For the Raptors, the biggest priority should be putting together a complete performance on both ends of the floor. They have shown they can score with some of the better teams in the league, but their defensive lapses and inconsistent stretches have made it difficult to string together strong performances. They cannot afford to give the Clippers easy baskets or allow the game to become a back-and-forth shooting contest.

Ultimately, Toronto needs to win this game. The Raptors have been too inconsistent through the opening stretch of the season, and opportunities against short-handed opponents are games they need to take advantage of if they want to establish themselves as a legitimate playoff team.

A road win would provide some much-needed momentum, but another inconsistent performance would only add to the concerns surrounding this group.
