#2842. Tactical Analysis: Real Madrid’s First-Half Domination Ends Racing Santander’s Home Unbeaten Run

Real Madrid are on a six-game winning streak after ending Racing Santander's unbeaten home record. Mourinho kept faith in most of the players who started El Clásico. The tactical ideas introduced since the Leipzig game are being progressively instilled in the team.

Against Racing Santander, these ideas tipped the balance in Real Madrid's favour, allowing them to dominate the first half and take control of the game early.

When José Alberto's side pressed high, Mourinho's team found ways to escape pressure and attack the spaces in behind. At the same time, they worked the ball out progressively, settled in the final third, and relied on wide connections to create chances.

In one of Real Madrid's direct attacks, Tchouaméni dropped between Konaté and Huijsen, giving Real Madrid a numerical advantage in the build-up. Alexander-Arnold's inversion and Diomandé tucking inside forced the wingers to narrow. This opened a passing lane to Güler on the right:

Güler's wide position encouraged the opposing left-back to step up. Arnold then made a diagonal run behind Vicente and past Martin. The open out-to-in passing lane allowed Güler and Arnold to combine with a series of give-and-goes, getting the English right-back in behind Racing Santander's centre-back:

Mbappé attacked the depth, with Diomandé arriving late behind him. Arnold's cross, however, was not accurate enough to reach Mbappé or the supporting runners:

In the move that led to Real Madrid's second goal, Tchouaméni dropped closer to Courtois, prompting Canales to close the out-to-in passing lane. Cucurella pushed slightly higher, dragging Luque with him. This freed Huijsen to advance near the touchline and play a diagonal ball directly to Mbappé.

Mbappé held the ball up well and laid it off to Arda Güler. The Turkish midfielder then played a through ball to Bellingham, who made a late run into the space behind Racing Santander's defence. Bellingham dribbled past the goalkeeper and guided the ball into the net:

Racing Santander tried to force Real Madrid into wide traps, but Los Blancos' setup gave Mourinho's side the tools to escape pressure on the flanks.

In one example, Huijsen operated as a situational left-back while Cucurella stayed high and wide, pinning Luque. This allowed Tchouaméni to dart behind Canales and offer Huijsen a passing option to progress the ball. When Luque left Cucurella to cut off the forward passing lane, it freed the Spanish left-back near the touchline. At the same time, Bellingham moved ahead of his marker as Martin was caught ball-watching.

Real Madrid progressed diagonally to the opposite side. Arda Güler then chose the individual option and shot from close range:

Since the Leipzig game, Real Madrid's attacking patterns have increasingly relied on wide connections in the final third, with the team's distribution across the pitch becoming more balanced.

On the left, Cucurella attacked the channel between the right-back and centre-back as Mbappé moved behind him, while Diomandé held the width. The right side mirrored this structure. In the centre, Bellingham had the freedom to roam. He dropped near Prati, while Bernardo Silva and Arda Güler occupied the opposing right-back and winger in the right half-space. This left Arnold free out wide:

After the switch of play, Arnold laid the ball off to Güler, and Racing Santander's block dropped deeper. Güler was able to deliver an uncontested inswinging cross. Bellingham and Mbappé attacked the blind side of Ramon and Mantilla. The ball reached Bellingham at the edge of the six-yard box, but the goalkeeper saved his header:

In another sequence, Jude Bellingham completed a wide triangle on the left with Mbappé joining the action around the ball:

The movements and spacing among the four players pushed Racing Santander's block deeper. Rotations between Cucurella, Diomandé, and Bellingham manipulated their markers and created space inside for the English midfielder to receive. Similar rotations occurred on the right. Bellingham's switch then found Arnold in space on the opposite flank. As Arnold prepared to cross, Bellingham attacked the blind side of Belocian. This time, Racing Santander's centre-back cleared the danger:

To progress into the final third in another phase, Tchouaméni darted behind Canales to draw him out and open a passing lane between Konaté and Bellingham. Bellingham moved against his marker's momentum to find Bernardo Silva on the right. As the ball advanced, Bernardo Silva and Arda Güler swapped positions, with the Portuguese midfielder attacking the box. Bellingham shifted toward the ball side, taking Bernardo's place in the triangle:

The close proximity of Bellingham, Güler, and Bernardo Silva around the box allowed them to play short passes in tight spaces and work the ball to the overlapping Arnold. Arnold's cutback was blocked by Martin:

Real Madrid dominated every aspect of the first half against Racing Santander, generating 2.71 xG while conceding only 0.26 xGA and controlling 57% of possession. These numbers dropped sharply in the second half: the team produced just 0.64 xG, allowed 0.71 xGA, and saw possession fall to 51%. This shift enabled Racing Santander to gain a foothold in the game.

Dominating and effectively closing out a match in the first half makes things easier for Real Madrid. However, easing off in the second half gives opponents the chance to build momentum and could ultimately cost the team points.

#2841. Luka Doncic is shining on defense, has perfect 2-word reaction to praise

Luka Dončić couldn't help but chuckle after a reporter asked him an unexpected question Sunday evening.

Would you rather win the MVP Award or Defensive Player of the Year?

But this season, the latter has increasingly felt like a misnomer, especially if you watched his brilliant two-way performance against the Raptors in the Lakers' 131-125 win on Sunday.

Dončić finished with 45 points (12-for-23 from the field, 8-for-12 from 3), 15 rebounds, six assists, five steals, one blocked shot and three drawn charges.

Dončić is the first player with five steals and three drawn charges in the same game since player tracking began in 2013-2014, per ESPN.

Said Lakers coach JJ Redick: "He's been awesome on defense."

Added Walker Kessler: "Five steals and three charges. Incredible. You know, incredible. Luka doesn't get enough credit for his defense."

When Doncic was told about the high praise he received, he didn't hesitate in his response.

"Oh, finally," he said, cracking a smile.

Luka Doncic of the Los Angeles Lakers looks to pass against the Toronto Raptors during an NBA game on Sunday, Nov. 1, 2026, in Los Angeles. NBAE via Getty Images

Dončić, who intends on winning over LA basketball fans, has been on an absolute tear this season.

He's leading the league in scoring with 37.7 points per game, the fifth-most points in NBA history through six games and the most since Michael Jordan in 1986-87. He's averaging more points than any Laker player ever through six games, surpassing his record from last season (37).

That's new. That's exciting. That could change the equation.

"Obviously, I'm not the best defender in the NBA, but I think this year I really took a step forward," Doncic said. "Obviously people are going to talk what they want to talk [about] me. But I know, and my team knows, that I'm better this year."

On Sunday, Doncic was sneaking behind players and knocking the ball out of their hands. He was deflecting passes in the lane. He was hammering the ball away from guys as they posted up against him in the paint. He was leaping into the air to deflect errant passes.

Luka Doncic of the Los Angeles Lakers handles the ball against the Toronto Raptors on Sunday, Nov. 1, 2026, at Crypto.com Arena in Los Angeles. NBAE via Getty Images

Defense has long been considered Doncic's weakness. When the Mavericks shockingly traded him to the Lakers in February 2025, his detractors unanimously pointed their finger at his production on the less glamorous side of the court. He's still regularly dragged through the mud for it.

But the thing is, Doncic can play defense. And when he so chooses, it changes the Lakers' ceiling.

Just look at what he did against the Jazz in his 52-point, eight-rebound, 10-assist and two-steal performance in the Lakers' 132-123 win on Oct. 25.

With 3:55 left and the score knotted at 116-116, he took a charge from Keyonte George as the Jazz's point guard drove into the paint. It sparked a 16-7 run for the Lakers.

On Sunday, Doncic repeatedly put himself in harm's way.

"I think taking a charge in general is just a form of leadership, right?" Redick said. "You're putting your body on the line for the team."

Added Doncic: "Before last season, I don't think I had maybe five charges in seven years, or six years. So, that gets everybody fired up. …I just wanted to get better at that and three charges is not bad for one game"

Doncic's buy-in on the defensive end changes things for the Lakers. He should be leading the MVP race right now regardless of what he's doing on defense. But the way he's playing on both ends of the court muzzles his detractors.

Luka Doncic of the Los Angeles Lakers handles the ball against the Toronto Raptors during an NBA game at Crypto.com Arena in Los Angeles, Sunday, Nov. 1, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

Doncic doesn't need to be great on defense. If he can be good, the Lakers can be great. And he can have a gold-paved path to the MVP Award while competing against two-way stars Victor Wembanyama and Shai Gilgeous-Alexander.

We all know what he's doing on offense is jaw-dropping, so much so that Redick waxed poetic while trying to put it into words.

"It feels like a supernova but it also feels normal," he said. "We live on a spaceship and we're going at the speed of light at all times when he's on the court. When he has it going, you know, …it's like pick your poison because he can just make any shot."

Doncic has recently gone from being a punchline to being a threat.

#2838. Cubs’ perfect trade offer for Brewers’ Trevor Megill

Cubs' perfect trade offer for Brewers' Trevor Megill appeared first on ClutchPoints. Add ClutchPoints as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

The Chicago Cubs have an opportunity to attack a late-inning problem while taking a bullpen weapon away from a division rival. Milwaukee is willing to listen on closer Trevor Megill, which makes this the right moment to test the market.

The Brewers will at least listen on offers for Megill. He has one year of arbitration control left before free agency, and Milwaukee typically moves players at that stage if it doubts it can re-sign them.

Mark Hoffman/Milwaukee Journal Sentinel / USA TODAY NETWORK via Imagn Images

Megill was dominant in 2026. He posted a 2.63 ERA, 0.89 WHIP and 30 saves, with 69 strikeouts in 61⅔ innings across 63 appearances. His 1.85 FIP ranked among the best marks for MLB relievers.

Watch sports LIVE with fuboTV (free trial)

He was just as good in October, with a 2.00 ERA, 0.89 WHIP and 11 strikeouts in nine postseason innings. Cubs fans know how costly a shaky ninth inning can be. A reliever with that track record gives the club a clear answer in high-leverage spots.

The contract is also manageable. Megill is projected to earn about $8 million through arbitration in 2027, so he would not strain Chicago's payroll.

Two cautions apply. He turns 33 and has only one year left before free agency, so Chicago would be paying real prospect value for a short window.

Milwaukee also has an internal replacement. Abner Uribe is under team control through 2030, which gives the Brewers leverage to trade Megill and still have a closer.

The Cubs should offer a package built around one polished position prospect and one developmental piece:

Kepley is the centerpiece. The 22-year-old outfielder is the Cubs' No. 5 prospect, stands 5-foot-8 and is projected to reach the majors in 2028. He finished 2026 with a .243/.422/.401 line, 10 home runs, 70 RBIs and 70 stolen bases.

His on-base percentage and speed are the selling points. His 120 walks and 70 steals show a player who creates value without hitting for much average. MLB.com also credits him with a 60-grade hit tool.

I could not find reliable 2026 performance details on Kaleb Wing. Treat him as a secondary piece in this proposal, and check his current status and ranking before publishing.

Milwaukee gets a young outfielder with a strong on-base profile, plus another controllable prospect. For a team trading from a position of bullpen depth, that is a reasonable return.

Kepley is one of Chicago's better prospects, and a reliever with one year of control is a risky use of that kind of asset. Relievers are volatile, and even Megill's strong 2026 does not guarantee a repeat.

The Cubs should therefore treat this package as the ceiling. If Milwaukee asks for a third prospect, Chicago should walk away and look elsewhere for bullpen help.

The deal also depends on how Milwaukee sees its own position. The Brewers just won the World Series and have a built-in replacement in Uribe, so they are not under pressure to sell. They are listening for a good price, not forced to move him.

For Chicago, a Kepley-and-Wing offer makes sense because it adds a proven closer without touching the top of the farm system. If the Brewers agree, the Cubs get a reliable ninth-inning arm, and Milwaukee gets value for a player it may lose after 2027.

#2833. Chiefs Fans ‘Spoiled’ For Calling for Andy Reid’s Job After Second Straight Loss

DENVER, CO - NOVEMBER 1: Head coach Andy Reid of the Kansas City Chiefs looks on before a game against the Denver Broncos at Empower Field at Mile High on November 1, 2026 in Denver, Colorado. (Photo by Justin Edmonds/Getty Images) Getty

The Kansas City Chiefs fanbase have a pretty solid claim to being the most pampered fans over the past decade. Three Lombardi trophies, five Super Bowl appearances and a streak of nine consecutive AFC West division titles that was snapped last season by the Denver Broncos has seen the Chiefs be unequivocally the most decorated franchise in recent years.

And yet, after having lost the past two games – both of which were to two of the four championship finalists from 2025 – some Chiefs fans are calling for Andy Reid's job.

"Relinquish play-calling duties, surrender meaningful control, or face replacement. Mahomes shouldn't have to overcome his own head coach to win." One user wrote on social media.

Other accounts online posted similar anti-Reid rhetoric in the wake of the team's second defeat of the season that leaves them second in the AFC West, standing at 5-2.

In response, other users online have called Chiefs fans "spoiled" for wanting change.

"Chiefs fans calling for Andy Reid to be fired." Randy Scott posted on X. "The man is 154–65, 16-7 in the postseason, 3 SB Championships and 5 appearances in KC. Currently 5-2, lost a road game to rival who just missed the SB last season. Spoiled fanbase. Zoom out and really think about this."

Some pushed back on Scott's comments, such as podcaster Farzin Vousoughian, who claimed that whilst Reid should be fired "yet", the veteran head coach's decision making over the past couple of weeks has "hurt the Chiefs".

GettyDENVER, CO – NOVEMBER 1: Head coach Andy Reid of the Kansas City Chiefs looks on before a game against the Denver Broncos at Empower Field at Mile High on November 1, 2026 in Denver, Colorado. (Photo by Justin Edmonds/Getty Images)

"I'm not calling for Andy to be fired yet. But to push back against fans who are fed up with the lack of consistency with Mahomes as your QB by using his past doesn't refute or debunk what fans are saying." Vousoughian wrote on X. "Reid is a HOFer, but his play calling and decision making recently has hurt the Chiefs."

At 68 years of age, Reid's retirement is certainly on the horizon, depending on how long the former Eagles head coach sees himself going until.

But given his success and the team's continued almost non-stop winning since taking over back in 2013, some things would have to go pretty terribly wrong in order for the Chiefs to ever part ways with Reid.

#2830. Late Closeouts, Empty Glass: Breaking Down the NBA’s Worst Defense

Last year, this defense was the whole identity of the team. We watched a roster nobody believed in become a problem every night because they chased, they swiped, they dove on the floor, and they made you earn every bucket. Four games into this season, that team is gone.

The Suns are 1-3 and have allowed 122 points or more in all four games.

Dillon Brooks stood at the podium and called it what it is: "We're 30th in defense, so that's not great. That's terrible." Then they spent three days drilling it in practice, came home, and let Denver rain threes in their own building.

The fanbase is past the "small sample size" stage. We are watching the same breakdowns repeat.

This stat from Cody Hunt on X/Twitter blew my mind. How much is it bad luck? How much is it real? I believe it's a bit of both, personally. And small sample sizes can blow stats like this out of proportion. But it is clear that this is a massive issue that needs correcting, ASAP.

Rotations to shooters are late, every time.

Murray and Braun combined for 14 threes, and Brooks and others kept rotating or closing out too late on kick-outs to Braun. Ott pointed out that Braun was "a guy that's made five all year." Credit to Braun, but when you leave a shooter wide open, he gets hot.

The switching scheme leaves too few defenders on the back side.

About a dozen times, the aggressive switching and double-teams on Jokic left one help defender covering two Nuggets on the perimeter. That scheme only works with perfect timing, and right now nobody is in sync. And we saw it snowball.

Brooks and Bridges are often stationed in the corner, where they tend to doze off, whether that means losing a backdoor cutter, reacting late, or failing to rebound. Booker's league-worst 137.2 individual defensive rating gets the headlines, but the leaks are coming from every spot on the floor.

Again, the problem I have with all of this is that they had a full three days to talk about this and work on it. And while they looked much sharper offensively (outside of Jalen Green), the defense stuck out like a sore thumb yet again.

This roster was built to be long and nasty, and the length is not showing up where it matters. At 27.7 defensive rebounds per game, only Golden State has been worse at ending opponent possessions. With Mark Williams out and Oso Ighodaro missing a couple of games, the frontcourt has been thin and young, and Khaman Maluach is taking on a big role early.

Ott explained the trade-off himself: the team is "so concerned on the glass" that players retreat to rebound instead of pressuring the ball, and without stops they end up inbounding after made baskets. Last year the athleticism produced steals and transition buckets. This year it is being spent cleaning up misses that should have been contested in the first place.

The defense was the engine for everything else. The Suns ranked third in steals last season, and that pressure is what made the chaos offense work. Without it, this is a half-court team with spacing problems. The front line defense is vital not only for containing penetration that leads to clean looks, but also for stopping guys like Jamal Murray from going supernova and dropping 39 with ease, which I talked about in my latest episode of Suns Hit.

The lineup questions are already here. Jalen Green's defensive rating sits at 130.7, while Collin Gillespie's is 120.3, and Ott went with Gillespie next to Booker down the stretch instead of Green.

They will need the defense to step up against a Warriors team that can shoot the basketball well from deep. They hunt three-point looks. And have some guy named Stephen Curry who is pretty good at shooting the basketball. Golden State drilled 22 threes on 45% shooting in a win over the Clippers on Sunday night, and have won 4 straight games since their loss to the Suns.

That decision (starters) is going to get louder every week the defense stays at the bottom of the league. The lone cure to that is winning, and even that may be a band-aid if they can't figure out how to make this group work long-term.

The talk is already done. The film is there.

Close out on shooters. Box out the man in your area. Defense should be the priority. If the Booker-Green-Brooks-Bridges core can't defend together, Ott has to stop forcing it and play the lineups that get stops.

Last year's top-10 defense came from effort and execution, and right now this team has neither.

#2828. Packers sign QB Cam Miller

ESPN's Jeremy Fowler is reporting that the Green Bay Packers have signed quarterback Cam Miller to their practice squad. The Packers had two openings on their practice squad coming into the week, so the team doesn't need to make any corresponding roster move to execute this deal. With that being said, Green Bay already had one practice squad quarterback in Kedon Slovis, so there's a possibility that his job moving forward is uncertain.

Miller originally worked out for the Packers in early September, but the team continued to roll with Slovis at the time. Only one other quarterback, UFL MVP Jack Plummer, has worked out with Green Bay since the cutdown deadline.

Miller was a sixth-round pick by the Las Vegas Raiders in 2025, and the former North Dakota State quarterback spent most of his rookie season on the practice squad. Late in the year, he was poached by the Miami Dolphins, where he spent training camp in 2026 before being released at the roster cutdown deadline.

Beyond Miller's September workout for the Packers, the Minnesota Vikings also kicked the tires on the quarterback about a month ago.

In two pre-seasons, Miller has gone 36 of 75 passing for 373 yards, one touchdown, one interception and seven sacks.

Here was The Athletic's Dane Brugler's blurb on Miller as a draft prospect, when he was graded as a draftable prospect by the analyst:

A four-year starter at North Dakota State, Miller was the point guard of former offensive coordinator Jake Landry's run-heavy scheme (averaged just 21.9 pass attempts per game). A two-time FCS national champion as a starter (2022 and 2024), he set school records for career passing yards (9,720), completions (759) and total offense (11,997).

Miller is an intelligent game manager who takes what the defense gives him and relies on timing and placement over pure arm power. The son of a football coach, his mental approach and on-field production showed improvements each season, although he will continue to have his doubters because of the level of competition he faced. Overall, Miller won't wow you right away because the physical traits are average. The more you watch, though, the more you come to appreciate his decision-making and feel for the position. He can be an asset in an NFL quarterback room as a reserve.

#2826. 1 trade Steelers must make during 2026 NFL season bye week

1 trade Steelers must make during 2026 NFL season bye week appeared first on ClutchPoints. Add ClutchPoints as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

The Pittsburgh Steelers have a chance to make a serious Super Bowl run during Aaron Rodgers' final NFL season, but general manager Omar Khan cannot afford to ignore the team's biggest weakness. Pittsburgh needs another wide receiver, and its upcoming bye week gives the front office an opportunity to address the position before the NFL trade deadline.

The Steelers have enough talent to compete with the AFC's top teams, but their receiving corps has become a concern. Michael Pittman Jr. and Roman Wilson are on injured reserve, leaving rookie Germie Bernard with a larger role than expected. Bernard has an opportunity to develop into a reliable contributor, but Pittsburgh should not expect him to carry the passing game this early in his career.

DK Metcalf has also struggled to provide the consistency Rodgers needs. His costly dropped pass against the Cleveland Browns, which turned into an interception, was another frustrating moment for a receiver expected to lead the group. Pittsburgh won that game comfortably, but the mistake reinforced why the Steelers cannot afford to settle for their current situation.

Watch sports LIVE with fuboTV (free trial)

Rodgers deserves another weapon, especially with his career approaching its end. The Steelers have leaned heavily on Jaylen Warren and Rico Dowdle to help carry the offense, but they need a more dependable passing attack to compete against the strongest defenses in the postseason.

The good news for Pittsburgh is that there are several wide receivers worth monitoring before the deadline. Josh Downs, Brian Thomas Jr., Jauan Jennings, Dontayvion Wicks, Chris Godwin Jr., Stefon Diggs, and Quentin Johnston are all potential names to consider if their respective teams become willing to negotiate.

Downs would give Rodgers another option who can create separation and provide a reliable target underneath. Thomas offers the possibility of adding another downfield threat, while Jennings could bring a physical presence and experience to the receiving room. Wicks is another intriguing name who could benefit from a change of scenery and provide Pittsburgh with another option in its passing game.

Godwin would give the Steelers an experienced receiver who has already proven he can produce in a high-level offense. Diggs also brings plenty of experience, while Johnston could offer another opportunity to add size and athleticism to the group. These are different types of receivers, but Pittsburgh should explore the market rather than limit itself to one potential target.

Not every name will be available, however. Chargers general manager Joe Hortiz has said he is not listening to trade offers for anyone right now, making Johnston a difficult target to pursue. The New York Jets have also indicated they are not trading Garrett Wilson, so Pittsburgh should not waste time expecting that situation to change.

Khan needs to identify which teams are actually willing to negotiate and determine what it would take to complete a deal. The Steelers should be willing to make a reasonable investment for a receiver who can help them win now.

One name Pittsburgh should avoid pursuing is George Pickens. A reunion might sound appealing to some Steelers fans, especially with the team's current need at wide receiver, but bringing him back does not automatically solve the problem.

Pickens and Rodgers would not necessarily be a good fit together, and Pittsburgh should recognize that some reunions are better left alone. The Steelers have already moved on from Pickens, and Khan should focus on finding a receiver who fits the current offense rather than revisiting a previous relationship.

There are enough other names to explore that Pittsburgh does not need to make a move that could create more questions than answers. The goal should be to give Rodgers another dependable option, not add another potential distraction to an offense that needs consistency.

Pittsburgh cannot control every team's willingness to trade, but it can control how aggressively it searches for help. The bye week gives Khan time to evaluate the available receivers and determine whether a deal can be completed before the deadline.

Rodgers is not going to have any more opportunities to chase another championship, and the Steelers have a roster capable of making a deep postseason run. Letting the receiving corps remain a weakness would waste an opportunity to improve while the team is still in position to compete.

Metcalf can still turn his season around, and Bernard deserves time to develop. However, neither should prevent Pittsburgh from adding another proven playmaker. Pittman and Wilson will eventually provide more depth when they return, but the Steelers need help now.

Khan has a chance to make a move that could change the outlook of Pittsburgh's offense. Whether that means pursuing Downs, Thomas, Jennings, Wicks, Godwin, Diggs, or Johnston, the Steelers need to find a receiver who can help Rodgers make the most of his final NFL season.

#2823. Week in Review: Wembanyama explodes back into form to lead Spurs to undefeated week

Welcome to Week in Review: a Monday feature that looks back at the week that was for the San Antonio Spurs, takes a look at the week ahead, and more. Enjoy!

Week 1: It wasn't the ideal start to the season for the defending Western Conference Finals champions. With Victor Wembanyama not looking like himself (among other issues), they got throttled at home by a fleetingly healthy Thunder squad and lost a heartbreaker in Austin to the Rockets before finally getting their first win of the season against a depleted Mavs team in Dallas.

There may be no better team to "get right" against than the Kings, and the Spurs made quick work of the annual cellar dwellers (which was a good thing considering it was the first night of a back-to-back). Wemby began his bounce-back by dominating the paint on his way to 29 points, and Julian Champagnie tied his franchise record for three-pointers in a half with 8 in the first.

The Spurs looked lethargic the next night against a shorthanded Jazz team, although they had the excuse of a rest disadvantage and not having time to acclimate to the elevation change. Regardless, after enduring another big game from noted Spurs killer Keyonte George, they woke up and dominated in overtime, with Devin Vassell hitting several key shots down the stretch and Wemby dominating his way to 36 points, including finally hitting some threes.

Not to be outdone, Geralt if Rivia Wemby took it another step further on Halloween night with 43 points and 6 blocks against a Timberwolves squad that was missing Anthony Edwards and Rudy Gobert (not that the latter has proven to a Wemby stopper). As with all four wins so far, the Spurs left a little something to be desired on the consistency front and weren't as dominant has they could have been considering the opponents/injuries, but you accept the baby steps (for now).

John Schuhmann, NBA.com — 4 (last week: 6)

OffRtg: 115.2 (9) DefRtg: 108.5 (8) NetRtg: +6.7 (8)

The Spurs lost their first two games of the season, but have since won four straight. They've scored 119.2 points per 100 possessions over the streak, with Victor Wembanyama totaling 79 points over the last two games.

1. Not surprisingly, Stephon Castle has had the biggest offensive adjustment with Dylan Harper moving into the starting lineup this season. Castle has gone from a team-high 5.7 minutes per game of possession (19% of his minutes on the floor) last season to 3.9 per game (third on the team and 13% of his minutes) this season. His efficiency is down and he had his first scoreless game in almost two years on Saturday.

2. But Castle's overall scoring and usage rate isn't down significantly. He's just playing more off the ball and averaging 6.3 catch-and-shoot 3-point attempts per game, up from just 2.9 per game last season. The Spurs are in the top six in both ball and player movement and lead the league by a wide margin in the percentage of their 3-point attempts (84%) that have been off the catch.

Coming up: The winning streak includes wins over the Jazz, Mavs and Kings, who the Spurs will face again in the next five days. The Mavs were without both Kyrie Irving and Cooper Flagg when the Spurs won in Dallas in Week 1.

Law Murray, The Athletic — 4 (last week: 12)

The Spurs handled their business in three spots where they should have handled their business: a woeful Kings team, a short-handed Utah team and a short-handed Timberwolves team traveling on zero days' rest. Still, San Antonio got impressive performances from Victor Wembanyama, culminating in a career-high 34 field goal attempts against the Timberwolves. The Spurs will look much deeper whenever Tobias Harris can get back.

Kurt Helin, NBC Sports — 7 (last week: 8)

San Antonio has found its footing somewhat after a slow start, and now boasts a top-10 offense and defense. And it has done that without the Spurs big off-season acquisition, Tobias Harris, who is nearing a return but there is no official timetable. The Spurs have stretches where they look like the best team in the league: Defending with ferocity, moving the ball, and getting easy buckets around the rim. Then the next quarter they fall in love with isolation basketball and things go cold, which happened too much against Minnesota on Halloween, but they got bailed out by 43 points from Victor "The Witcher" Wembanyama.

Brett Siegel, Clutch Points — 4 (last week: 9)

Victor Wembanyama was not about to let the San Antonio Spurs be below .500 for too long. Following a 0-2 start to the season, the Spurs have rattled off four straight wins and look like the team they were several months ago en route to the 2025 NBA Finals.

Over their last four games, the Spurs have averaged 122.5 points per game, led by Wembanyama, who has averaged 32.8 points, 9.5 rebounds, and 4.3 blocks per game during this win streak. Wemby says he is aiming for the MVP award, and these are definitely MVP-like numbers.

One of the more underappreciated players on this roster has been Julian Champagnie, who has shot 16-of-31, or 51.6 percent, from 3-point range in this span. He is shooting 47.6 percent from 3-point range on the season and proving to be one of the most impactful forwards off the bench in the entire NBA.

Fri. 11/6 at Sacramento Kings (Emirates Cup, 9:00 PM CT)

Sat. 11/7 at Portland Trail Blazers (9:00 PM CT)

The Mavs with Cooper Flagg and noted Spurs killer Kyrie Irving, who both missed the first match-up, are a different animal, and strange things always happen in Portland, especially on the second night of a back-to-back. I believe the Spurs will ultimately split those games, but I'm more than ready to be proven wrong.

Not really Spurs related, but there has been so much drama in the NBA in the first two weeks alone — from injuries, to current and former teammate arguments, and scuffles — that it makes you appreciate the drama-free Spurs (outside of the first two games, of course). #2822. Monday Musings: Revisiting an old mantra; Notre Dame has an obvious issue

Happy Monday, errrrrrrrrrrrrrrrrrrrrbody. Hope your weeks have gotten off to a great start.

It's another victory Monday. It's a victory Monday after playing North Carolina. IT'S A VICTORY MONDAY AFTER PLAYING NORTH CAROLINA IN CHAPEL HILL! IT'S A VICTORY MONDAY AFTER A BILL BELICHICK-LED TEAM SCORED 35 POINTS (an actual first in the history of football)!!! What a victory Monday indeed.

So, without further added hype, let's get to it.

1) I wrote about winning a game after a struggle about 11 years ago. I do wonder if it's applicable here.

The Miami Hurricanes led Nebraska 33-10 in the fourth quarter at Hard Rock Stadium in 2015. Nebraska stormed back to tie the game with a last-minute touchdown and two-point conversion. A Corn Elder pick and a walk off field goal delivered victory. I wrote about the game, saying there's a W in "wrangle". As in there was a fight but it ended with a win. This is from that piece:

But here's the thing – it didn't. Miami won. Perhaps my disgust has subsided a great deal over the past week and a half….but the fact still remains. They…..won.

And no one has ever complained about an undefeated team. Right now, that's what Miami is. Their ability to play down to the level of their competition (or below it) has been maddening during Golden's tenure (and Shannon's, and the end of Coker's, etc). I get that. And now, fans will be waiting for the other shoe to drop, and I can't say that's not an unreasonable way to feel by any means.

But, the Canes sit here undefeated. And that's what Miami will try to maintain on national television against a team that just piled on the points and yards in alarming quantity with a backup freshman quarterback last week. With Gunner Kiel out, the Bearcats will be starting said freshman, Hayden Moore, against the Canes, who helped Cincinnati shatter six school records last week against Memphis.

Well, the bottom fell out on that 2015 team, and the rest was history, as we all know. Ugh, that Cincinnati game.

2) But I write that as a setup to wonder if the struggles we're seeing in pass defense is a sign of bad things to come. Is it fair to consider again the "W in wrangle" mantra in a win like this and look at it in a positive light? Yeah….I still think it is.

It's of course hard to win in conference on the road, and generally it's even harder to win in the NIL/transfer portal era. The parity is amazing. The talent level has never been more evenly distributed. Notre Dame was pushed in Chapel Hill as well.

And this Miami team's base talent level is worlds better than what we saw 11 years ago, obviously. It's reach-the-national-championship-game-level base talent. That's the kind of talent you can feel good about stepping up the next week and playing better, not that the talent itself is not good enough as we saw in 2015 and a win there can be a bit of a mirage.

That's at least how I choose to look at things after a few rough games on the back end of the defense.

3) Now, I will say, Corey Hetherman has to get back into the lab and get to work on how to mix things up, both in pass rush scheme and in coverage.

After the game, Bill Belichick was asked about Miami's scheme on the play where Billy Edwards, Jr. was sacked and stripped of the ball. His response was…interesting.

"They didn't mix it up," Belichick said. "They did what they did the whole game. They ran what they ran the whole game, which is honestly what they've been running the whole year."

Well, that's possibly not great. And when the pass rush isn't getting home as it has not been lately, that seems kind of relevant. Need a hard look and reset going into South Bend, in pass rush game plan and on the back end.

4) And as for the penalties on the UNC onside kick attempts, THERE. WAS. NO. CONTROVERSY. THERE.

And yeah, I've read A. LOT. of comments saying those flags should not have been thrown. Buddy, they were a yard offside, multiple players. And on the second attempt, the UNC player clearly blocked in the 10-yard area by a full yard.

But yeah, push stupid narratives. Put those in one hand and poop in the other and see which one fills up more quickly. It was a clean win free of actual controversy to anyone but FSU fans and other ardent haters.

5) Miami isn't the only team with some injury issues going into this top 5 tilt with Notre Dame.

The Irish are banged up coming into this one, with the Irish seeing a trio of important defenders going down against Navy. Here's the injury update to start the week.

Defensive captain Drayk Bowen is out, along with starting safety Brauntae Johnson and CB DJ McKinney. Both defensive tackles are banged up in the middle and questionable for the game. Not the best news for a defense that will have their hands full trying to slow down the best offense in all of college football.

Will hold off with a prediction for now, but lots more to come this week here at State of the U. Go Canes!

#2821. As Giants pursue Jonathan Taylor, could they pivot to Derrick Henry?

The Giants (and other teams) hope to land Colts running back Jonathan Taylor via trade. With Baltimore’s season potentially falling apart following Sunday injuries to starting quarterback Lamar Jackson (thumb) and his backup Tyler Huntley (toe), there could be another running back to potentially be pursued at or before the November 10 deadline, by the Giants or other teams.

Derrick Henry, now 32, is far closer to the end of his career than the beginning. His contract pays out $12 million in 2027, with none of it guaranteed.

Now could be the time for the Ravens to turn the remainder of his contract into a draft pick or two.

And the Giants would make plenty of sense. Beyond the fact that the Giants view Taylor as on the second tier of current NFL tailbacks with Henry, Giants coach John Harbaugh has experience with Henry.

After a Week 5 loss to the Saints, Harbaugh explained the distribution of carries to a wide swath of tailbacks by pointing out that they don’t have a clear-cut workhorse.

“I would love for us to be in that kind of a circumstance,” Harbaugh said at the time. “A Derrick Henry circumstance or a Saquon Barkley circumstance or whatever it might be.”

The best way to be in a “Derrick Henry circumstance” would be to, you know, get Derrick Henry.

Whether the Ravens would trade Henry depends on whether they plan to keep him beyond this season. If they’d cut him before a $1 million roster bonus comes due in March, they wouldn’t get a compensatory draft pick. The only way to get value for his contract would be to trade it.

Through eight games, Henry leads the league with 158 carries. He has 632 yards (4.0 per carry) and eight touchdowns.

Given the quarterback injuries in Baltimore, the Ravens surely will get a call or two about Henry. The Giants, given their interest in Taylor, could be one of them.

#2817. ‘Right now a top-10 player’: This NBA insider says it with his chest about Cooper Flagg’s arrival

If you've been locked in for the Dallas Mavericks' first six games of the 2026-27 season, you already know. The proof is in the dominance-flavored pudding.

And ESPN reporter Tim MacMahon has been. So he knows.

That's why MacMahon had no reservations when he declared flatly on Monday's episode of the Hoop Collective podcast that Cooper Flagg has already arrived. He's not going to be one of the best in the league. He is one of the best in the league. Right now. Today.

This is no longer something opposing teams can put off until some future point in Flagg's development. He will wreck your shit tomorrow. The Houston Rockets found out about it in Friday's first and fourth quarter s of the Mavericks' 116-111 win at the AAC to improve their record to 4-2.

"This is going to get aggregated, and I'm okay with it," MacMahon said while discussing the Mavericks with podcast host Brian Windhorst, who himself banged a loud drum for Flagg before the start of the season. "I would not trade Cooper Flagg for Luka [Doncic], just considering age, contract, all that kind of stuff. Cooper Flagg I believe right now is a top-10 player."

"I think he's a top-10 player. … There's only one guy in the league I'd trade him for."

I don't know why there's a need to commingle this discussion with who one would or wouldn't trade for Flagg, because the Mavericks ain't trading Flagg, just as much as the San Antonio Spurs ain't trading Victor Wembanyama any time soon. Irrelevant.

What's relevant is that Flagg's development has curved toward the exponential in the tail end of what are still his teen years, which should be a horrifying proposition for the rest of the league, and the Mavericks' roster is showing signs that the same may be true of the team surrounding the young superstar.

Flagg and Kyrie Irving went off for 35 and 33, respectively, in Friday's upset of the Rockets, who were admittedly playing without Alperen Sengun. MacMahon also stressed on the podcast that it is becoming less and less likely that the team trades Irving this season as the team's fortunes seem to be turning this year.

"Cooper being this good at bringing the ball up and initiating the offense takes a lot of pressure off him and takes a lot of strain off him. Kyrie, I think, is very, very happy and comfortable with his situation in Dallas," MacMahon said. "My educated guess is that this is probably a situation that plays out this summer, but we'll see."

Irving has a player option for 2027-28 at just north of $42 million before he becomes an unrestricted free agent after next season.

Irving and Flagg work well on the court for the same reason that Irving and Doncic worked, and for the same reason that Irving and LeBron James worked during the Cleveland Cavaliers' championship season in 2015-16. Having a running partner who can also bring the ball up and initiate offense gives Irving the freedom to play both on and off the ball, maximizing his ability to play-make and get into advantageous matchups for isos.

Flagg comes into Monday's game against the Sacramento Kings averaging 26.8 points per game, which is seventh in the NBA, through six games. He's also fifth in steals (2.6 per game) and 21st in blocked shots (1.2), making him perhaps the most dominant force on both sides of the ball this side of Wembanyama.

Campbell was taken to the hospital following the fourth-quarter collision that left him motionless. He was immobilized and transported off the field.

At the hospital, Campbell has been treated for his neck injury. The team has provided few specifics at this point. Campbell remained hospitalized overnight.

We wish him the best as the recovery process continues, and we will provide updates as they emerge.

#2812. Lakers coach makes stern demand of players or else: ‘I’ll take these guys out’

JJ Redick is seeing the Lakers create the kind of offensive advantages that come with having Luka Dončić on the team.

He's also tired of those opportunities not being taken care of, making it clear there are consequences for players who don't let it fly.

Luka Doncic is creating the offensive advanatges the Lakers want. NBAE via Getty Images

Dončić has reached another level of offensive rhythm to start the season, averaging a league-best 37.7 points on 48.2% shooting (43.2% on 3-pointers) to go with 8.2 rebounds and 7.3 assists in his first six games after the Lakers' win over the Raptors on Sunday.

With the roll he's been on, defenses have been aggressive with blitzing, trapping and generally sending two defenders at the Slovenian guard when they can – creating 4-on-3 offensive opportunities.

While Dončić is doing "a really good job of making plays" when two defenders are on, as Redick put it, the team results haven't been there.

Los Angeles Lakers head coach JJ Redick is tired of his team not take advantage of the easier offensive opportunities Luka Dončić creates, NBAE via Getty Images

And Redick sees that as an issue with the decision-making after the superstar guard passes out of the double team, mentioning there were multiple opportunities during Sunday's win over the Raptors in which the Lakers passed up good shots.

"I know in the second half I can think of four times that we turned down wide open threes, like I'll take these guys out," Redick said. "Like you gotta shoot the ball when you're open."

While Redick didn't call out any player by name, he sees the indecision as a result of having so many new players not accustomed to what the Lakers want to do when those advantages are created.

While JJ Redick didn't call out specific players, Quentin Grimes has been one of the biggest culprits of passing up open shots. NBAE via Getty Images

"And again it's the new group thing," Redick said. "These guys gotta understand what the purpose is of what we're trying to do. And when you get an advantage, you gotta keep the advantage and shoot it."

There's also the factor of players not wanting to make mistakes early on with their new team, leading to overthinking.

"Because I lived this as a player, it's like, 'Ahhh, I want to shoot well. I want the Lakers fans to like me. I want to score. I want to do this,'" Redick said. "You're thinking too much. Just go play basketball and do our stuff and I promise you there's going to be opportunities to score."

#2808. Thunder announce they'll retire Russell Westbrook's No. 0 jersey in February

Nobody will wear No. 0 for the Oklahoma City Thunder again.

The Thunder announced Monday they'll retire Russell Westbrook's jersey on Feb. 7 during a home game against the New York Knicks.

General manager Sam Presti made the announcement at Oklahoma City's Day One Luncheon on Monday with Westbrook on stage. Westbrook wiped back tears when the announcement was made.

The Thunder then released a video montage celebrating Westbrook's moments with the franchise.

"Many players make an impact but only great players leave a legacy," Presti said, via a team statement. "Russell Westbrook's legacy was built both on the court and in the community."

Westbrook was a member of the Thunder from the beginning. He was drafted by the Seattle SuperSonics with the No. 4 pick in 2008, weeks before the franchise officially moved to Oklahoma City and became the Thunder.

Not including previously retired Sonics jerseys, Nick Collison's No. 4 is the only other jersey the Thunder have retired.

Westbrook played 11 of his his 18 NBA seasons with the Thunder from 2008-19, including his 2016-17 NBA MVP campaign. He made eight of his nine All-Star teams and earned eight of his nine All-NBA selections with Oklahoma City.

Westbrook is the NBA's all-time triple-double king. He became the first player since Oscar Robertson to average a triple-double during his MVP season and did so three more times throughout his career. His 209 triple-doubles are the most all time.

Westbrook retired from the NBA in August having also played for the Wizards, Lakers, Clippers, Nuggets and Kings. But he's most closely associated with elevating a Thunder franchise to championship contention alongside Kevin Durant, James Harden and Serge Ibaka.

From that core, he remains the most beloved in the Oklahoma City community. Now his jersey will have a permanent place in the Thunder's rafters.

#2805. What waiving Elliott Davison means for the Patriots

One day after their win over the Miami Dolphins, the New England Patriots decided to part ways with a member of their safety group. Elliott Davison, who had originally joined the 53-man roster from the practice squad in mid-October, was waived.

Let's assess what the move means for the Patriots from a big-picture perspective.

When the Patriots announced their inactives on Sunday, the six-players list included four healthy scratches. Davison was one of them, reflecting his position near the bottom of the roster and the volatile status of his spot on the 53-man team.

From that perspective alone, him getting the axe on Monday was no particular surprise — and that's before putting injured reserve into consideration as well. After all, wide receiver Mack Hollins and fullback Reggie Gilliam are both eligible to return to practice and subsequently off IR this week, while offensive tackle Marcus Bryant's 21-day window for activation is set to close on Wednesday.

New England had no openings on its roster coming out of Week 8. It now has one, and it is likely to be used sooner rather than later.

The Patriots have had to deal with their fair share of depth questions across the roster this season, but the safety spot has been in relatively good shape. Led by team captain Kevin Byard and sophomore starter Craig Woodson, the group has enjoyed a level of stability others on the team can only dream of.

Removing Davison from the equation does little to change that, given that he was the sixth option and played only 10 defensive snaps — all late during a blowout win over the Raiders in Week 5.

53-man roster (5): Kevin Byard III (31), Craig Woodson (4), Jaylen Reed (23), Brenden Schooler (41), Mike Brown (33)

The big question mark among the group is Dell Pettus, who remains on injured reserve despite being eligible to come back since Week 7. Should he be cleared for a return, he and Mike Brown might end up competing for one spot on the team as a backup/third safety and personal punt protector.

Before being made inactive for the Dolphins game on Sunday, Davison saw most of his action on special teams. He played 57 combined snaps during his three active games, a share of 67% over that span.

He lined up on kickoff coverage, kickoff return, punt return and field goal/extra point block — roles that were already redistributed versus Miami. From that point of view, his removal from the 53-man roster will not have a notable impact: he already had been replaced in that regard.

An undrafted rookie in 2025, Davison will have to pass through the waiver wire before entering the open market. There is a theoretical chance that a team ends up putting in a claim on the 24-year-old to bring him aboard, but the expected outcome is that he won't be picked up that way.

If so, the Patriots re-signing him to their practice squad seems like a realistic option. Davison already spent most of the early parts of the regular season on New England's "second roster."

If such a move were to happen, however, a follow-up would be necessary. The addition of running back Terrell Jennings on Monday means that the club currently has a full 16 players — not counting offensive tackle Lorenz Metz, who is exempt — signed to the practice squad.

#2802. Grizzlies tore down their core. So how are they the NBA's last unbeaten team?

There's only one undefeated team left in the NBA, and few would have predicted it before the 2026-27 season began.

The Memphis Grizzlies are raising eyebrows across the league with a perfect 6-0 record after their front office spent the past 18 months recalibrating the team's roster by trading away Ja Morant, Jaren Jackson Jr. and Desmond Bane. One more victory on Monday, Nov. 2, against the New Orleans Pelicans would set a record for the best start in franchise history.

A Grizzlies' nucleus featuring 2026 first-round pick Cameron Boozer, 2025 first-round pick Cedric Coward, 2024 first-round pick Zach Edey and guard Ty Jerome appears capable of exceeding expectations after they were the only team in the league to not have a player included in USA TODAY's top 100 player rankings prior to the season. Six games later, they could be the Western Conference playoff contender nobody expected.

During the past 20 seasons, the last remaining undefeated team in the NBA has failed to qualify for the playoffs just three times. No team since the 1998-99 Seattle Supersonics has started a season with at least six wins in a row and not made it to the NBA's postseason.

So how have the Grizzlies pulled off this early season surprise? Here are three reasons why Memphis is the last remaining undefeated team in the NBA before games played on Monday, Nov. 2:

NBA: Spike Lee hopes Knicks skip Trump White House visit despite Dolan's wishes

Yaxel Lendeborg is suddenly the trendy pick to be the NBA's rookie of the year thanks to a batch of big early season performances, but Grizzlies forward Cameron Boozer is the only rookie with a game-winner and a triple-double so far. The No. 3 overall pick in the 2026 NBA Draft has quickly become a hub for the Memphis offense, distributing the ball on offense and forming a fearsome pairing inside with Edey.

Though his overall shooting numbers are lagging and he won't wow you with athletic feats, Boozer is still shooting nearly 37% from 3-point range and looks to be a potential cornerstone in the mold of Tim Duncan with his diverse skill set. Perhaps more importantly, his promising production in the first six games have helped shift the narrative after an offseason of uncertainty about the changing roster.

The Grizzlies might not have a star with the commercial crossover potential Morant once had, but the roster was in much better shape than many NBA prognosticators expected after the team traded away Morant, Jackson, Bane and Santi Aldama in various deals. Memphis currently has 13 players averaging at least 12 minutes per game as coach Tuomas Iisalo often uses an 11-man rotation.

Jerome, who missed most of last season after being signed by the Grizzlies, was the league's leading scorer during the first week of the season. Coward and Jaylen Wells have shown growth as 3-point shooters and Edey, despite being on a minutes restriction, has been a dominating rebounder and defensive anchor based on advanced metrics. Jerami Grant and Isaiah Stewart, acquired in trades this past offseason, have provided a veteran presence off a bench that seems to come in waves.

That approach might not work in the postseason, and it caused issues when Morant clashed with Iisalo last year. But it's proving effective during the first month of an NBA season that's been defined by the number of injuries impact players have already suffered.

Memphis has shown undeniable signs that it was overlooked heading into the 2026-27 season and swept a four-game road trip as part of this hot start. But it also hasn't beaten a team expected to contend for either conference title.

The Grizzlies have beaten the NBA's only winless team twice (Sacramento Kings) and have two wins over a team with a 1-5 record heading into Monday (Utah Jazz). They'll be favored against the struggling Pelicans.

But Memphis also orchestrated a comeback over the Golden State Warriors and handed the Portland Trail Blazers their first loss of the season. Morant played against his former team for the first time in Portland and left with a hamstring injury that will sideline him for at least a month.

Six games don't validate a rebuild, and the schedule toughens in the coming weeks. But as Morant faces another extended absence and the Grizzlies keep piling up wins, the franchise's decision to dismantle the roster built around him looks less like a gamble and more like foresight.

This article originally appeared on USA TODAY: Grizzlies tore down their core. So how are they the NBA's last unbeaten team?

Expectations are even higher in Chicago after the Bears' blockbuster trade for All-Pro defensive tackle Jeffery Simmons. Unfortunately for the Bears, they will have to topple the reigning champs without one of their best offensive weapons.

Second-year running back Kyle Monangai, who is still recovering from his respective turf toe and thumb injuries suffered in early October, will miss his fourth straight game against the Seahawks. With a Week 10 bye just around the corner, the Bears are electing to give the powerful running back as much time to heal as possible, rather than push him to possibly return too early and exacerbate his injuries.

But what does that mean exactly for Monangai's return timeline for the Bears? Honestly, it seems pretty straightforward.

With the tailback missing Monday night, I'd guess he will also miss the Bears' home Sunday night game against the Tampa Bay Buccaneers next weekend. The Bears will then have their bye and come back for a home game against the New Orleans Saints on Sunday, November 22nd. That is most likely when we will see Monangai return to the Bears' lineup, with the running back having enjoyed roughly seven weeks of rest by that point.

It stinks for the Bears that Monangai, one of their most dependable offensive players, will likely miss approximately 30 percent of the regular season. But the Bears are in the business of competing for a Lombardi Trophy now. That means playing the long game to ensure that Monangai is as healthy as possible for the stretch run and the postseason at all costs.

Technically speaking, Monangai is the Bears' backup running back to regular starter D'Andre Swift. The Bears like to use a platoon system when they have both talented runners available. Monangai's absence lately has meant that Swift has taken greater responsibility in Chicago's backfield, with veteran Roschon Johnson occasionally filling in.

#2790. Stephen A. Smith happy Michael Wilbon is prioritizing his health: ‘This is good news’

While Michael Wilbon's indefinite leave of absence from ESPN is sad news for many, Stephen A. Smith insists it is a good thing.

Less than two weeks after celebrating the 25th anniversary of Pardon The Interruption, Michael Wilbon announced he was stepping away from his ESPN responsibilities to address medical issues. Wilbon insisted this wasn't a retirement announcement, saying his goal was to get back on-air, but the longtime journalist and sports host called the absence an "indefinite leave."

"This isn't retirement, but it is a trip to the injured list. Hopefully not an extended one," Wilbon wrote. "Struggles with multiple health issues in recent months lead me to take an indefinite leave of absence from my PTI and NBA duties at ESPN and ABC."

Wilbon cited multiple health issues in recent months, adding that others have noticed "worrisome signs in recent years" that he hesitated to address. And Stephen A. Smith is happy to see Wilbon finally prioritizing those issues.

"Obviously I know a thing or two about this subject matter, that's his business to tell," Smith said during his Monday afternoon Mad Dog Sports Radio Show on SiriusXM. "I will tell you, as much as I love seeing him on the airwaves, as much as I'm gonna miss seeing him on the airwaves – hopefully for a short time – I'm happy he's doing this. This is good news to me. Because that means the health concerns that he's had are gonna take priority with him addressing it.

"Because he himself is a workaholic. Always gets on me for being a workaholic; he's a workaholic too. And this industry, and this world, is a better place with Mike Wilbon in it. He's not only one of the greatest talents that I've ever seen in this industry as both a writer and a broadcaster, he's an even a better person. He's one of the greatest human beings I have ever known in my life."

Smith added that he knew this leave of absence was coming for Wilbon. "But I'm happy it's here," he continued. "Because we need him around for a very, very long time."

The 67-year-old Wilbon suffered a minor heart attack in 2008 and was later diagnosed with Type 2 diabetes. The heart attack occurred early Monday morning on Jan. 28, 2008. After taking a few days to recover, Wilbon wrote his first column for The Washington Post on Jan. 31, and he sought to rejoin PTI on Friday, but Tony Kornheiser, Dan Le Batard and producer Erik Rydholm convinced him to wait until the following Monday.

It sounds like this break might be longer than his shockingly brief leave of absence following his heart attack. Wilbon joined The Tony Kornheiser Show Monday to explain he already missed a full week of PTI because he was "beyond exhausted, lifeless," noting this has been going on for a while.

Hopefully Wilbon can return to PTI and his other ESPN responsibilities soon. More importantly, we hope Wilbon returns in better health.

#2787. Oklahoma City to retire Russell Westbrook's jersey in Feb. 7 ceremony

James Harden demanded a max contract and wouldn't budge, so Oklahoma City traded him to Houston. Kevin Durant left to form a super team in Golden State.

Russell Westbrook stayed, collected triple-doubles at a historic rate, won an MVP, and forever became a hero to fans in Oklahoma City. Fittingly, the Thunder are going to retire his jersey on Feb. 7, a Sunday night game against the Knicks, a ceremony and a game you will be able to watch on NBC and stream on Peacock, part of Sunday Night Basketball.

"Many players make an impact but only great players leave a legacy. Russell Westbrook's legacy was built both on the court and in the community," Thunder Executive Vice President Sam Presti said in a statement announcing the honor. "His mindset was relentless, his commitment deep. For all those reasons, Oklahomans consider him one of their own and his number will be raised to the rafters, in Oklahoma City forever."

It was considered a bit of a reach, and a bet on his athleticism, when Presti drafted Westbrook No. 4 out of UCLA — a bet that paid off handsomely. Westbrook played 11 seasons in Oklahoma City and was key to the Thunder reaching their first NBA Finals in 2012. In those 11 seasons, the Thunder made the playoffs nine times and reached the Western Conference Finals four times.

Westbrook's greatest season in Oklahoma City was the 2016-17, when he racked up 42 triple-doubles on his way to averaging 31.6 points, 10.7 rebounds and 10.4 assists — plus making a host of clutch plays for the team — and going on to win Most Valuable Player. Westbrook finished his career with 209 triple-doubles, the most in league history.

Westbrook is a lock future Hall of Famer: An MVP and member of the NBA's 75th Anniversary Team, a nine-time All-NBA and All-Star, and a two-time scoring champion.

"We are incredibly proud to retire Russell's number. Russell made a lasting mark on the city and state during his time with the team, and his involvement in the community has endured long after," Thunder Chairman Clayton I. Bennett said in a statement. "We are deeply grateful for his genuine care for the people of Oklahoma, and he will always be considered a member of the Oklahoma City community."

That community will rally to celebrate him on Feb. 7.

#2783. Nate Oats on why freshmen remain key to Alabama basketball amid NCAA eligibility woes

The changing landscape of college basketball has given coaches more opportunities to construct experienced rosters, oftentimes at the expense of high school prospects, but Nate Oats isn't abandoning one of the principles that has helped Alabama basketball succeed.

Nine newcomers joined the Crimson Tide for the 2026-27 season, six of them freshmen — including Cole Cloer, who spent his first year of college hoops sidelined by injury at NC State.

That's nothing new for Oats, who had 10 newcomers when he took the helm in 2019 and again when Alabama reached the Final Four during the 2023-24 season.

"I still think, with the way that we play, the way I always want to do things, freshmen are a big deal," Oats said Monday, Nov. 2.

REQUIRED READING: How much Alabama basketball is paying 2026 nonconference opponents, per contracts

Asked how his approach to developing so many young players — whether new to his system, or college altogether — has evolved amid college athletics' changing financial landscape, Oats pointed to the contributions Alabama has received from freshmen in previous seasons. He also highlighted how this year's rookies impacted the preseason.

Philadelphia 76ers guard Labaron Philon Jr. made an immediate impact before he declared for the NBA draft the first time after his freshman season with the Crimson Tide. Returning players Amari Allen and London Jemison helped Alabama to a fourth consecutive Sweet 16 as freshmen last season.

Oats' latest group, he believes, can follow those examples.

"We've got five of them that I think will be pretty strong in the rotation," Oats said.

Three have already demonstrated their potential through Alabama's exhibition wins over Furman, Ole Miss and Auburn.

Oats noted Qayden Samuels and Cloer each averaged 16 points per game as Tarris Bouie averaged 12, combining to account for more than 40% of Alabama's scoring in the preseason.

The Crimson Tide's 2026 signing class ranked sixth in the nation in the 247Sports composite rankings. Injury, however, prevented the two 5-stars in that class — forward Jaxon Richardson and guard Anderson Diaz — from showing their full potential in exhibition play.

Richardson, the son of NBA former Jason Richardson, debuted against Auburn, but Diaz is still recovering from a left elbow injury ahead of the opener.

"Jaxon can really make us a special team with all the different things he can do, and then Anderson can really help," Oats said.

But the continued survival of Oats' philosophy faces challenges as eligibility disputes and lawsuits to compete despite age or professional level remain on the rise.

"I think this 2027 high school class is gonna get a little bit, I don't know, however you want to say, they're gonna get squeezed a little bit," Oats said. "Except for the guys suing to get the fifth year now, everybody that's playing, that's not playing off a lawsuit, pretty much they can all come back this year."

The NCAA Division I Cabinet approved the new five-year eligibility rules on June 23, 2026, with the changes finalized June 24. They took effect Aug. 1, 2026, and will apply fully to incoming college athletes beginning in fall 2027. The new rules do not restore eligibility to players who exhausted their four seasons by the end of 2025-26, but some athletes have separately secured additional eligibility through court rulings, which is another factor contributing to the uncertainty for high school prospects.

Oats said Alabama is "still going to recruit some freshmen" and noted returning guard Preston Murphy Jr. previously had a medical redshirt before transferring to Alabama, which would result in his eligibility expiring at the conclusion of the 2026-27 season.

"So we will have some openings and we're planning on filling them with some of the really good freshmen that we're going after," Oats said.

Alabama picked up the second 5-star of its 2027 recruiting class on Oct. 31 in small forward Zion Green out of Arizona, who joins guard Chase Lumpkin out of Georgia.

While legal battles could leave fewer opportunities for incoming freshmen across college basketball, Oats remains committed to giving his the chance to help the Crimson Tide win now.

Emilee Smarr covers Alabama basketball and Crimson Tide athletics for The Tuscaloosa News. She can be reached via email at esmarr@usatodayco.com.

#2778. Giants pursuing 'aggressive' strategy as trade deadline approaches: 'We’re turning over every stone'

Since losing franchise quarterback Jaxson Dart to a complex knee injury in Week 1, the Giants have faced an onslaught of injuries on both ends of the field, including starters Brian Burns and Tremaine Edmunds. Tyrone Tracy is on injured reserve with a hamstring injury. Theo Johnson, Marcus Mbow, Greg Newsome II, and Jevon Holland were all listed as questionable as of Oct. 27. Most recently, Cam Skattebo tweaked his hamstring at the tail end of a 42-yard run in overtime during their Week 7 win over the Houston Texans.

They've found ways to weather the storm, battling their way to an NFC East-leading 4-2-1 record, but it hasn't been pretty. With just nine days to go until the trade deadline, set for 4 p.m. on Nov. 10, head coach John Harbaugh said the team is doing everything it can to fill some of those holes.

"We're turning over every stone. We're making every call," the head coach said Monday afternoon. "We're getting all the information we can possibly get at all times, and we are not apologizing for that at any point in time, ever."

Harbaugh even got in on the action last week, calling Tennessee Titans owner Amy Adams Strunk in an attempt to trade for star pass rusher Jeffrey Simmons. Though the unorthodox move didn't work — Simmons went to the Chicago Bears — Harbaugh said his bid was part of the all-hands-on-deck mentality the Giants' front office holds when it comes to making the most of the deadline.

"The way they've done it here in the past, it's transparent in our building," he said. "We're all talking to each other. We all know what each other's doing. I know who Joe's calling. If I'm calling somebody, Joe knows who I'm calling."

He added that his 28 years of NFL coaching experience have allowed him to develop strong relationships with people across the league, especially team owners. While general manager Joe Schoen might have a better shot at negotiating trades with other GMs, Harbaugh feels he also has a responsibility to take advantage of his connections.

But he was clear that the Big Blue aren't shopping just to shop. Harbaugh said he's confident in the group of players currently in New York's locker room, and he wouldn't want to jeopardize the system the team has already built.

"We've got a great camaraderie, a great brotherhood, synergy," he said. "All those things are really, really good right now, and you want to take that into account, even to the point where you probably talk to some of your guys before you do something like that. Make sure that everybody understands, and they're on board with it.

Besides, with only six picks in the 2027 Draft, the Giants don't have much to spend. As a result, he's more interested in protecting the resources they already have and adding for the future than looking for a quick, potentially short-lived jolt.

"For me, [I wouldn't want it to be] a one-and-done kind of thing," he said. "You want to have a short-term and a long-term win if you're going to give up draft capital."

#2777. The NFL, Caleb Williams shoe dispute takes center stage on Monday night

Monday night’s game between the Bears and Seahawks in Seattle comes with a subplot. Other than the ongoing Joe Buck and Troy Aikman drama.

The league has twice fined Chicago quarterback Caleb Williams for displaying an “unauthorized logo” (i.e., “the logo of a company that doesn’t pay us”) on his shoes. After the Week 6 game against the Falcons, the league fined Williams $11,941. After the Week 7 game against the Patriots, the league fined Williams $23,882 — twice the amount of the first fine.

Williams has said he will continue to wear the Caddix cleats, citing safety considerations. Unlike most if not all other NFL players who use Caddix footwear, Williams declines to cover the identifying markings with tape.

While the controversy calls back to former Bears quarterback Jim McMahon’s battles with the league over his Adidas headband during a playoff game (after being fined $5,000, he went with “ROZELLE” for the NFC Championship and “POW-MIA” for the Super Bowl) and to Bears linebacker Brian Urlacher’s whopping $100,000 fine for wearing a VitaminWater hat to Super Bowl XLI media day, Williams’s choice of footwear is rooted in the basic belief that the shoes promote player safety.

As to the refusal to cover the shoes with tape to hide the Caddix markings, it’s unclear whether he’s concerned about undermining the performance of the shoe or whether he’s simply trying to prove a point.

The NFL is clearly trying to prove a point, by fining him repeatedly. And while both prior fines are pending on appeal, a third fine is inevitable unless Williams bends.

Regardless, the next chapter arrives tonight. Neither of the fine letters threaten Williams with preventing him from playing if he doesn’t tape over the logos and markings. If the league decides to do that, Williams will have a pretty big decision to make.

Coach Ben Johnson was asked about that possible outcome on Saturday. “He’s all about the team, and I know he’s not going to hurt the team,” Johnson said.

The question of whether Johnson would be “hurting the team” is a matter of perspective. Williams wouldn’t be “hurting the team” by refusing to play; the NFL would be “hurting the team” by refusing to let Williams play in his preferred shoes.

Either way, both sides have been stubborn. It seems that a compromise could be reached, somehow.
