#2776. Disciplined Cole Young is rewarded with first career Gold Glove

When the new GTA game drops in two weeks, don't expect to find Mariners second baseman Cole Young in Vice City.

"I know it can be very addicting," he laughed on a media call earlier today. "I don't want to get addicted to that game."

The disciplined Young has plans for his off-season that don't involve boosting virtual cars (or, one presumes, collecting fairy bottles). Instead, he plans to build on the season that ended with a significant piece of hardware, as Young took home his first career Gold Glove yesterday.

A steady performer in the minor leagues who made an equally steady climb up to MLB, Young has gotten to where he is largely thanks to his ability to be the same person every day: never getting too high or too low, his gift is his consistency. But even Young couldn't deny how much he wanted to win the Golden Glove when his name started emerging as a candidate mid-season, a vision that sharpened until becoming reality on Sunday night.

"Just talking with [Mariners infield coach] Perry Hill, we were talking about if I had a chance or not," said Young the day after his big win. "Honestly, I had no idea. I knew it was a close close run, and I'm super grateful to be in that position."

Young put himself into that position thanks to the same thing that's propelled him steadily through his professional career: his top-tier discipline. In identifying areas he could improve in from his rookie year to his sophomore campaign, Young put refining his defense front and center, honing in on it as one of the things over which he could exert the most control in a sport that often feels out of a player's hands.

"The thing I've noticed with defense is that you can control a lot of the stuff," said Young. "You can control your pre-pitch routine. You can control where you think the ball is going to get hit. There's just so many controllables on defense."

Young was maybe a dark horse in the second base race compared to the other candidates, the Royals' Michael Massey and Chase Meidroth, who went to the playoffs with the White Sox. Both had better numbers by MLB's in-house stat, Outs Above Average (OAA), a range-based stat, but Massey had significantly fewer games at second base than the other two. However, Young outperformed Meidroth in Sports Info Solutions' Defensive Runs Saved (DRS), racking up 17 – best among AL second basemen and eighth-best overall – to Meidroth's 0.

For Young, the award is a partial reward for an off-season spent attacking and correcting the flaws that limited his playing time in his rookie season. Young realized that in baseball, everything begins with the body, and applied himself to both overhauling his conditioning and improving his feet and his hands, using as a basis Perry Hill's "six Fs" of fielding (feet, field, funnel, footwork, fire, follow).

"The number one thing for me this past off-season was getting my body in good shape, just so I could move better, get my body in better position so I could make difficult plays. That was number one for me," said Young, who said this year he felt like he could have gone on playing well past the end of the season thanks to a better focus on conditioning and taking care of his body to withstand the rigors of a 162-game season.

"The second one was improving my hands. So I did a lot of stuff I learned from 'Bone' (Hill), the six F's, and worked really hard the past season so when I show up to spring training, I'm able to play every single day. Hold my own on defense and not be a liability."

Young said he noticed the difference immediately upon arriving at spring training, and that confidence carried forward as he stacked strong innings in spring on both sides of the ball that carried over into the regular season.

"Once I got to spring training, that's when I noticed – once I got out on the field I just felt like I was able to move better. I felt really good in spring and that gave me some confidence going into the season that I could play every day in the big leagues and be good defensively. But I think it started in spring training, getting a lot of reps and making myself feel comfortable, that's when I noticed I made a leap forward and it gave me a bunch of confidence."

That confidence was hard-fought and built over the off-season where Young focused intently on improving his turns at second base, trying to master the switch to the other side of the bag and the different body positioning needed. But even as a (now Gold Glove-winning) second baseman, Young says he still carries a shortstop mentality, trying to think about being rangy and having a strong arm, something he said he wants to focus especially hard on training this off-season just in case he's needed at shortstop, as he was called upon to do a few times this season.

"I'm so used to the second base throws, so just making sure throughout the season that I'm maintaining my arm strength, and that starts in the off-season…just building the arm strength and being versatile."

"It's weird, because coming up in the minor leagues I was a shortstop, and you work on having a ton of range [at that position], so I feel like I'm always training like I am a shortstop, so when I do go to second base I can still have that range and have a strong arm. So deep down, I try to look at myself as a shortstop, just to like, not limit my range or anything like that."

Positional versatility is something Perry Hill preached constantly, both in general and to Young as an individual. With the coaching staff overturn, the Mariners opted not to bring Hill back with one year left on his contract, so Young will have to make another adjustment this upcoming season after spending years building his relationship with "Bone" – although he's quick to say this isn't the end of their friendship.

"I feel like me and Bone in the past couple years have developed such a good relationship, and I know it's not going to be the end of the relationship," said Young. "I've talked to him a couple times this off-season, and I know we're definitely going to stay in touch. But it's just, I learned so much from him, and he's changed me defensively in such a positive way, and I definitely would not have won this award if it weren't for him. So we're definitely going to stay in touch forever. He's meant so much to me."

Perhaps sensing that a coaching change was coming, Young allowed himself to want to win the Gold Glove – something over which he had no control, except with the defensive effort he put out on the field every day.

"It's hard," he admitted. "You have to come up every single day and have that mentality, and it takes a lot of discipline. So for me, I feel like I worked really hard on defense to make those improvements, and I feel like I was disciplined every single game, and I was on top of that, so it means a lot to win this award. I'm happy all the work I put in this past off-season paid off, and the discipline this whole year – I'm glad it paid off."

Appropriately, the low-key sophomore second baseman had a low-key celebration at home with his dad in Pittsburgh, where he spends the off-season in the snow and the cold rather than retreating to Florida or Arizona like many of his other baseball peers. Rather than attending a fancy training academy, Young works out with two pitchers: Pittsburgh-born Mason Miller and Mariners farmhand Scott Effross.

His other training partner: dad Rick, who he says is always willing to hit ground balls or feed the fielding machine for him. That's apparently where the Gold Glove trophy will reside, as well, with Young's dad:

"He's already got a spot picked out in his room," said Young.

Although they're turning the ball over to Minshew, he won't have an easy go in his first start of the year set to come on the road in Week 9 against the Seahawks—who Arizona hasn't beat since 2021. As if playing at Lumen Field wasn't difficult enough, he'll face head coach Mike Macdonald's defense, who's allowing the fewest yards and third-fewest points per game.

#2770. Browns look to bounce back after a reality check in Pittsburgh

CLEVELAND (AP) — The Cleveland Browns surprised many with five wins in their first seven games.

Sunday's 27-6 loss to Pittsburgh though proved that the Browns should quiet down their bark.

Especially when it comes to playing their AFC North rivals.

Jared Verse and his teammates did plenty of talking after Cleveland's 27-24 victory on Oct. 1. The rematch in Pittsburgh was a different matter as the Steelers dominated all four quarters and showed that the Browns remain a young team.

"It's a good learning lesson for this young team, that it's week to week, and the bottom line is in this league is you speak with your play," coach Todd Monken said. "That's what's going to happen when you play a good team, a well-coached team; and you don't play your best, you don't give yourself a chance. That's what we did. And that's going to happen. And we earned that one."

In what was a prove-it-game for Cleveland, many players didn't rise to the challenge. At the forefront was the linebacker Verse — who gave the Steelers plenty of bulletin board material for what he considered dirty play in the first game — had only one assisted tackle and wasn't a factor.

The run game reverted back to its struggles, averaging only 2.3 yards per carry with the longest gain being 7 yards.

Deshaun Watson had 324 passing yards, but 155 came in the fourth quarter when the game was out of reach.

"We didn't do enough on our end ... and they did exactly what they needed to do," Verse said. "That's completely on us, on offense, defense, special teams, across the board ... nobody did enough."

The challenge for Monken and the coaching staff is to get things back on track and make sure the 5-3 Browns don't drop consecutive games for the first time this season. The rest of the November schedule is favorable, beginning with next week's trip to New Orleans and a home game against underachieving Houston on Nov. 15 before the bye week.

Cleveland then hosts Las Vegas on Nov. 29 before resuming division play against Cincinnati the following week.

Monken said on Monday that most of the focus was looking at the film and improvements.

"Then you flush it and you say, 'We're 5-3.' and it's good to have high expectations," he said. "This is a good spot to be in. To be disappointed that you're not 6-2 and that you didn't find a way to win the game on the road and be 3-0 in the division."

Cornerback Denzel Ward has picked off passes in consecutive games for the second time in his career. It is the fourth time in his nine-year career he has three interceptions in a season, but he has never had four or more.

Run defense. Pittsburgh's Jaylen Warren is the third running back to have a 100-yard game against the Browns. Warren had 143 yards and the Steelers finished with 200 rushing yards.

Harold Fannin Jr.'s 154 receiving yards were the second most in franchise history by a tight end. He also set a career high with 10 receptions.

The left side of the offensive line, which allowed four sacks and 12 quarterback pressures. Rookie offensive tackle Spencer Fano had his toughest game of the season, giving up seven pressures and a pair of sacks. Guard Zion Johnson allowed five pressures and two sacks. The Steelers finished with eight sacks.

Defensive tackle Maliek Collins (elbow) and cornerback Tyson Campbell (concussion) continue to be evaluated.

23: Consecutive regular-season games the Browns have lost at Pittsburgh, tied for the longest road losing streak against an opponent in NFL history. Detroit lost 23 straight at Green Bay from 1992 through 2014.

The Browns head to New Orleans and look to snap a three-game losing streak against the Saints. Cleveland has dropped its past two trips to the Superdome.

#2766. 5 reasons why the Cowboys lost to Cardinals

There's something clearly wrong with the Dallas Cowboys. For as much talent as they have, and yes, that phrase has been used so often over the last decade that it's become cliché, it still rings true. The Cowboys' front office made a concerted effort to field a better defense, and some of those additions didn't come cheap.

Yet the results continue to be underwhelming. The squandered opportunity against the Arizona Cardinals is simply the latest chapter in what has become a disappointing 2026 season. The Cowboys had every opportunity to win this game and failed to capitalize.

So where did things go wrong? Here are five reasons the Cowboys lost.

After last week's performance in Philadelphia, it felt like Javonte Williams and the run game might be turning a corner. Williams set a career high in rushing yards, and there was reason to believe Dallas could ride that momentum into this week's matchup. That never happened. Williams was given very little room to operate behind the offensive line, and whenever Dallas needed a tough yard, Arizona seemed to have an answer.

He was stopped short on a critical fourth-down attempt in the first quarter on Dallas' side of the field. Later, he was stuffed near the goal line in another key situation. Simply put, it wasn't his day, nor the offensive line's, who was just as much at fault.

Williams finished with 24 carries for 56 yards, averaging just 2.3 yards per carry. While the running game was certainly a factor in the loss, it wasn't nearly as damaging as some of the other issues that plagued Dallas throughout the afternoon.

Tyler Allgeier is a solid running back, but the Cowboys made him look like an All-Pro. Allgeier finished with 191 yards from scrimmage after stepping into a larger role due to injuries in Arizona's backfield. Once Dallas built its lead, it looked as though the Cardinals would struggle to generate offense consistently.

Instead, Allgeier took over the game in the second half. His 63-yard touchdown run perfectly encapsulated everything that's been wrong with Dallas defensively this season. Missed tackles. Poor pursuit angles. Failure to shed blocks. At no point did it feel like the Cowboys were playing with the urgency necessary to stop him.

He followed that up with another explosive run of 52 yards, highlighted by Arizona center Hjalte Froholdt flattening Caleb Downs twice on the same play. More than anything, though, this speaks to a larger issue. The Cowboys simply do not tackle well. They haven't all season, and they continue to allow explosive plays because of it.

Allgeier's touchdown reception was another example. Multiple defenders converged on him near the goal line, but instead of wrapping up and finishing the play, they allowed him to extend the football across the plane.It's not that Allgeier is some unstoppable force. It's that Arizona played with a level of effort and physicality that Dallas failed to match.

No matter what happens, it's almost always preferable to receive the second-half kickoff. Whether you're playing from ahead or behind, having the first possession after halftime gives you a chance to either extend your lead or cut into a deficit.

Dallas found themselves in an ideal situation entering halftime. The Cowboys led 14-3 and had multiple opportunities to completely take control of the game if only they hadn't squandered them.

The first opportunity came with just over a minute remaining in the second quarter. Dallas got the ball looking to add points before the break, but the drive quickly went nowhere after a Dak Prescott sack helped force a three-and-out. Then came a second chance.

After forcing a fumble, the Cowboys got the ball back with another opportunity to put points on the board before halftime. Unfortunately, Brandon Aubrey missed the field goal attempt, and Dallas headed into the locker room still leading by just 11.

The missed opportunities didn't stop there. Dallas opened the second half with the football and put together an impressive drive that marched deep into Cardinals territory while taking more than eight minutes off the clock. It looked like the exact response a good football team should have after halftime. Instead, Ryan Flournoy mishandled a pass from Prescott and stalled the drive. The Cowboys settled for three points.

Had Dallas converted before halftime and punched in a touchdown to open the third quarter, this game likely looks very different. Arizona probably never feels the need to shake things up at quarterback, and the comeback attempt may never materialize in the first place.

The Cowboys had multiple opportunities to put the game away and failed every time. They cracked the door open for Arizona and the Cardinals kicked it down.

If Dallas truly wanted to establish the run, it would help to feature the one receiver on the roster most capable of forcing defenses to respect the deep ball. For the second straight week, George Pickens was largely an afterthought.

Ten targets over the last two weeks simply isn't enough for a player with his skill set. Pickens finished with two catches for 48 yards, including a 37-yard reception, and appeared to have a 59-yard touchdown wiped off the board by a holding penalty. Even after proving he could challenge Arizona vertically, Dallas never made a concerted effort to get him more involved.

That's been one of the more frustrating parts of the offense lately. There are stretches where Pickens completely disappears for an entire half. While CeeDee Lamb was heavily featured and Ryan Flournoy made some important plays, the Cowboys needed an offensive spark and rarely looked to one of their most explosive weapons.

That became even more puzzling as the game wore on. Particularly late in regulation and into overtime, Pickens should have been a focal point. There's simply too much talent invested in him for Dallas to go extended stretches without throwing him the football. The Cowboys can point to several reasons why the offense came up short. Not utilizing one of their most dangerous playmakers enough belongs near the top of the list.

There's no easy way around it. Coaching is the biggest reason the Cowboys lost. The defense giving up points is unfortunately nothing new. Allowing 33 points feels almost routine at this stage. It's disappointing, but it isn't surprising. What's becoming increasingly concerning is how the offense is being managed. The questionable decision-making started early.

Backed up deep in their own territory, Brian Schottenheimer opened a drive with a quarterback sneak. He followed it with an incomplete pass before calling a halfback draw on third down, effectively waving the white flag and choosing to punt from deep in his own territory.

The frustrating part is that we've seen this before. The Cowboys are paying significant money to Dak Prescott, CeeDee Lamb, and George Pickens. Yet time and again their head coach removes the game from their hands by calling conservatively in situations where aggression is warranted.

It's reminiscent of the decision-making that has followed Schottenheimer throughout the season. Rather than trust his playmakers to create an advantage, he routinely defaults to the safest possible option. The most damning example came late in the game.

Holding a 27-24 lead with just over two minutes left, Schottenheimer had an opportunity to put the game away. Instead of remaining aggressive and forcing Arizona to defend the entire field, the Cowboys became conservative. He effectively challenged a backup quarterback to march the length of the field despite watching Arizona move the ball successfully on multiple consecutive possessions.

Again, he trusted his defense over Dak Prescott, CeeDee Lamb, and George Pickens. At best, that's negligent game management. At worst, it's coaching scared. Too often this season, Schottenheimer has coached not to lose rather than coached to win. That mindset has shown itself repeatedly and goes a long way toward explaining why Dallas sits at 3-5 despite possessing far more talent than its record suggests.

For all the talk about being an elite play-caller, the results simply haven't matched the confidence. That's not to absolve Christian Parker and the defense from criticism. They deserve plenty of it. But Parker is at least relatively inexperienced in his current role.

Schottenheimer doesn't have that excuse. He has decades of NFL experience, and throughout this season he has repeatedly shown an inability to maximize the talent available to him. His game management, play design, and in-game adjustments have often left the Cowboys playing uphill when they should be dictating the action. Simply put, he's not giving Dallas an advantage on Sundays.

This situation feels different than the Jason Garrett years. Back then, you could at least argue the front office wasn't fully committed to maximizing the roster. They rarely pursued major free agents and were hesitant to make splashy moves.

That's no longer the case. The Cowboys invested in this team. They spent money on defense. They added talent offensively. They made aggressive moves and trades with the expectation of competing now. Yet for all of Schottenheimer's confidence at the podium, the performance on the field hasn't backed it up.

Look around the roster. Count the first-round picks. Look at the money invested in star players on both sides of the ball. This team has far too much talent to be sitting at 3-5 and watching a once-winnable division begin to slip away. At this point, the Cowboys appear to be in over their heads, and much of that falls on the head coach.

The roster has flaws. The defense has flaws. The players deserve criticism. But the biggest problem facing the Cowboys right now is that their head coach doesn't appear capable of elevating the talent around him. And until that changes, losses like this one are going to keep happening.

#2765. JJ Redick believes Sandro Mamukelashvili is a ‘rare’ offensive player

After bouncing around the league for a few seasons and struggling to truly break out, Sandro Mamukelashvili made a name for himself last year with the Toronto Raptors. He played in 80 games, finished 10th in Sixth Man of the Year voting and averaged career highs in points (11.2), rebounds (4.9) and assists (1.9) per game while shooting 38.9% from deep and 64.2% inside the arc. Over the summer. the Los Angeles Lakers took note of that stellar campaign and inked him to a four-year, $52 million deal.

Although Mamukelashvili hasn't been as efficient beyond the arc as last year yet (7-of-21, 33.3%), his versatile offensive game — rich with driving, passing and spacing abilities — is helping lather up the Lakers' attack. According to databallr, their offensive rating is 4.4 points better with him on the floor thus far (122.0 points vs. 117.6 points per 100), whatever you deem that worth in a small sample through seven games.

"He's a really rare offensive player because he's an advantage creator in some ways. So if he's guarded by a five and he can pick and pop into space, that's an advantage creation," head coach JJ Redick told reporters before Sunday's win over the Raptors. "He's really good at follows and touch rolls, roller behind, so that's an advantage creation. So he's an advantage creator and then his 0.5 [second] decision-making and his shooting ability and finishing ability, he's an advantage finisher. And then on top of that, he's also a connector. There's very few offensive players that are all three of those things and he is."

Mamukelashvili's malleable skill-set enables the Lakers to deploy him in an array of beneficial ways. He can space or screen off of the ball. He can attack closeouts when Luka Dončić and Austin Reaves compromise defenses. He can operate as a playmaking hub to ease Dončić and Reaves' creation responsibilities. There's plenty to like and that's been on display to begin the year.

His next chance to showcase all that he provides is Monday night at 7 p.m. against the Miami Heat.

#2764. Drake Maye's Former UNC Teammate Would Be Perfect Trade Target for Patriots

Drake Maye's Former UNC Teammate Would Be Perfect Trade Target for Patriots originally appeared on NESN. Add NESN as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

The New England Patriots came away with a Week 8 win against the Miami Dolphins, but it didn't feel like they played great football.

The Dolphins, a team that isn't as bad as its record indicates at 1-6, actually played pretty decent football in this game.

When we also factor in the weather situation in Miami, there were many potential problems at play.

While the Patriots came away with a win, and there were some reasons why they might not have played great, it did lead to some questions about the wide receiver position once again. A.J. Brown was out, and Drake Maye completed just 16 of 20 passes.

Again, the weather was certainly a factor, but to see Maye only attempt 20 passes is a somewhat telling sign of how much trust there is in this receiving corps at the moment. In order to change that, the Patriots would have to make a trade before the deadline.

One interesting fit would be Josh Downs. Downs, with the Indianapolis Colts, is expected to be available. Interestingly enough, he was also teammates with Drake Maye in college, which has led some to believe that there could be a possible connection there.

"The Patriots have a Drake Maye connection and a thin receiver room. Downs and Maye overlapped at North Carolina in 2022, when Downs caught 94 passes for 1,029 yards and 11 touchdowns, and Sports Illustrated's Mike Kadlick listed New England among the best fits for Downs.

"The need is real. Romeo Doubs is on injured reserve with a shoulder injury, Mack Hollins is out with a calf injury and A.J. Brown has nine catches for 101 yards since coming back from his ankle sprain, which is why we argued his injury should alter the Patriots' expectations. At 4-3, New England is still deciding whether to buy, which is the only thing keeping it out of the top spot," Neal Coolon of Athlon wrote.

Downs has had a decent season, but I wouldn't say it's been as promising as some of his prior years.

He has 30 catches for 330 yards, good for 11 yards per catch. However, he scored his first touchdown of the season in Week 8 against the Jacksonville Jaguars, despite only catching four passes for 23 yards.

In his defense, the Colts' offense is one of the worst in football, so it wouldn't be crazy to see him return to what he was in 2023 and 2024 when he was a consistent 750-plus-yard receiver.

MÜNCHEN (Bulinews) In their 141st-ever meeting, Bayern Munich and Borussia Dortmund played out and exciting and dramatic 2-2 draw at the Allianz Arena this evening. The Bavarian giants looked certain to have held out for victory until Fabio Silva arrived off the bench to secure BVB a previous point.

On top of an already raucous atmosphere before a ball was kicked, Harry Kane walked out to a hero’s reception. The Englishman raised his recently acquired Ballon d’Or aloft and thanked everyone for their support. Then it was down to business. Both teams had early spells of possession, but Bayern looked more threatening in comparison.

The first notable chance fell the way of the hosts, Alphonso Davies managed to burrow his way into the box. A kind deflection sat up nicely for Michael Olise, catching his half-volley sweetly. The post came to a rooted Gregor Kobel’s rescue. Dortmund also has a sniff at goal, but Bayern’s rearguard remained defiant in protecting Jonas Urbig.

That changed swiftly midway through the half when Bayern’s shot-stopper produced a fine parry low to his right to stop Jobe Bellingham. Kobel followed suit, denying Ismael Saibari in a one-on-one chance. Most worrying for Aaron Danks’ side, the Moroccan limped off the field as Nathaniel Brown filled in as the auxiliary attacking midfielder.

Serge Gnabry fell foul of injury too, forcing Josip Stanisic onto the field prompting Davies to advance on the left flank. Drama was at an all-time high when Dortmund appeared to grab the lead. Kim Min-Jae – at fault for a defensive mix-up – would be bailed out by VAR spotting Carney Chukwuemeka obstructing Urbig as the ball hit the net.

Four minutes later, the first goal arrived for the Bavarians. Olise’s pin-point pass released Konrad Laimer. The Austrian managed to convert at the second time of asking after Kobel’s initial save. Upon the restart, Kobel intervened once again after Olise and Laimer combined on the right for the latter to seek the bottom corner.

Bayern continued to pressure where possible, eventually yielding a penalty when Kane’s volley from a corner struck Felix Nmecha’s hand. Normally ever reliable from the spot, fans got a collector’s item as the Ballon d’Or winner’s penalty thundered back off the aluminium. Four minutes later, Borussia Dortmund struck for parity.

Nico Schlotterbeck stole the ball, fed Chukwuemeka on the right and the Austrian assisted the lurking Guirassy who steered it inside the left post. However, the sides remained level for all of six minutes. Kimmich’s clever chip to the back was redirected by Kane. Dayot Upamecano given the easy task of a tap-in finish.

Entering the closing chapter, Kostas Karetsas, Marcel Sabitzer and Fabio Silva gave BVB fresh legs. The Portuguese marksman would prove to be highly useful in the end. Swivelling in the box, he managed to squeeze the ball beyond Urbig's reach. An eruption of noise in the away end followed as BVB held out through four minutes of injury time to snatch a vital point.

#2761. John Harbaugh admits calling Amy Adams Strunk about Jeffery Simmons trade

Giants coach John Harbaugh apparently ruffled some feathers in Nashville by making a phone call to Titans owner Amy Adams Strunk in an effort to spark trade discussions for defensive tackle Jeffery Simmons. And Harbaugh doesn’t care about any “code” that he may have violated by going over the head of Titans G.M. Mike Borgonzi.

Harbaugh admitted to reporters on Monday that he made the call. And he vowed to make similar calls, if it’s in the best interests of the team.

“I did, I did,” Harbaugh said regarding whether he called Strunk. “I left a message. . . . Because you turn over every stone. You know, gonna try everything. I mean, I just, I’ll put it out there, man, we’re open for any call. Anybody wants to call us or call anybody. Call.”

Next came a discussion about songs that send the “call me” message, from the Blondie song of that name to the more recent Call Me Maybe.

“Let’s put that out there,” Harbaugh said. “Put that out there. It was kind of, like, fun to see the outpouring of interest in that. I enjoyed it. We’re not like — we’re not part of any secret society oath that says you can only talk to somebody. We’re not in that. We didn’t get memoed on that.  We’re not in that agreement. So like I said, anybody can call us. We won’t be adhering to that particular agreement.”

Amen to all of that. The only rules that should matter are the written rules. Those who tiptoe around unwritten rules undermine their chances to get shit done.

Besides, calls between teams happen at many different levels. There isn’t an exclusive G.M.-to-G.M. network of bat phones.

In this specific case, the Giants apparently weren’t getting anywhere with Borgonzi. So they got creative, and resourceful.

As we understand it, they didn’t hear from the Titans to see if they’d match or beat the offer the Bears made for Simmons. Which would seem to be the sensible thing to do before finalizing a deal — unless the Bears expressly set a firm and quick take-it-or-leave-it deadline that prevented the Titans from exploring whether more could have been gotten from another team.

#2760. Todd Monken expects to see sacks start to "pile up" for Jared Verse

Browns defensive end Jared Verse made a fair amount of noise heading into Sunday’s game against the Steelers, but he was mostly silent on the field in Pittsburgh.

Verse had one tackle in the 27-6 Steelers win and that continued the slow start to his Browns tenure. Verse had 8.5 sacks and 34 quarterback hits in 20 total games for the Rams in 2025, but had no sacks and six quarterback hits since coming to Cleveland in the Myles Garrett trade.

At a Monday press conference, Browns head coach Todd Monken was asked about the lack of splash plays and whether the expectations for immediate impact were unrealistic. Monken preached patience while expressing confidence that bigger things are coming for Verse.

“Well, I think it’s too early to say anything. I mean, I love the way Jared works every day since he’s come here, just like all of our guys, he loves to work,” Monken said, via a transcript from the team. “He likes being on the field and practicing. And I think it’s just a matter of time before, you know, he really gets comfortable in what we’re doing defensively. And it’s unfair to compare him to Myles Garrett. Be ahead in games, force the other team to throw it, and I think you’ll start to see his sacks start to pile up.”

The Browns have a 5-3 record despite the lack of contributions from Verse, but their hopes of making a playoff push over the second half would feel stronger if he starts making more headlines with his play than his locker room quotes.

#2759. Coby White to make season debut for Hornets after missing team's first 7 games with strained calf

The Charlotte Hornets are getting one piece of their projected starting guard tandem back in the lineup.

Point guard Coby White was not listed on Monday's injury report, and the team announced he will make his 2026-27 season debut versus the Detroit Pistons.

White, 26, strained his left calf during training camp, sidelining him through the preseason and the Hornets' first seven games of the regular season. In his absence, Dennis Schröder has played well, averaging 11.7 points and 5.4 assists.

However, Charlotte presumably signed White to a 3-year, $74 million contract so he could be the starting point guard. The Hornets acquired White from the Chicago Bulls at last season's trade deadline, and in 21 games, he averaged 15.6 points with 3 assists while shooting 39% on 110 3-point attempts.

In addition to re-signing White to a new contract, the Hornets made his role clear by trading point guard LaMelo Ball to the Minnesota Timberwolves in exchange for center Naz Reid, several draft picks and first-round swaps.

White's best season was during the 2024-25 campaign when he took on more of a scoring role for the Bulls. He averaged 20.4 points and 4.5 assists, shooting 37% on a career-high 584 3-point shots. Over the past three seasons, White has scored 19.1 points per game with 4.6 assists and 3.9 rebounds.

While he may not be as much of a scorer or playmaker as Ball, the Hornets are hoping White will be more consistent. The eight-year veteran has shot 52% or better during the past four seasons. Additionally, Charlotte is looking for Kon Knueppel to assume more of a playmaking role when he returns.

Knueppel will comprise the other part of the Hornets' starting backcourt once he becomes available. He is out for Monday's game, but is close to returning from a strained left hamstring he sustained before training camp. Knueppel was assigned to the team's G League affiliate to practice last week before being recalled to the Hornets.

Charlotte announced on Monday that the team picked up Knueppel's third-year option for $11 million. He also has a $13.9 million team option for 2027-28 as part of his four-year rookie contract.

Following Monday's matchup with the Pistons, the Hornets (3-4) visit the Brooklyn Nets (1-5) on Wednesday and host the Boston Celtics (5-1) on Friday.

#2758. It's hard to tell because of all the Texas hype, but Missouri is a more complete team than Longhorns

AUSTIN - The question was harmless enough.

With three road games left in the last four weeks after only one true-road game all season, what's it going to take to get the job done?

"It's gonna take a lot, definitely," Texas linebacker Rasheem Biles said Monday. "We got some good teams coming up, on the road as well. But what better places? Where else would you want to play out on the road than LSU and Texas A&M. Obviously, we got one this week, but hey that's why you come to Texas."

Oh right, they do play another team, one that deserves its full attention.

The Longhorns have one this week, and it's not LSU or Texas A&M. Those are down the line.

This week it's Missouri, which like Texas is a middle-of-the-SEC pack team scrambling for College Football Playoff relevance. Both teams are 6-2 overall and 3-2 in league play, but not all records are created equal.

The Tigers lost to two teams that the Longhorns have beaten in Ole Miss and Mississippi State, but Texas had the luxury of playing both at home. Missouri fell to both on the road, but faced a full-strength MSU squad with a healthy Kamario Taylor whereas the Bulldogs had to come to Austin with their second- and third-string quarterbacks.

Sign up for Kirk Bohls' new newsletter for news, exclusive analysis and insights on University of Texas sports.

Certainly Biles didn't mean to suggest this week's Texas game at 11 a.m. in Columbia is an after-thought. Anything but. The Longhorns had better think of the Tigers night and day and any hours in between.

No one is saying the Tigers are a bunch of average Joes. They're just not one of the biggest name brands in the sport. Not a blueblood. Probably not even a light blueblood.

Remember, Missouri was the first Big 12 team to put its hand up and tell the Big Ten, "Take me, take me." The Big Ten replied no thanks.

The SEC, however saw value in Missouri and gratefully accepted the Tigers and the Aggies into the fold. Missouri even won the SEC East in two of its three years in that league. A&M's still waiting to win something.

Missouri's not Georgia, not Alabama, not a lot of teams.

What the Tigers are, however, is a damn good football team. Really good. A team on the cusp.

It's a complete team, more complete than Texas. It has an explosive quarterback and a powerhouse running attack that Steve Sarkisian called "fantastic" and excellent receivers. It's a team still in the hunt for the school's first SEC championship and, in fact, its first conference championship of any kind since - hold on, I'm checking - 1969. Been a minute.

And that was just a Big Eight co-championship. Its last outright came in 1960 when it claimed a national championship. Missouri never won the Big 12. Not once. The school claims just 12 conference titles, 10 of them coming in 1945 or prior.

Missouri head coach Eli Drinkwitz calls out towards officials during the first half of an NCAA college football game against Mississippi, Saturday, Oct. 17, 2026, in Oxford, Miss. (Rogelio V. Solis/AP Photo/Rogelio V. Solis)

It has had its share of very successful head coaches like legit Hall of Famers Dan Devine and Gary Pinkel, and Eli Drinkwitz could ultimately belong in that company as well. Like Sarkisian, Drinkwitz likes to call his own plays and played football as an all-state linebacker in high school at Alma, Ark., as Sark played quarterback at BYU.

Drinkwitz is one of the very few coaches who takes his job seriously but not himself. He brings a light-hearted fashion to the job.

He often channels his inner Mike Leach. He loves taking jabs at other schools and trolling victims as he did Saturday when he praised Arkansas for its new light bulbs at the stadium. While the Razorbacks might measure progress in 250 new LED lights, the Tiges are looking for more.

In any light, Drinkwitz is a winner and would be a terrific hire for Florida State, which is probably a better job if only because the Seminoles compete in an inferior ACC league.

This year's Tigers team is more than capable of beating anybody.

Sure, two of Missouri's six wins came against Group of 6 opponents in Arkansas-Pine Bluff and Troy, but two of Texas' were blowout victories against non-Power 4 opponents Texas State and UTSA. Yeah, Troy just knocked off G6 contender James Madison, but Texas State came within a whisker of upsetting G6 frontrunner Boise State and UTSA is 7-1 and leads the American Conference.

While their records are identical, Texas and Missouri, they couldn't be any more different in a lot of categories.

No team - and especially no two-loss team - gets more attention than the Longhorns with their ubiquitous A-list celebrity, Arch Manning, and stir-the-pot head coach. Texas will dominate any national conversation, be the Longhorns 6-2 or 2-6.

For real, the Longhorns are the Taylor Swifts of college football. The constant center of attention. The highest of profiles and guilty of overexposure. One's married to a marquee athlete, the other's wedded to controversy, whether it's an interview brushoff with Holly Rowe or a urination celebration on the field or a linebacker coach's DUI. Swift has one hit after another. Tenth-ranked Texas, college football's No. 1 team for four weeks, is basically a show-stopper as well while Missouri's the warmup act, albeit an accomplished one.

Texas quarterback Arch Manning (16) runs the ball during the game against Mississippi State at Darrell K Royal–Texas Memorial Stadium on Saturday, Oct. 31, 2026 in Austin. (Aaron E. Martinez/Austin American-Statesman)

No quarterback garners more attention than Manning. But Missouri's Austin Simmons has the better numbers with 16 touchdowns and two interceptions to Arch's line of 15 scores and seven picks.

Texas can't run the ball. Ran for 60 yards against Mississippi State. Missouri runs the ball for a living. Jamal Roberts, who started the season as Ahmad Hardy's backup, has already gone over 1,000 yards with back-to-back games of 200-plus yards. And now Hardy's back after missing seven games following his May incident in which he got shot at a concert.

And while Missouri is getting Hardy back into action, Texas has lost left tackle Trevor Goosby for the season, anxiously holds its breath until sack leader Colin Simmons returns from a high ankle sprain and is sweating the availability of four other starters who left the last game with injuries.

Texas can't consistently protect its quarterback. The Tigers have allowed eight sacks. Heck, the Longhorn offensive line gave up six in one game to Tennessee and another five to Ole Miss. That's why when Manning was asked if he was more bruised Saturday than any other game this year, he said, "Probably."

The SEC's leading punt returner plays for Missouri, not Texas. Ryan Niblett's great, but was denied a second runback for a touchdown by one of the Longhorns' dumb penalties. Missouri's DaMarion Fowlkes tops all SEC punt returners with an average of 17 yards.

Missouri also has two of the league's top eight receivers in leader Cayden Lee, who averages 97 yards a game, and Donovan Olugbode. No Longhorn ranks in the top 10.

The Tigers have committed just five in eight games and sit a plus-eight. Texas ranks 99th nationally at a minus-three.

In fact, Texas probably has an edge only on defense, and that's not even a huge separator. As strong as the Longhorns are against the run, the Tigers are even better numerically, allowing only 104 yards on the ground to Texas' 107.

But defense is where the Longhorns must flex their muscles. They might even need to score on that side of the ball, especially with the way Missouri protects the ball.

"They're really good, and they're going to be rolling at home," Manning said. " We're excited to be road warriors for the week and get it done."

This article originally published at It's hard to tell because of all the Texas hype, but Missouri is a more complete team than Longhorns.

#2754. Jayson Tatum named Eastern Conference Player of the Week for 14th time in career

Jayson Tatum is collecting hardware again, and we're only two weeks into the NBA season.

The NBA announced Monday that Tatum has been named Eastern Conference Player of the Week for Week 2 of the 2026-27 season, recognizing a four-game stretch in which he averaged 30.3 points, 5.8 rebounds and 7.0 assists while leading the Celtics to a perfect 4-0 record.

It marks the 14th Player of the Week honor of Tatum's career, bringing him within one of Larry Bird's 15 for second-most in Celtics franchise history. Paul Pierce holds the record with 17. Pretty good company to keep before your 29th birthday.

Victor Wembanyama took home Western Conference Player of the Week honors after averaging 36.0 points, 10.3 rebounds and 4.3 blocks while leading the San Antonio Spurs to a 3-0 record.

Tatum's award-winning week included a pair of blowouts, a dramatic overtime buzzer-beater against Brooklyn and plenty of evidence that his slow start from three hasn't done much to limit his effectiveness.

Boston opened the week by demolishing Chicago 134-99 on October 26. Tatum finished with 23 points, seven rebounds and six assists in just 28 minutes, getting to enjoy most of the fourth quarter from the bench.

The following night against Brooklyn was a very different experience.

Tatum erupted for 40 points, five rebounds and eight assists in Boston's 125-124 overtime victory over the Nets, capped by his game-winning 17-foot jumper at the buzzer. He also went 18-for-22 from the free-throw line, played 42 minutes and helped the Celtics survive a game that looked like it might end in heartbreak more than once.

He also finished with zero turnovers, an impressive feat considering how much of Boston's offense ran through him. Between his scoring and playmaking, Tatum managed to carry a heavy offensive workload without giving Brooklyn a single extra possession.

Against Chicago in Friday's NBA Cup opener, Tatum added 26 points, seven rebounds and seven assists as the Celtics built a lead as large as 32 before eventually winning 125-111.

He capped the week Sunday in Orlando, finishing with 32 points, four rebounds and seven assists in a 108-97 win over the Magic. Boston has now won five straight games since dropping its season opener in Detroit.

Beyond his scoring, Tatum's playmaking has been a standout part of his early-season performance. He's consistently found teammates cutting toward the basket and drawn additional defensive attention to create open looks elsewhere. His developing chemistry with Paul George has also given Boston another reliable way to initiate offense, with both players comfortable creating opportunities for each other and everyone else.

Tatum has been effective despite struggling with his outside shot, connecting on just 24.4 percent of his three-point attempts through six games. He's compensated by attacking the basket and getting to the free-throw line, where he made 46 of 55 attempts during his four award-winning games. That included 22 attempts against Brooklyn and another 17 against Orlando.

The strong start is particularly encouraging given Tatum's recovery from the Achilles injury he suffered in 2025. After returning to the court last season, he's quickly reestablished himself as Boston's offensive centerpiece while continuing to develop his blossoming partnership with George.

In short, Jayson Tatum looks like Jayson Tatum again. And he's apparently still getting warmed up.

#2751. Ranking the five worst games of the 2026 season

2026 was the most successful season in Brewers history, culminating in the franchise's first World Series championship. Still, even the best teams have off days.

Just like last year, I'll be ranking the five best and five worst Brewers games of the season, and since there was a lot more good than bad in 2026, I'm starting with the worst and saving the best for last.

The Brewers fell behind early in this one, but took the lead in the seventh on a two-run double by Blake Perkins and an RBI single from David Hamilton. However, Angel Zerpa gave up a game-tying solo homer to Jahmai Jones in the eighth. Abner Uribe came on in the ninth and struck out the first batter he faced, but then fell behind Spencer Torkelson 3-1. Torkelson sent the next pitch over the fence for a walk-off home run.

On top of the pain of a walk-off, this loss makes the list because it came in the middle of a stretch of five losses in six games. There's plenty of time to bounce back from April losses, but it's never great to start the season with a losing streak.

For most of this game, it looked like the Brewers were going to escape with a win against Braves ace Chris Sale. Sale didn't allow an earned run, but the Brewers scratched out two unearned runs against him on five hits. In the ninth, Aaron Ashby came in for what would have been his first save of the season and instead took his first loss. He got one out, then gave up a single to Matt Olson and a walk-off homer to Ozzie Albies.

This game was uniquely frustrating because Albies didn't exactly square up Ashby. His homer traveled just 339 feet and had an expected batting average of .090, per Statcast. This was a game the Brewers should have won; instead, it became their third straight loss.

#3: October 27, 2026 (World Series Game 4) — Rays 4, Brewers 3

The two worst losses listed in last year's article were both playoff games, which made sense given that the Brewers were swept in the NLCS. This year, their playoff run featured just two losses altogether: Game 3 against the Padres, and this one.

Milwaukee entered this game up 3-0 in the World Series with a chance to keep their foot on the gas and sweep the Rays for the first championship in franchise history. The Rays took an early lead, but the Brewers tied the game on a home run by light-hitting third baseman David Hamilton. Pat Murphy used closer Trevor Megill to get one out in the eighth, then brought in Aaron Ashby for what would be his last outing of the season, and Ashby delivered 2 1/3 scoreless innings.

Unfortunately, by the eleventh inning, the bullpen was running on fumes. Antonio Senzatela came in for Ashby after the first out of the eleventh, and while he managed to send the game to the twelfth, the only pitchers left in the bullpen were Kyle Harrison, Brandon Woodruff, and Shane Drohan. Milwaukee finally took the lead in the top of the twelfth courtesy of a homer from Cooper Pratt, but a throwing error from Joey Ortiz put Junior Caminero on first to start the bottom half. Drohan then came in and gave up four straight hits to lose the game.

It was the only World Series game the Brewers lost on their way to a five-game series win, which certainly softens the blow. Still, it makes the list because of how painful it was in the moment and how great a win would have been. If the Brewers had swept the Rays on a twelfth-inning homer from their rookie shortstop, and Drohan, who'd made just one other playoff appearance, had closed it out, it would have made for an incredible story. Instead, Milwaukee needed one more game.

This one makes the list simply because it was, statistically, the worst loss of the season, a 13-run defeat. Starter Thomas Pannone allowed a three-run homer to Daniel Susac, but the Brewers clawed back to make it 3-2 heading into the sixth. Then things went off the rails. Chad Patrick and Ashby combined to give up seven runs in the sixth, and by the eighth, infielder Andrew Vaughn was on the mound. Just one of those days.

#1: April 10, 2026 — Nationals 7, Brewers 3

This game takes the top spot because, even more than the others, it was a game the Brewers should have won (or at least shouldn't have lost this badly).

Going into the top of the ninth, the score was 3-3. Brewers manager Pat Murphy brought in his closer, Trevor Megill, to send the Brewers to the bottom of the frame with a chance to win. Here's a chronological recap of the beginning of the ninth inning:

Joey Wiemer reached on the first bunt hit of his career, partially because Luis Rengifo was playing way too far back.

Per Pat Murphy, "it wasn't even a good bunt."

Luis García Jr. singled into center field, scoring Abrams and sending Wiemer to third.

Jorbit Vivas laid down a safety squeeze but bunted it right back to the mound. Megill tried to get Wiemer at home but instead shoveled the ball past catcher William Contreras, and Wiemer scored on the throwing error.

Drew Millas laid down yet another bunt, right at Megill, but Megill, perhaps still rattled from the previous play, took way too long to throw it home. Pinch-runner Nasim Nuñez scored to make it 6-3, Nationals.

Murphy pulled Megill for Easton McGee, who gave up a 115.3-mph RBI double to James Wood.

For a team that preaches (and practices) sound fundamentals, this game was hard to watch. It was a very un-Brewers-like way to lose, which makes it, in my opinion, the worst loss of the season.

So, what do you think? Did I miss any losses that should have made the list? Let me know in the comments.

#2748. Todd Monken expects to see sacks start to "pile up" for Jared Verse

Browns defensive end Jared Verse made a fair amount of noise heading into Sunday's game against the Steelers, but he was mostly silent on the field in Pittsburgh.

Verse had one tackle in the 27-6 Steelers win and that continued the slow start to his Browns tenure. Verse had 8.5 sacks and 34 quarterback hits in 20 total games for the Rams in 2025, but had no sacks and six quarterback hits since coming to Cleveland in the Myles Garrett trade.

At a Monday press conference, Browns head coach Todd Monken was asked about the lack of splash plays and whether the expectations for immediate impact were unrealistic. Monken preached patience while expressing confidence that bigger things are coming for Verse.

"Well, I think it's too early to say anything. I mean, I love the way Jared works every day since he's come here, just like all of our guys, he loves to work," Monken said, via a transcript from the team. "He likes being on the field and practicing. And I think it's a matter of adapting to our style, which is, you know, there are some things that aren't as similar to what he was used to doing with the Rams and the attack style. And I think it's just a matter of time before, you know, he really gets comfortable in what we're doing defensively. And it's unfair to compare him to Myles Garrett. I think that's always been unfair. Myles is in an elite category of his own. That's okay. I mean, that's who he is. But we just need Jared to be the best version of Jared, and I think we're going to continue to see growth there. And again, what will help us? Be ahead in games, force the other team to throw it, and I think you'll start to see his sacks start to pile up."

The Browns have a 5-3 record despite the lack of contributions from Verse, but their hopes of making a playoff push over the second half would feel stronger if he starts making more headlines with his play than his locker room quotes.

#2747. Bayern provide initial injury updates on Olise, Saibari and Gnabry

Bayern Munich suffered a triple injury blow in their 2-2 draw with Borussia Dortmund in the Bundesliga on Saturday night.

Ismael Saibari was the first to be forced off after a collision with BVB goalkeeper Gregor Kobel, before Serge Gnabry also had to come off before half-time.

In the second half, Michael Olise became the third Bayern player to signal that he needed to be substituted, going off towards the end of the game.

After the final whistle, Bayern board member for sport Max Eberl provided an initial injury update on the trio.

Speaking about Saibari, who left the stadium on crutches, and Gnabry, Eberl told the media (via iMiaSanMia):

“Right now, it feels like things are getting a bit overwhelming with everything happening around us. Both [Saibari and Gnabry] injuries appear to be somewhat serious, although I can't give a prognosis or diagnosis at this stage. Both look more serious. Accordingly, today came at a very, very heavy cost.”

The initial signs are more encouraging for Olise, although he left the stadium limping, but further examinations are still pending.

“Regarding Olise, at the moment, our feeling is that it's not that serious, but it's difficult to say that with the adrenaline still pumping after a game like this. We'll have to wait and see,” Eberl added.

Bayern assistant coach Aaron Danks also had his say on the three injuries in an interview with Sky.

“Salibari was the ankle. Gnabry was a muscle injury and I'm unsure with Michael towards the end as well," he said.

"The physio staff will play a really important role now in helping all the players recover.”

Joshua Kimmich expressed concern about the injuries as well, telling Sky:

“When you look at our injuries today, you realise that, ultimately, the most important thing is to stay fit.

“It didn’t look like any of them would be back in two days. The images of Ismael didn’t look good. The way his ankle buckled, it didn’t look good.”

Bayern were already without two key attacking players, Jamal Musiala and Luis Diaz, for Saturday's clash.

The Bavarians will have little time to recover, with a Champions League meeting against Atletico Madrid coming up on Tuesday.

#2746. Phillies ace named among finalists for NL Cy Young award

Phillies ace named among finalists for NL Cy Young award originally appeared on The Sporting News. Add The Sporting News as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

While the MLB season is fully in the rearview mirror, and the early days of the offseason are in motion, the award season is in full swing. Whether it's the MVP awards for the respective leagues, Silver Sluggers, or Golden Gloves, they're all up for grabs.

But there's one award that keeps finding some ties back to the Philadelphia Phillies: the Cy Young Award. For the second year in a row, one of their starters has been nominated for the prestigious award.

The nomination comes off the heels of what was quite an impressive season for Sanchez, despite the Phillies' struggles over the course of the campaign. Across his 33 starts last season, the Phillies ace recorded an 18-7 record, with his 18 wins being an MLB-leading metric, posting an ERA of 2.91 and fanning 231 batters in the process.

To put it simply, when he was on the mound, Sanchez was on top of things, and this impact was felt on the team as he posted yet another Major League-leading metric, a WAR of 8.1, the same as he did in the year prior.

However, even with these impressive feats, Sanchez has some stiff competition in the category, including World Series winner Jacob Misiorowski, and divisional rival, Chris Sale from the Atlanta Braves.

The winner of the award will be named on November 11. If he is to win the award, Sanchez would be the first Phillie to do so since Roy Halladay in 2010.

#2745. Bucks exercise rookie-scale options on Kel’el Ware, Kasparas Jakučionis

The Milwaukee Bucks will exercise their rookie-scale options on offseason acquisitions Kel'el Ware and Kasparas Jakučionis, according to HoopsHype's Michael Scotto.

Ware, a 22-year-old seven-footer acquired in the trade that sent Giannis Antetokounmpo to the Miami Heat, has played solely in a reserve role for the Bucks to begin the season, averaging a career-high 13.8 PPG to go along with 8.7 RPG and 1.5 stocks (steals plus blocks). But for all his talent and flashes of brilliance, Ware has continued to show the motor and decision-making issues that marred his first two years in the league, leading to career lows in shooting (46.8% overall, 26.1% from three) and minutes (19.7 MPG), despite Myles Turner's struggles and the Bucks' lack of size in the frontcourt.

Jakučionis, also acquired in the Giannis trade—and by all accounts a must-have component of it—has arguably been one of Milwaukee's brightest spots to start the season. Quickly emerging as a Taylor Jenkins favourite, the 6'5" Lithuanian point guard has become the team's sixth man and earned enough trust to feature in many of the team's closing lineups too. So far, the 20-year-old is averaging career highs across the board, including points (8.3 PPG), rebounds (2.8 RPG), assists (4.0 APG), and minutes (22.5 MPG). However, like many of his teammates, he's struggled shooting from distance, hitting just 23.5% of his three-point attempts, down from 42.3% last season.

On-court production aside, Ware and Jakučionis figure to be key parts of the Bucks' present and future, so seeing their rookie-scale options picked up is a positive sign. As per Scotto's reporting, Ware's option for the 2027-28 season comes in at $7.14m while Jakučionis' is for $4.02m.

Here's hoping the pair continue to grow as the season progresses.

#2744. Fantasy Football 2026: Waiver Wire, Week 9 edition

Welcome to the Fantasy Football Waiver Wire for Week 9.

Week 8 was a strange one, through and through. Can we chalk that up to Halloween? Maybe.

Perhaps the biggest headline is that Lamar Jackson came back after missing three games with an ankle injury and his return lasted all of one play, and now he's on IR and might miss the rest of the season with a thumb injury. That's going to negatively impact all of the Ravens. Throw in other injuries and the steady drumbeat of bye weeks, and the waiver wire is once again going to be busy this week.

Unfortunately, it's one of the weaker free agent crops of the season – so far.

The Process: Each week, I try to identify the top Waiver Wire targets and evaluate the short- and long-term prospects of those players. I also do my best to reveal which players who are coming off a big week are fool's gold. Finally, I look at streamers who might help you in a pinch (this is usually more important during Bye weeks).

Some players discussed won't be available in your league. I try to list players who are available in more than 50% of leagues. But what does that even mean? A 10-team league with six bench spots will have a much larger Free Agent pool than a 14-team league with eight. Most leagues fall somewhere in between. This isn't "one size fits all." By way of example, I won't list Kirk Cousins, Jonah Coleman, Rachaad White, Makai Lemon, Jordyn Tyson, DeVaughn Vele, T.J. Hockenson, or Brenton Strange, among others who probably aren't available in any of your leagues.

Note that I'm writing this column BEFORE the Week 9 Monday night game (CHI @ SEA).

Geno Smith makes my list again, and the veteran QB is delivering in his return to the team that drafted him. His last two outings: 24.5 and 19.0 fantasy points. Kansas City in Week 9 isn't a good matchup, but the schedule is favorable for the following three games (Bills, Chargers, and Dolphins).

Let's stay in New York (OK fine, New Jersey). J.J. McCarthy has looked outstanding through five quarters as a Giant. As in, he's completely unrecognizable – so far – from the quarterback who was so atrocious on Minnesota last season. That excellence didn't translate to big fantasy points in their last game (an OT upset win in Houston), but those will come if he keeps hitting his weapons all over the field, and using his legs as needed. If you need a second QB in a Superflex he's a good target, as the first-place Giants have been moving the ball and scoring. And if he turns back into a pumpkin, it was worth the risk (same as for Big Blue).

Jacoby Brissett and Daniel Jones got benched in Week 8. I don't know what that means long-term, but Gardner Minshew and Anthony Richardson could be in line for some starts for at least a little while. Minshew has already been named the starter for Week 9. The Colts are in free-fall, and maybe the whole team should just be avoided, minus Jonathan Taylor if he's still there. Richardson — if he starts some games —can run and that always has value. The Cardinals throw the ball a ton and Minshew has produced at times in the past. Arizona is at Seattle this week, so you won't want to start Minshew in Week 9. But the Colts host the Cowboys and their terrible defense, so if you really want to gamble…Anyway, of the two, I prefer Minshew.

Need a Week 9 streamer? Malik Willis gets the Lions and their generous defense.

There are some very good options here, but availability will vary a lot. Some of the publicly available roster percentages on the guys I'm about to list feel lower than they actually are, in practice.

Mike Washington, Jr. got the first six touches in the Raiders' win over the Jets on Sunday and ended up with 25 carries to just two for Ashton Jeanty. He didn't have any catches, though, and Jeanty (who may have been in the doghouse) is still dominating the third down and passing game work. This is a situation to watch. Washington is a high priority add if he's somehow available in your league.

Tyler Allgeier had a monster game on Sunday (RB1 for the week!). It was against Dallas (the league's worst run defense), and Jeremiyah Love is still not at 100%. It was Allgeier's first game with more than more than 6.5 fantasy points in a month. So yeah, it's an outlier performance. Still, he's a very capable back, and Arizona can score. He's a good player to have on you roster for spot starts and maybe more.

Seth McGowan should be rostered everywhere, while we all wait and see whether Jonathan Taylor gets moved. It might just get so ugly in Indianapolis that it's an offense to completely avoid, but "first string" running backs should always be rostered. In the same vein, both Tyjae Spears and Nick Singleton are speculative adds, as Tony Pollard could possibly get traded as well.

Kenny Gainwell erupted for a monster game Sunday, but the Bucs only attempted three passes in the rain and ran both of their backs down Atlanta's throat. I don't think this performance is all that repeatable, and Gainwell has mostly disappointed this season after his mini-breakout last season. Still, he should be rostered.

Devin Singletary is worth a roster spot. It's possible that New York could be adding a back via trade, but with Tyrone Tracy, Jr. on IR and Cam Skattebo straining his hamstring at the end of the Week 7 game, Singletary should be in for more work on a productive rushing offense.

Emmett Johnson and Kaelon Black are exciting rookie backups who've shown plenty of burst when given the chance and are in great handcuff situations. Did you see Johnson's long TD catch-and-run at Denver? Wow. Both can be flexed in an emergency and are lottery tickets that should be rostered.

Jonathon Brooks (IR stash) is another to consider if you want to speculate.

The cupboard is pretty bare at receiver this week.

Here's something I thought I'd never write: The Jets have a lot of weapons. A week after everyone ran out to get Omar Cooper, Jr., Cooper goose-egged and Adonai Mitchell, who put up zero points the week before in his return from injury, exploded for 7-142-1. Welcome to fantasy football! It's going to be frustrating at times, but both players will have good games, and bad ones, on an offense that's constantly chasing points. Cooper probably isn't available in your league, but Mitchell might be.

De'Zhaun Stribling is an intriguing IR stash, given how banged-up the 49er receivers are. DeMarcus Robinson is too, for that matter.

Need depth? I'll repeat a bunch of names I've listed in recent weeks, together with some new ones: Antonio Williams, DeMario Douglas, Keenan Allen, Malik Washington, Jahan Dotson, Caleb Douglas, Travis Hunter, Jakobi Meyers, Savion Williams, and Tank Dell.

Speculative stashes: I'm slightly intrigued by Treylon Burks, who is getting deep shots and has the frame and high catch point to be useful in the red zone, and Kyle Williams, given all the injuries to New England's receivers.

I don't think I'd chase Chimere Dike's big Week 8, unless you get points for return yardage. He's likely to go back to a very part-time role in the offense once Carnell Tate is back, and the Titans are on a bye this week which gives Tate another week.

Mike Gesiscki has a TD in five straight games, and six on the season. We can't continue to ignore this. A bonus: This week he even caught a few other passes. Joe Burrow is playing out of his mind. The volume is low, and maybe the TD production isn't sustainable, but Gesicki is scoring more fantasy points than a lot of guys who are in fantasy starting lineups each week.

Eli Raridon gets a nice bump if Hunter Henry misses time, and especially with so many New England receivers banged up.

Chig Okonkwo is now a mainstay in this column. He's making plays, even if the volume isn't great. Terry McLaurin's injury has opened the door for multiple players to see more targets.

Terrance Ferguson is still an IR stash, although good luck guessing which Rams' tight end to play every week.

It's a low-scoring offense, but if you need depth, Greg Dulcich is getting decent volume (at least four catches and five targets in three straight games).

That's all I've got. Good luck with your waiver claims!

#2743. Texas A&M''s Kaia Castle named SEC Defensive Player of the Week

Kaia Castle dominated at the net for Texas A&M volleyball last week. On Monday, the conference recognized her performance.

Castle was named SEC Defensive Player of the Week after recording 17 blocks across two matches against No. 6 Tennessee and No. 14 Texas. The junior middle blocker averaged 2.43 blocks per set and accounted for 19.5 points. Castle delivered her best performance Friday against Tennessee, finishing with a season-high 12 blocks in the Aggies' four-set victory. Her total ranked fourth in program history for a four-set match and helped Texas A&M record a season-high 16 team blocks.

She followed that performance with five blocks Sunday against Texas, marking her seventh match this season with at least five blocks. Although the Aggies fell in three sets, Castle remained a consistent presence defensively.

Below is the excerpt from the release from conference officials:

Texas A&M's Kaia Castle, a junior middle blocker from Buffalo, Minn., averaged 2.43 blocks per set over two matches this past weekend. Castle recorded a season-high 12 blocks in Friday's win over No. 6 Tennessee, the most by any SEC player in a four-set match this season and the second-best four-set block total in program history. She followed with five blocks against No. 14 Texas, her seventh match this season with five or more blocks and her sixth in her last seven matches.

Castle and the Aggies (14-9, 7-4 SEC) return to action Friday, Nov. 6, when they travel to Nashville to face No. 23 Vanderbilt. First serve is scheduled for 7 p.m. CT.

#2742. Game Preview: Suns hit the road for rematch against Warriors

Who: Phoenix Suns (1-3) @ Golden State Warriors (4-3)

Watch: Arizona's Family 3TV, Arizona's Family Sports

The Phoenix Suns have the worst defense in the NBA, and tonight they get Steph Curry and Draymond Green on the road.

Nine days ago this matchup felt like relief. Booker dropped 43 on a Warriors team missing its stars, the bench flipped the game, and we all exhaled after Portland. Since then? Phoenix lost in Oklahoma City, lost to Denver on Booker's 30th birthday, and sat on a 1-3 record all weekend. Golden State went the other way and hasn't lost since leaving Phoenix.

So here's where the fanbase is at: nobody's panicking about the offense anymore. We're panicking about the other end. Every opponent has hung 120-plus on this team, and the Warriors just made 22 threes without Steph on the floor.

You do the math on what happens if Phoenix shows up with the same effort on the perimeter.

Golden State rested Curry and Draymond on the front end of the back-to-back against the Clippers, saving both for Phoenix.

Phoenix won the home opener 131-122… still, their only its first win of the season. Devin Booker went for 43 points, 7 rebounds, and 7 assists with only two made threes, didn't miss a shot in the third quarter, and broke the franchise home-opener scoring record held by Eddie Johnson and Charles Barkley.

The real story was the bench. Ryan Dunn, Jordan Goodwin, Collin Gillespie and Luke Kennard were a combined +56 in a 9-point win. Gillespie went scoreless in the first half, then rattled off 9 points in 3 minutes to swing the momentum. Kennard hit big shots in his Suns debut.

Now, the asterisk, and we have to be honest about it. That was Golden State's C team on the second night of a back-to-back: no Curry, no Draymond, no Podziemski, no Horford. It was not an impressive win. And the Warriors will likely want to prove that tonight in front of their home crowd.

Even then, a depleted Warriors group put up 122. The first unit looked discombobulated with sloppy turnovers in the first half, and Booker himself said the second unit had better chemistry than the starters. Rebounding was the other red flag he flagged postgame. These are the two worst defensive rebounding teams in the NBA, with Phoenix at 30 and Golden State at 29 overall.

So now the question is: how does Phoenix stop Stephen Curry? A heavy dosage of Jordan Goodwin could be in order. Even then, good luck.

Stop the ball before it reaches the paint

Phoenix entered last week with a 124.5 defensive rating, dead last in the league, and opponents are now shooting nearly 44% from three against them. The sequence repeats every night: a guard gets beat off the dribble, nobody stops the ball, the forward helps late, and it's a layup or a wide-open corner three. Against Denver, Jamal Murray and Christian Braun went 14-of-18 from deep, and Phoenix allowed 54% from three overall.

Braun was a 23% shooter coming in. Golden State's role guys will get the same invitation if the help keeps collapsing.

Denver shot 30 free throws to Phoenix's 11, and Murray alone took more than the entire Suns roster. Some calls were questionable. Plenty were dumb fouls, technicals, a delay of game, and the Brooks ejection. You can't give Steph free points on top of open threes.

Collin Gillespie had 16 points, 10 assists, and 4 steals against Denver and was +8 in a 7-point loss. Jalen Green had 2 points on 1-of-6 in 25 minutes. The Suns look their best with Gillespie on the floor, period. Against a small, switch-heavy Warriors team, this is also the perfect night to promote Oso. Gillespie said it himself: Oso plays like a point forward and makes the flow easy. Watch how early Jordan Ott staggers and who closes, because that tells you where his head is at.

Somebody has to sacrifice. Devin Booker scored 43 last time against a skeleton crew. He won't get that runway with Draymond, Melton, Yaxel, and others loading up on him.

In that same vein, who steps up? Does Green bounce back off the bench or as a starter? Can Miles Bridges keep attacking downhill as he did in his 24-point, 10-rebound game against Denver instead of settling for threes? It takes a team to win. Hero ball needs to fade, and the hunt every possession should be to get the best possible shot. This is not on one guy. It's on the entire team to buy in.

While the Suns showed some progress in their loss to Denver, their defense is still a major concern. I do not see them winning this one, but I hope to be proven wrong.

#2741. Dodgers Have One Month to Keep Tarik Skubal Before MLB Lockout

As free agency begins, ESPN insiders revealed the timeline for Los Angeles Dodgers pitcher Tarik Skubal to sign.

Skubal is not only the Dodgers' biggest free agent but also the biggest domino in the market, and as in the past, his landmark deal will likely unlock the floodgates for MLB transactions.

As the offseason really kicks into gear, ESPN's MLB reporters all chimed in for an intel report on the biggest storylines of the next month before the lockout.

Aug 28, 2026; Detroit, Michigan, USA; Los Angeles Dodgers starting pitcher Tarik Skubal (29) watches from the dugout against the Detroit Tigers in the fifth inning at Comerica Park. Mandatory Credit: Lon Horwedel-Imagn Images

Top MLB insider Jeff Passan reports that Skubal is expected to sign before the December 1st lockout, giving the Dodgers and the rest of the league a month to make their best offers.

"Skubal is far and away the best player in this class, and the expectation is he will sign before the Dec. 1 lockout. The two most obvious suitors are the two teams with the highest payrolls in baseball: The Los Angeles Dodgers and New York Mets," Passan wrote.

"There are bound to be others. The Chicago Cubs badly want Skubal. They also are skeptical that they will be willing to compete at the sort of dollars Skubal is expected to get — well, in excess of $300 million, potentially in the $400 million-plus range."

On paper, the Dodgers have the money to match any offer for Skubal, given their revenue and their tendency to splash cash when they feel it is appropriate.

Skubal, being a high-caliber difference-maker, has the markings of a player that the Dodgers would do a lot to keep.

Oct 29, 2025; Los Angeles, California, USA; Los Angeles Dodgers co-owner Mark Walter looks on in the eighth inning between the Toronto Blue Jays and the Los Angeles Dodgers during game five of the 2025 MLB World Series at Dodger Stadium. Mandatory Credit: Kirby Lee-Imagn Images

However, as team owner Mark Walter continues to sell off pieces of his sports portfolio, and news broke today that he is looking to sell Cadillac F1, questions about how his monetary needs will affect the Dodgers continue to grow.

If Walter needs money urgently, spending big on Skubal may be the last thing he wants to do.

Walter has publicly maintained that the team will not slow down spending-wise, and if the negative speculation is just that, speculation, then Skubal's timeline is moot, and the team will be in the race until the bitter end.

Rams edge rusher Myles Garrett, who has been on injured reserve since undergoing knee surgery after the season opener, will practice this week, coach Sean McVay said Monday.

The Rams will open the 21-day window for Garrett to return to the roster, but McVay said the Rams would proceed cautiously before determining when Garrett will play in a game.

The Rams (4-4) play the Washington Commanders (2-4-1) on Sunday in Landover, Md.

"I want to be careful to even set any sort of expectations," McVay said during a videoconference with reporters. "I think just taking it a day at a time is going to be the best way that we can approach it.

"Think that's the right thing for him and really just to see how he responds."

Read more: Sean McVay's unusual kicker experiment leaves Rams with little margin for error

McVay said there was "a good chance" tight end Terrance Ferguson will return to practice this week but a decision has not been made. Ferguson has been on injured reserve since Oct. 2 because of an ankle injury.

The Rams acquired Garrett in June by sending edge rusher Jared Verse, a first-round draft pick and future picks to the Cleveland Browns for the two-time NFL defensive player of the year, who amassed an NFL season-record 23 sacks last season.

Garrett, 30, was sidelined for most of training camp because of a left knee problem. The five-time All-Pro practiced the week before the Sept. 11 opener against the San Francisco 49ers in Australia, but he did not make a tackle in the 27-7 defeat. Garrett said afterward that it was "the worst version of myself," and that there were times he was thinking about his health rather than what the 49ers were doing on offense.

"Getting him back on the field I think is going to be really good overall for the team and as a defense," cornerback Trent McDuffie said Monday, adding that Garrett's energy will bolster a unit that is beginning to ascend.

McVay said both would be evaluated throughout the week before making a determination about their availability for Sunday.

With the NFL trade deadline on Nov. 10, McVay reiterated that the Rams are counting on the return of Garrett, Ferguson and other key players such as receiver Davante Adams and defensive lineman Kobie Turner as opposed to making deals for new players.
